Using this REGROW rather than GROW approach reflects a particular interpretation of what it means for a parameter γ \gamma to be nuisance: we have no idea of what the true γ \gamma might be and are therefore prepared to incur the same expected loss of growth for not knowing γ \gamma , irrespective of what γ \gamma is. Solving this problem for all δ + ≥ δ − \delta^{+}\geq\delta^{-} gives us a collection of e -variables ℰ ≥ δ − := { E δ ∗ : δ ≥ δ − } \mathcal{E}_{\geq\delta^{-}}:=\{E^{*}_{\delta}:\delta\geq\delta^{-}\} . Now suppose there exists another e -variable E ∗ E^{*} such that That is, we pick the worst-case optimal e -variable among ℰ ≥ δ − \mathcal{E}_{\geq\delta^{-}} , thereby applying GROW on a meta-level as it were, after restricting ourselves to e -variables that are themselves REGROW for fixed δ \delta and unknown γ \gamma . This E ∗ E^{*} is then our choice for solving the original problem ( 23 ).

<sub>§4.2 Composite ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} , nuisance parameters present</sub>

### 5. Theorem 1 in Full: Application to Bayesian and Sequential t t -test

If we try to apply Theorem 1 as above to the t t -test, a prototypical nuisance setting, we run into the issue that the minimum KL is not achieved. This problem can be solved by extending the theorem further, allowing for densities on a coarsening of 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} . This is any random variable 𝐕 \mathbf{V} that can be written as a function of 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} , i.e. 𝐕 = g ⁡ ( 𝐘 ) \mathbf{V}=g(\mathbf{Y}) for some function g g ; we retrieve the previous version of Theorem 1 if we take g g the identity and 𝐕 = 𝐘 \mathbf{V}=\mathbf{Y} . We now present Theorem 1 in full generality, allowing for such coarsening and additionally for considering the best e -variable on a modified ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} , consisting of any convex set of Bayes marginal distributions with priors on Θ 1 \Theta_{1} . This is needed for accommodating the t t -test. It also allows us to incorporate robust Bayesian settings ( Berger, 1985 , Grünwald and Dawid, 2004 ) , but we will not further pursue those here. In the theorem we use the following notation: for (sub-) distribution P P for 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} , P [ 𝐕 ] P^{[\mathbf{V}]} denotes the marginal (sub-) distribution of P P for 𝐕 \mathbf{V} , and p ′ p^{\prime} denotes its density. Theorem 1 , Full Generality Let f ⁡ ( θ ) f(\theta) be a function that is bounded on Θ 1 \Theta_{1} . Suppose all P θ , P θ ′ P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}} with θ , θ ′ ∈ Θ 1 \theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{1} satisfy D ( P θ ∥ P θ ′ ) < ∞ D(P_{\theta}\|P_{\theta^{\prime}})<\infty , and have full support. Let 𝒲 1 ⊆ 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 1 ) \mathcal{W}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{1}) be convex. If for some coarsening 𝐕 \mathbf{V} of 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} we have: p ∗ ′ 0 p^{*^{\prime}}_{0} being the density of P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0} , a (potentially sub-) distribution for 𝐕 \mathbf{V} that satisfies inf W 0 ∈ 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 0 ) D ( P W 1 ∗ ∥ P W 0 ) = D ( P W 1 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ∥ P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ) \inf_{W_{0}\in\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{0})}D(P_{W^{*}_{1}}\|P_{W_{0}})=D(P^{[\mathbf{V}]}_{W_{1}^{*}}\|P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0}) . E f E^{f} satisfies, essentially uniquely: If further D ( P W 1 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ∥ P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ) = D ( P W 1 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ∥ P W 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] ) D(P_{W_{1}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]}\|P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0})=D(P_{W_{1}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]}\|P_{W_{0}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]}) for some W 0 ∗ ∈ 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 0 ) W_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{0}) , then P 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] = P W 0 ∗ [ 𝐕 ] P^{*[\mathbf{V}]}_{0}=P_{W_{0}^{*}}^{[\mathbf{V}]} . The previous version of Theorem 1 is the special case obtained by setting 𝒲 1 = 𝒲 ⁡ ( Θ 1 ) \mathcal{W}_{1}=\mathcal{W}(\Theta_{1}) , 𝐘 = 𝐕 \mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{V} and using linearity of expectation. We call E f E^{f} as in ( 27 ) the REGROW e -variable relative to offset f f and set of priors 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} . If f f is constant (no offset), we call it 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} -GROW , noting that it gives worst-case optimal growth rate under all priors in 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} .

<sub>§4.3 Theorem 1 in Full: Application to Bayesian and Sequential t t -test</sub>

### 6. The t t -test Setting

We return to the setting with a nuisance parameter with notation as in Section 4.2 . Jeffreys (1961) proposed a Bayesian version of the t t -test; see also ( Rouder et al., 2009 ) . We start with the models ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} and ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} for data 𝐘 = ( Y 1 , … , Y n ) \mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}) given as ℋ 0 = { P 0 , σ ​ ( 𝐘 ) ∣ σ ∈ Γ } \mathcal{H}_{0}=\{P_{0,\sigma}(\mathbf{Y})\mid\sigma\in\Gamma\} ; ℋ 1 = { P δ , σ ​ ( 𝐘 ) ∣ ( δ , σ ) ∈ Θ 1 } \mathcal{H}_{1}=\{P_{\delta,\sigma}(\mathbf{Y})\mid(\delta,\sigma)\in\Theta_{1}\} , where Δ = ℝ , Γ = ℝ + \Delta=\mathbb{R},\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{+} , Θ 1 ≔ Δ × Γ \Theta_{1}\coloneqq\Delta\times\Gamma and Θ 0 = { ( 0 , σ ) : σ ∈ Γ } \Theta_{0}=\{(0,\sigma):\sigma\in\Gamma\} , and P δ , σ P_{\delta,\sigma} has density (with y ¯ = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n y i \overline{y}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i} ) Jeffreys proposed to equip ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} with a Cauchy prior W c ​ [ δ ] W^{\text{\sc c}}[\delta] on the effect size δ \delta , and both ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} and ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} with the scale-invariant prior measure with density w H ​ ( σ ) ∝ 1 / σ w^{H}(\sigma)\propto 1/\sigma on the variance. The same formula with the same prior on σ \sigma but other priors on δ \delta was suggested by Lai (1976) with a non-Bayesian, martingale interpretation. Below we will see that, even though w H ​ ( σ ) w^{H}(\sigma) is improper (whereas the priors appearing in Theorem 1 are invariably proper), the resulting Bayes factor E ∗ E^{*} is an e -variable. We then present Theorem 2 which shows that, for priors W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] with more than 2 moments, E ∗ E^{*} in fact even is 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} -GROW with 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} the set of all product priors on δ × σ \delta\times\sigma with marginal W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] on δ \delta , i.e. it has a worst-case optimal growth rate property relative to all distributions in ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} compatible with W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] . Thus, a form of GROW-optimality holds for most priors W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] one might want to use, including standard choices (such as a standard normal) or the point prior we will suggest further below — but we do not know if it holds for the moment-less Cauchy proposed by Jeffreys.

<sub>§The t t -test Setting</sub>

### 7. Almost Bayesian Case: prior on δ \delta available

For any proper prior distribution W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] on δ \delta and any proper prior distribution W ⁡ [ σ ] W[\sigma] on σ \sigma , we define p W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] ​ ( y ) = ∫ δ ∈ Δ ∫ σ ∈ Γ p δ , σ ​ ( y ) ​ d ​ W ​ [ δ ] ​ d ​ W ​ [ σ ] , p_{W[\delta],W[\sigma]}(y)=\int_{\delta\in\Delta}\int_{\sigma\in\Gamma}p_{\delta,\sigma}(y)\,\textnormal{d}W{[\delta]}\,\textnormal{d}W{[\sigma]}, as the Bayes marginal density under the product prior W ⁡ [ δ ] × W ⁡ [ σ ] W[\delta]\times W[\sigma] . For convenience later on we set the sample space to be 𝒴 n = ( ℝ ∖ { 0 } ) × ℝ n − 1 \mathcal{Y}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1} , assuming beforehand that the first outcome will not be 0. Now we define 𝐕 ≔ ( V 1 , … , V n ) \mathbf{V}\coloneqq(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}) with V i = Y i / | Y 1 | V_{i}=Y_{i}/|Y_{1}| . We have that 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} determines 𝐕 \mathbf{V} , and ( 𝐕 , Y 1 ) (\mathbf{V},Y_{1}) determines 𝐘 = ( Y 1 , Y 2 , … , Y n ) \mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n}) . The distributions in ℋ 0 ∪ ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{0}\cup\mathcal{H}_{1} can thus alternatively be thought of as distributions on the pair ( 𝐕 , Y 1 ) (\mathbf{V},Y_{1}) . 𝐕 \mathbf{V} is “ 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} with the scale divided out”: as is well-known ( Lai, 1976 , Berger et al., 1998 ) and easily shown (Appendix B.3 ), under all P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathcal{H}_{0} , i.e. all P 0 , σ P_{0,\sigma} with σ > 0 \sigma>0 , 𝐕 \mathbf{V} has the same distribution P 0 ​ [ 𝐕 ] P_{0}[\mathbf{V}] with density p 0 ′ p^{\prime}_{0} . In the same way, one shows that under all P W ⁡ [ δ ] , σ {P}_{W[\delta],\sigma} with σ > 0 \sigma>0 , 𝐕 \mathbf{V} has the same pdf p W ⁡ [ δ ] ′ p^{\prime}_{W[\delta]} (which therefore does not depend on the prior on σ \sigma ). We now get that, with we must have 𝐄 𝐕 ∼ P ​ [ E ∗ ] = 1 \mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{V}\sim P}[E^{*}]=1 for all P ∈ ℋ 0 P\in\mathcal{H}_{0} , hence it is an e variable. Remarkably, this ‘scale-free’ e -variable coincides with the Bayes factor one gets if one uses, for σ \sigma , the prior w H ​ ( σ ) = 1 / σ w^{H}(\sigma)=1/\sigma suggested by Jeffreys, and treats σ \sigma and δ \delta as independent. That is ( Lai, 1976 , page 273) (a full derivation is in Appendix B.3 ), we have Despite its improperness, w H w^{H} induces a valid e -variable when used in the Bayes factor. The equivalence of this Bayes factor to E ∗ E^{*} simply means that it manages to ignore the ‘nuisance’ part of the model and models the likelihood of the scale-free 𝐕 \mathbf{V} instead. The reason this is possible is that w H w^{H} coincides with the right-Haar prior for this problem ( Eaton, 1989 , Berger et al., 1998 ) , about which we will say more below. Amazingly, it turns out that the e -variable ( 29 ) has a GROW property (among all e -variables for data 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} , not just the coarsened 𝐕 \mathbf{V} ) under the weak condition that the prior W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] has a ( 2 + ϵ ) (2+\epsilon) th moment. This follows from a special case of Theorem 4.2. of ( Perez et al., 2022 ) (for the case that W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] puts all its mass on a single δ \delta ) and Corollary 8.3. (for general W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] . For convenience we re-state this special case here. Let, for priors W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] W[\delta],W[\sigma] , P W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] [ 𝐕 ] P_{W[\delta],W[\sigma]}^{[\mathbf{V}]} be the marginal distribution with density p W ⁡ [ δ ] , W ⁡ [ σ ] ′ p^{\prime}_{W[\delta],W[\sigma]} . We have: The theorem allows us to use Theorem 1 as above with constant f ⁡ ( δ , σ ) = 0 f(\delta,\sigma)=0 (note that 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} is convex) to conclude that E ∗ E^{*} as in ( 28 ) is equal to E f E^{f} as in ( 27 ): the Bayes factor based on the right Haar prior, is not just an e -variable, but is even GROW relative to the set of all priors on δ × σ \delta\times\sigma that are compatible with W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] .

<sub>§Almost Bayesian Case: prior on δ \delta available</sub>

### 8. REGROW-GROW safe t t -test with minimum effect sizes

Suppose we want to test Θ 1 \Theta_{1} vs. Θ 0 \Theta_{0} as in ( 23 ) with fixed effect sizes δ + \delta^{+} and δ − \delta^{-} and with σ 2 \sigma^{2} in the role of γ \gamma . We proceed exactly as we did underneath ( 23 ): we first consider the test { ( δ + , σ 2 ) : σ 2 > 0 } \{(\delta^{+},\sigma^{2}):\sigma^{2}>0\} vs. { ( δ − , σ 2 ) : σ 2 > 0 } \{(\delta^{-},\sigma^{2}):\sigma^{2}>0\} for the fixed given δ + \delta^{+} using the REGROW criterion with f ⁡ ( ( δ , σ ) ) = GRO ​ ( δ , σ ) f((\delta,\sigma))=\text{GRO}(\delta,\sigma) . We have ( Koolen and Grünwald, 2021 , Section 4.3) that f ⁡ ( δ + , σ ) = ( n / 2 ) ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + δ + 2 ) f(\delta^{+},\sigma)=(n/2)\log(1+\delta^{+2}) is constant on σ \sigma . Therefore we can use Theorem 1 in its most general form above in combination with Theorem 2 (applied with point prior W ⁡ [ δ ] W[\delta] on δ + \delta^{+} ) to conclude that (both the GROW and) the REGROW e -variable are given by E δ + ∗ := p δ + ′ ​ ( 𝐕 ) / p δ − ′ ​ ( 𝐕 ) E^{*}_{\delta^{+}}:=p^{\prime}_{\delta^{+}}(\mathbf{V})/p^{\prime}_{\delta^{-}}(\mathbf{V}) . Since Proposition 3 is applicable to sets of distributions defined on 𝐕 \mathbf{V} rather than 𝐘 \mathbf{Y} (details in Appendix B.3 ), we find that, with ℰ ≥ δ + := { E δ ∗ : δ ≥ δ − } \mathcal{E}_{\geq\delta^{+}}:=\{E^{*}_{\delta}:\delta\geq\delta^{-}\} that sup E ∈ ℰ ≥ δ + inf σ > 0 , δ ≥ δ + 𝐄 𝐘 ∼ P δ , σ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] = inf σ > 0 , δ ≥ δ + 𝐄 𝐘 ∼ P δ , σ ​ [ log ⁡ E δ + ∗ ] \sup_{E\in\mathcal{E}_{\geq\delta^{+}}}\inf_{\sigma>0,\delta\geq\delta^{+}}\mathbf{E}_{{\bf Y}\sim P_{\delta,\sigma}}[\log E]=\inf_{\sigma>0,\delta\geq\delta^{+}}\mathbf{E}_{{\bf Y}\sim P_{\delta,\sigma}}[\log E^{*}_{\delta^{+}}] so E δ + ∗ E^{*}_{\delta^{+}} may be thought of as first applying REGROW, to get rid of the nuisance parameter, and then applying GROW — just like in the 2 × 2 2\times 2 Example 7 .

<sub>§REGROW-GROW safe t t -test with minimum effect sizes</sub>

### 9. Extension to General Group Invariant Bayes Factors

In a series of papers ( Berger et al., 1998 , Dass and Berger, 2003 , Bayarri et al., 2012 ) , Berger and collaborators developed a theory of Bayes factors for ℋ 0 = { P 0 , γ : γ ∈ Γ } \mathcal{H}_{0}=\{P_{0,\gamma}:\gamma\in\Gamma\} and ℋ 1 = { P δ , γ : δ ∈ Δ , γ ∈ Γ } \mathcal{H}_{1}=\{P_{\delta,\gamma}:\delta\in\Delta,\gamma\in\Gamma\} with a nuisance parameter (vector) γ \gamma that appears in both models and that satisfies a group invariance; the Bayesian t t -test is the special case with γ = σ , Γ = ℝ + \gamma=\sigma,\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{+} and with the scalar multiplication group and δ \delta an ‘effect size’. Other examples include regression based on mixtures of g g -priors ( Liang et al., 2008 ) , testing a Weibull vs. the log-normal and many more ( Dass and Berger, 2003 ) . The reasoning of the first part of this section straightforwardly generalises to all such cases: the Bayes factor based on using the right Haar measure on γ \gamma in both models gives rise to an e -variable. Theorem 4.2. of Perez et al. (2022) shows that, if the underlying group satisfies a condition called amenability (which holds, e.g., for scaling as in the t-test, but also for e.g. rotations and affine transformations as in parametric linear regression models), then the resulting Bayes factor is GROW relative to a suitably defined set 𝒲 1 \mathcal{W}_{1} . Theorem 2 above is the very special case of their result when instantiated with γ \gamma instantiated to the variance in the t-test (scaling). Although its proof is quite different, the general result may be viewed as the ‘e-variant’ of the classical Hunt-Stein theorem ( Lehmann et al., 2005 , Section 8.5) , with ‘power’ in that theorem replaced by ‘GROW’. ( Perez et al., 2022 , Proposition 4.4) then implies that in all such cases, this GROW Bayes factor is in fact also REGROW. Remarkably therefore, with parameters representing group transformations, unlike e.g. for the 2 × 2 2\times 2 case, GROW and REGROW e-variables generally coincide.

<sub>§Extension to General Group Invariant Bayes Factors</sub>

### 10. (RE)GRO(W), Optional Continuation and Stopping

We focused on Type-I error safety under optional continuation (OC). Can we also get safety under optional stopping (OS), and what is the difference? The GRO-criteria were chosen to optimise expected capital (logarithmic) growth ‘locally’, within a study. How well do GRO-criteria go together with OC over several studies? To make the questions concrete, we consider a specific set-up with data stream Y j , 1 , Y j , 2 , … Y_{j,1},Y_{j,2},\ldots corresponding to the j j -th study to be performed. In the first study we observe batch of outcomes 𝐘 ( 1 ) = ( Y 1 , 1 , … , Y 1 , N ( 1 ) ) \mathbf{Y}_{(1)}=(Y_{1,1},\ldots,Y_{1,N_{(1)}}) ; in the second study (if it is performed at all) 𝐘 ( 2 ) = ( Y 2 , 1 , … , Y 2 , N ( 2 ) ) \mathbf{Y}_{(2)}=(Y_{2,1},\ldots,Y_{2,N_{(2)}}) ; and so on. For further simplicity we will assume that all Y j , i Y_{j,i} are i.i.d. The set-up slightly differs from Example 3 in which the second study’s data was part of the same stream as the first; at the expense of additional notation, everything that follows can be formalized in that setting as well. The conditional e -variables determining our test martingale are now determined by a sequence of stopping times N ( 1 ) , N ( 2 ) , … N_{(1)},N_{(2)},\ldots . The first stopping time N ( 1 ) N_{(1)} is defined as a stopping time on the first sequence Y 1 , 1 , Y 1 , 2 , … Y_{1,1},Y_{1,2},\ldots relative to filtration ( σ ⁡ ( Y 1 n ) ) n (\sigma(Y_{1}^{n}))_{n} and defines a stopped σ \sigma -algebra ℱ ( 1 ) := σ ⁡ ( Y 1 N ( 1 ) ) = σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) ) \mathcal{F}_{(1)}:=\sigma(Y_{1}^{N_{(1)}})=\sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)}) . N ( 2 ) N_{(2)} is defined on the second sequence Y 2 , 1 , Y 2 , 2 , … Y_{2,1},Y_{2,2},\ldots , but it is also allowed to depend on previous data 𝐘 ( 1 ) \mathbf{Y}_{(1)} , i.e it is a stopping time relative to filtration ( σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , Y 2 n ) ) n (\sigma({\bf Y}_{(1)},Y_{2}^{n}))_{n} , and defines a stopped σ \sigma -algebra ℱ ( 2 ) := σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , 𝐘 ( 2 ) ) \mathcal{F}_{(2)}:=\sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\mathbf{Y}_{(2)}) . In general, N ( m ) N_{(m)} is defined relative to filtration σ ​ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , … , 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) , Y m n ) n \sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{(m-1)},Y_{m}^{n})_{n} , and defines a stopped σ \sigma -algebra ℱ ( m ) := σ ⁡ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , … , 𝐘 ( m ) ) \mathcal{F}_{(m)}:=\sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{(m)}) . We let 𝒩 ( m ) \mathcal{N}_{(m)} be the collection of all stopping times for the m m -th study, i.e. relative to σ ​ ( 𝐘 ( 1 ) , … , 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) , Y m , 1 , … , Y m , n ) n \sigma(\mathbf{Y}_{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{(m-1)},Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,n})_{n} . The sequence of stopped σ \sigma -algebras thus defines a new filtration ( ℱ ( m ) ) m (\mathcal{F}_{(m)})_{m} , which we call the filtration at the study level , since ℱ ( m ) \mathcal{F}_{(m)} denotes all information available after m m studies or trials have been completed — it is the filtration referred to in Proposition 2 (for the t-test we need to extend this set-up a little, see Example 8 ).

<sub>§5 (RE)GRO(W), Optional Continuation and Stopping</sub>

### 11. OC vs. OS

In the optional continuation setting, we assume that, after having observed and analyzed m − 1 m-1 studies, we either stop, or continue to the next study. In the latter case we need to specify a stopping time N ( m ) ∈ 𝒩 ( m ) N_{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{(m)} and a conditional e-variable E ( m ) ∈ ℰ ( m ) E_{(m)}\in\mathcal{E}_{(m)} where ℰ ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{(m)} is the set of all ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -conditional e-variables. For example, in the setting of Example 1 , with a simple null ℋ 0 = { P 0 } \mathcal{H}_{0}=\{P_{0}\} and composite alternative ℋ 1 = { P θ : θ ∈ Θ 1 } \mathcal{H}_{1}=\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{1}\} and N ( m ) ∈ 𝒩 ( m ) N_{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{(m)} an arbitrary stopping time for the m m -th study, e N ( m ) , W ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , N ( m ) ) := p W ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , N ( m ) ) / p 0 ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , N ( m ) ) e_{N_{(m)},W}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,N_{(m)}}):=p_{W}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,N_{(m)}})/p_{0}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,N_{(m)}}) is an e -variable for every ‘prior’ distribution W W on Θ 1 \Theta_{1} (here we generalize the notation of Example 1 to allow for data-dependent stopping times). In this setting the analyst can freely choose (or somebody else can impose) any N ( m ) N_{(m)} and any W W and use the corresponding e -variable e N ( m ) , W e_{N_{(m)},W} ; all such e -variables are contained in the set ℰ ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{(m)} . As explained in Example 1 , this includes the choice to set W ( m ) := W ( 1 ) W_{(m)}:=W_{(1)} (re-use the original prior) and the choice to set W ( m ) := W ( 1 ) ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) ) W_{(m)}:=W_{(1)}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)}) , i.e. use the Bayes posterior based on the previous studies. We can now interpret our various GRO criteria as each providing a prescription to choose specific elements of ℰ N ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{N_{(m)}} , for all stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} that are constant given the outcomes of previous studies 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) \mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)} , i.e. that are ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -measurable. To see this, note that each GRO criterion in combination with ℋ 0 \mathcal{H}_{0} and ℋ 1 \mathcal{H}_{1} defines, for each n n , an e -variable S [ n ] S^{[n]} for a single sequence Y 1 , … , Y n Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n} . In all cases, this can be written as S [ n ] = s [ n ] ​ ( Y n ) S^{[n]}=s^{[n]}(Y^{n}) for some function s [ n ] s^{[n]} ; the function s [ n ] s^{[n]} is what is really specified; we call s [ 1 ] , s [ 2 ] , … s^{[1]},s^{[2]},\ldots an e-specification. If N ( m ) := n m N_{(m)}:=n_{m} , we can thus simply set E ( m ) := s [ n m ] ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , n m ) E_{(m)}:=s^{[n_{m}]}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,n_{m}}) . We call this the plug-in method for constructing E ( m ) E_{(m)} from specification s [ 1 ] , s [ 2 ] , … s^{[1]},s^{[2]},\ldots (such specification may correspond to one of our GRO criteria, but in general it could be arrived at in different ways as well) . The running product of E ( m ) E_{(m)} thus constructed provides, via Proposition 2 , a test martingale at the study level. In contrast, optional stopping scenarios usually concern only a single data-level process Y 1 , Y 2 , … Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots , without any super-structure in terms of subsequent studies. The scenario is completely determined by a sequence of conditional e -variables S 1 , S 2 , … S_{1},S_{2},\ldots , i.e., applying Definition 1 at the data level, such that for all n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} , for all P ∈ ℋ 0 : 𝐄 P [ S n ∣ ℱ n − 1 ] ≤ 1 a.s. \text{for all $P\in\mathcal{H}_{0}$:}\ \ {\bf E}_{P}[S_{n}\mid\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]\leq 1\text{\ a.s.} , with ℱ n = σ ⁡ ( Y n ) \mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(Y^{n}) . Their running product S [ 1 ] , S [ 2 ] , … S^{[1]},S^{[2]},\ldots (with S [ n ] = ∏ i = 1 n S i S^{[n]}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i} ) then forms a test martingale. Recall from Corollary 1 in Section 1.3 that for any nonnegative random process E ( 1 ) , E ( 2 ) , … E^{(1)},E^{(2)},\ldots at the study level (adapted to ( ℱ ( m ) ) m (\mathcal{F}_{(m)})_{m} ) we say that the corresponding threshold test is safe under OC (with respect to Type-I error) if the Ville-Robbins inequality ( 7 ) holds. Extending this definition in the natural way, we say, for a data-level process of nonnegative random variables S [ 1 ] , S [ 2 ] , … S^{[1]},S^{[2]},\ldots , i.e. with S [ j ] S^{[j]} adapted to ℱ j \mathcal{F}_{j} , that the corresponding threshold test is safe under OS (with respect to Type-I error) if again the Ville-Robbins inequality holds (with E ( n ) := S [ n ] E^{(n)}:=S^{[n]} ).

<sub>§OC vs. OS</sub>

### 12. Sequentially Decomposable e -Specifications

In many (not all) cases, the GRO-specification s [ 1 ] , s [ 2 ] , … s^{[1]},s^{[2]},\ldots forms itself a test martingale relative to some filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} : there exist a sequence of functions s 1 , s 2 , … s_{1},s_{2},\ldots , with s i s_{i} a function on 𝒴 i \mathcal{Y}^{i} , such that, for all n n , s [ n ] ​ ( Y n ) = ∏ i = 1 n S i s^{[n]}(Y^{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{i} with S i := s i ​ ( Y i ) S_{i}:=s_{i}(Y^{i}) and { S i } i \{S_{i}\}_{i} is a conditional e-variable collection relative to filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} . We will say that such an e -variable specification is sequentially decomposable , or seqdec for short, relative to filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} ; in all our examples except the t-test (Example 8 ) we can take 𝒢 n = σ ⁡ ( Y n ) \mathcal{G}_{n}=\sigma(Y^{n}) . Seqdec specifications have a direct link with the OS setting, resulting in three remarkable properties: first, assuming still that data are i.i.d., any study-level test martingale process we can construct via the plug-in method (see above) based on a seqdec specification also defines a sequence of conditional e -variables and hence a test martingale at the corresponding concatenated data level Y 1 ′ , Y 2 ′ , … Y^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{2},\ldots where Y i ′ Y^{\prime}_{i} is arrived at by relabeling Y 1 , 1 , Y 1 , 2 , … , Y 1 , N ( 1 ) , Y 2 , 1 , Y 2 , 2 , … , Y 2 , N ( 2 ) , Y 3 , 1 , … Y_{1,1},Y_{1,2},\ldots,Y_{1,N_{(1)}},Y_{2,1},Y_{2,2},\ldots,Y_{2,N_{(2)}},Y_{3,1},\ldots in order (so that e.g. Y N ( 1 ) + N ( 2 ) ′ = Y 2 , N ( 2 ) Y^{\prime}_{N_{(1)}+N_{(2)}}=Y_{2,N_{(2)}} ). This defines a concatenated data-level filtration ( 𝒢 t ′ ) t (\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{t})_{t} with 𝒢 t ′ = σ ⁡ ( Y 1 ′ , … , Y t ′ ) \mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{t}=\sigma(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{t}) . The corresponding sequence of conditional e -variables is then given by S 1 ′ , S 2 ′ , … S^{\prime}_{1},S^{\prime}_{2},\ldots where, for T ( 0 ) := 0 T_{(0)}:=0 , m ∈ ℕ m\in\mathbb{N} , 1 ≤ i < N ( m ) 1\leq i<N_{(m)} , T ( m ) := ∑ j = 1 m N ( j ) T_{(m)}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}N_{(j)} , we set S T ( m ) + i ′ := s i ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , i ) S^{\prime}_{T_{(m)}+i}:=s_{i}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,i}) . This means that besides engaging in optional continuation, we can also safely do optional stopping at this concatenated-data level, since the Ville-Robbins inequality holds at this level by Corollary 1 . Second, we can use seqdec specifications to extend the plug-in method (which required constant stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} ) to prescribe conditional E-variables for stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} that are not constant given 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) \mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)} : for arbitrary N ( m ) ∈ 𝒩 ( m ) N_{(m)}\in\mathcal{N}_{(m)} , we set E ( m ) := ∏ i = 1 N ( m ) s i ​ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , i ) E_{(m)}:=\prod_{i=1}^{N_{(m)}}s_{i}(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,i}) . By construction, this reduces to the plug-in method whenever N ( m ) N_{(m)} is constant given 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) \mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)} , so it is a proper extension, and it follows as a direct corollary of the fact that E ( m ) E_{(m)} can be rewritten as ∏ i = 1 N ( m ) S T ( m − 1 ) + i ′ \prod_{i=1}^{N_{(m)}}S^{\prime}_{T_{(m-1)}+i} , i.e. a product of factors in a test martingale, that any E ( m ) E_{(m)} constructed in this manner for any seqdec specification is a ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -conditional e -variable. Third, seqdec specifications allow for alternative ways to create study-level processes from e -specifications beyond the plug-in method used thus far. For example, we can set E ( m ) := ∏ i = 1 N ( m ) s T ( m − 1 ) + i ​ ( 𝐘 ( m − 1 ) , Y m , 1 , … , Y m , i ) E_{(m)}:=\prod_{i=1}^{N_{(m)}}s_{T_{(m-1)}+i}(\mathbf{Y}^{(m-1)},Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,i}) for arbitrary stopping times N ( 1 ) , … , N ( m ) N_{(1)},\ldots,N_{(m)} . Once again, this also defines a martingale at the concatenated data-level — it is simply the martingale that arises if we view the m m studies as one single, long sequence of T ( m ) T_{(m)} data points. We call this the sequential application of the e -variable specification — see Figure 3 . Summarizing, the practical setting we have in mind when we speak about OS and OC respectively is quite different: OC concerns study-level martingales constructed by deciding, on the fly , after the m − 1 m-1 -st study, whether to continue to the m m -th study and if so, what new ℱ ( m − 1 ) \mathcal{F}_{(m-1)} -conditional e -variable to take from the set ℰ ( m ) \mathcal{E}_{(m)} of possible e -variables of use. OS is about data-level martingales with only a stop/continue choice. Nevertheless, the formal definitions of (Type-I error) ‘safety under OS’ and ‘safety under OC’ only differ in that ‘study-level’ is replaced by ‘data-level’. We may say that combining e-variables E ( m ) E_{(m)} by multiplication is always Type-I error safe under OC. If the e-variable prescription used to construct E ( m ) E_{(m)} has the seqdec property, then the stopping times N ( m ) N_{(m)} used in each study do not need to be specified before the study starts and can even be externally imposed, so that we have Type-I error safety not just under OC but also under OS within each individual study. There is one final subtlety to consider: in the OS setting, with a single stream of data Y 1 , Y 2 , … Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots and conditional e-variables S 1 , S 2 , … S_{1},S_{2},\ldots and test martingale S [ 1 ] , S [ 2 ] , … S^{[1]},S^{[2]},\ldots , the Ville-Robbins inequality ( 7 ) implies that our Type-I error bound α \alpha is guaranteed no matter when we stop — in particular, the actual stopping time does not have to be taken relative to the filtration ( 𝒢 n ) n (\mathcal{G}_{n})_{n} — we may even peek into the future to decide whether to stop now. This suggests that our care in specifying the correct filtrations for the t-test was unnecessary — it seems we can use any stopping rule we like! But this becomes incorrect once we move from OS at the data-level to OC at the study-level: if, in the t-test setting with the plug-in construction of the e -variable E ( m ) E_{(m)} for the m m -th study, we were to set the N ( m ) N_{(m)} so that they are not stopping times relative to ( ℱ ( m − 1 ) ∪ σ ⁡ ( V m , 1 , … , V m , n ) ) n (\mathcal{F}_{(m-1)}\cup\sigma(V_{m,1},\ldots,V_{m,n}))_{n} but only relative to the more refined ( ℱ ( m − 1 ) ∪ σ ⁡ ( Y m , 1 , … , Y m , n ) ) n (\mathcal{F}_{(m-1)}\cup\sigma(Y_{m,1},\ldots,Y_{m,n}))_{n} , we could end up creating fake conditional e -variables at the study-level, i.e. so that 𝐄 P 0 ​ [ E ( m ) ∣ ℱ ( m − 1 ) ] > 1 \mathbf{E}_{P_{0}}[E_{(m)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{(m-1)}]>1 for all m m ( ( Perez et al., 2022 , Appendix B) constructs such a random variable for the t-test). And then the Ville-Robbins inequality may not hold any more at the study-level, and we loose the Type-I error guarantee under optional continuation.

<sub>§Sequentially Decomposable e -Specifications</sub>

### 13. Local vs. Global GRO

Now consider two data streams, Y 1 , 1 , … , Y 1 , n 1 Y_{1,1},\ldots,Y_{1,n_{1}} and Y 2 , 1 , … , Y 2 , n 2 Y_{2,1},\ldots,Y_{2,n_{2}} of fixed lengths n 1 n_{1} and n 2 n_{2} . We may alternatively model these two streams as a single stream Y 1 ′ , … , Y n 1 + n 2 ′ Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{n_{1}+n_{2}} of length n 1 + n 2 n_{1}+n_{2} . If we use an e -variable specification with the seqdec property to generate a study-level test martingale in the sequential way (i.e. not the plug-in way) described above, we will get that, E ( 2 ) = E ( 1 ) ⋅ E ( 2 ) E^{(2)}=E_{(1)}\cdot E_{(2)} constructed for the first two data streams (with E ( 1 ) = ∏ i = 1 n 1 s i ​ ( Y i ) E_{(1)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}s_{i}(Y^{i}) and E ( 2 ) = ∏ i = 1 n 2 s n 1 + i ​ ( Y ′ n 1 + i ) E_{(2)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{2}}s_{n_{1}+i}(Y^{\prime n_{1}+i}) ) coincides with E ( 1 ) ′ = ∏ i = 1 n 1 + n 2 s [ i ] ​ ( Y i ′ ) E^{\prime}_{(1)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}s^{[i]}(Y^{\prime}_{i}) constructed for the single alternative stream. We may thus say that the sequential application of seqdec e -specifications is always coherent : applying the specification sequentially-‘locally’ (separately for both studies) or sequentially-globally (for the concatenated data viewed as one study) gives the same result. For example, the Bayesian W W -GRO specification of Section 2.2 , the GROW specification of Proposition 3 and Example 4 and the GROW and REGROW specifications in the t-test example are all seqdec and hence all have this coherence property when applied sequentially. Sometimes the sequential application of an E-prescription is not feasible or desirable; for example, not all details of previous data may be known. We may then prefer the plug-in application of the E-prescription. Unfortunately, the seqdec property is not sufficient to get coherence for the plug-in method: clearly, if we use the same prior W ( 1 ) W_{(1)} as prior in the Bayesian Example 3 for the first and the second study, this leads to different E ( 2 ) E^{(2)} and E ( 1 ) ′ E^{\prime}_{(1)} , the latter being equivalent to using the posterior of the first study as prior in the second. A sufficient condition for a plug-in application to satisfy coherence after all is that it satisfies both the seqdec property, and further that s i s_{i} , with S i = s i ​ ( Y i ) S_{i}=s_{i}(Y^{i}) as in the definition of seqdec, can be rewritten as s i ​ ( Y i ) = s ′ ​ ( Y i ) s_{i}(Y^{i})=s^{\prime}(Y_{i}) for a single function s ′ s^{\prime} , for all i i . Then in fact the plug-in and the sequential application of the e -prescription will coincide, and coherence is guaranteed. This happens in the subset of our examples in which s [ i ] ​ ( Y i ) s^{[i]}(Y^{i}) takes the form q ⁡ ( Y i ) / p ⁡ ( Y i ) q(Y^{i})/p(Y^{i}) for the same p p and q q , for all i i , as happens e.g. in Example 4 .

<sub>§Local vs. Global GRO</sub>

### 14. An Open Question concerning GRO

In practice we may very well be in a situation in which OS at the data-level is desirable (see the next section for why it would be), so we want to use a seqdec specification, yet the GRO criterion we are interested in does not give one — Example 9 illustrates this for the 2 × 2 2\times 2 case. We may then try the following approach, which for simplicity we only describe for the REGROW criterion: let E n f E^{f}_{n} be the REGROW e -variable ( 19 ) achieving ( 20 ) with f ​ ( θ ) = GRO ​ ( θ ) f(\theta)=\text{GRO}(\theta) for samples of size n n . We try to find a sequence of e -variables E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\ldots such that E i E_{i} is a σ ⁡ ( Y i − 1 ) \sigma(Y^{i-1}) -conditional e -variable for Y i Y_{i} and the product e -variable E [ n ] := ∏ i = 1 n E i E^{[n]}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}E_{i} achieves ( 20 ) to within some fixed ϵ \epsilon for all n n larger than some minimal n 0 n_{0} , i.e. By construction, the sequence E [ 1 ] , E [ 2 ] , … E^{[1]},E^{[2]},\ldots is seqdec and allows for optional stopping, and if we can find E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\ldots such that ϵ \epsilon is small for all n n larger than or equal to the n 0 n_{0} corresponding to the smallest sample we’d ever be interested in analyzing, we can say that the full process (and not just an instance at a fixed n n ) is ‘almost’ REGROW in the desired sense. The example raises an important question: under what conditions (on model, minimal batch sizes and the like) can we create a seqdec specification that behaves optimally for our desired GRO criterion (as in Section 2.2 with W W -GRO, and in Example 4 , with the GROW criterion) or almost optimally (as in the 2 × 2 2\times 2 example above with batch size 2, with the REGROW criterion)?

<sub>§An Open Question concerning GRO</sub>

## 总结与展望
Safe testing 主张用「安全检验」（e-value 与测试鞅）取代 p 值，好处是可以一直看数据、随时决定停、还可以把证据累积起来。它对本方向的启示是：证据应该是可累积的量，而不是一次性的判决。

**It connects to**

- `2210.01948` — 同一理论的两个入口，一个走游戏论一个走贝叶斯。
- `2009.03167` — 那篇证明非负性来自一个非负鞅，是同一理论的基石。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** In our examples, we have used (and will keep using in the next section) the REGROW approach to first eliminate nuisance parameters, if they are present, followed by a GROW approach for the parameters of interest. ( Turner et al., 2021 , ter Schure et al., 2021 ) find that this gives intuitive e -variables that perform well in practical applications, not just directly in the GRO sense but also in terms of secondary measures such as a power analysis (Section 6 ) or as the basis of anytime-valid confidence intervals ( Turner et al., 2021 ) . Still, it may not always be the best way to go. For example, a REGROW approach for the parameter of interest when a minimum effect size is given may sometimes be sensible as well. Let us consider this a bit further for (for simplicity) the case with a minimum effect size δ + \delta^{+} but without nuisance parameters, as in Example 4 . REGROW would amount here to using E W 1 ∗ = p W 1 ​ ( 𝐘 ) / p δ − ​ ( 𝐘 ) E^{*}_{W_{1}}=p_{W_{1}}(\mathbf{Y})/p_{\delta^{-}}(\mathbf{Y}) with some prior W 1 W_{1} spread out on the set Θ 1 = { δ : δ ≥ δ + } \Theta_{1}=\{\delta:\delta\geq\delta^{+}\} . Then we would get 𝐄 P δ ​ [ log ⁡ E W 1 ∗ ] < GROW ​ ( Θ 1 ) ​ for δ close to δ + ; 𝐄 P δ ​ [ log ⁡ E W 1 ∗ ] ≫ GROW ​ ( Θ 1 ) ​ for δ ≫ δ + \mathbf{E}_{P_{\delta}}[\log E^{*}_{W_{1}}]<\text{GROW}(\Theta_{1})\text{\ for $\delta$ close to $\delta^{+}$\ };\mathbf{E}_{P_{\delta}}[\log E^{*}_{W_{1}}]\gg\text{GROW}(\Theta_{1})\text{\ for $\delta\gg\delta^{+}$\ } so we would win if we are ‘lucky’ and it turns out that δ ≫ δ + \delta\gg\delta^{+} . However, in practice we often deal with small sample sizes, and δ \delta ’s that may very well be very close to δ + \delta^{+} . Then (as our experiments done for the papers above indicate) the difference in ‘ < < ’ above is non-negligible, and the GROW approach seems safer, since for the GROW e -variable we automatically have 𝐄 P δ ​ [ log ⁡ E W 1 ∗ ] ≥ GROW ​ ( Θ 1 ) \mathbf{E}_{P_{\delta}}[\log E^{*}_{W_{1}}]\geq\text{GROW}(\Theta_{1}) for all δ ∈ Θ 1 \delta\in\Theta_{1} .

- 贝叶斯安全检验依赖先验，我们的 α 与 λ 没有先验依据。

# Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems

arXiv:2110.14168 · 2021 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2110.14168)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
In recent years, large language models have demonstrated impressive skills across many diverse tasks ( Wang et al., 2019 , Brown et al., 2020 ) . Kaplan et al. (2020) describe the consistent benefits of increasing model size, characterizing scaling trends that hold across many orders of magnitude. However, even the largest models falter when required to perform multi-step mathematical reasoning ( Hendrycks et al., 2021 ) . Model samples frequently contain catastrophic mistakes, even after the model has been appropriately finetuned. Mathematical reasoning thus reveals a critical weakness in modern language models.

<sub>§1 Introduction</sub>

## Pipeline
```mermaid
flowchart LR
  N0["§4 Methods"]
  N1["§4.1 Finetuning"]
  N2["§4.2 Verification"]
  N0 --> N1
  N1 --> N2
```

![A comparison between finetuning and verification using 6B and 175B model sizes. Verification considers 100 solutions per problem. Mean and standard deviation is shown across 3 runs, except for 175B verification which shows only a single run.](https://arxiv.org/html/2110.14168/2110.14168v2/full_completion_vf_6b.svg)

*A comparison between finetuning and verification using 6B and 175B model sizes. Verification considers 100 solutions per problem. Mean and standard deviation is shown across 3 runs, except for 175B verification which shows only a single run.*

<sub>figure: `2110.14168v2/full_completion_vf_6b.svg`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. Methods

We investigate two methods to solve problems in GSM8K: finetuning and verification. Finetuning, our baseline method, uses the same language modeling objective as the generative pretraining in GPT-3 ( Brown et al., 2020 ) . At test time, we judge performance by autoregressively sampling a single low temperature solution and checking whether the final answer is correct. In contrast, verification consists of sampling multiple high temperature solutions, assigning each solution a score, and outputting the highest ranked solution. Verifiers are trained to judge the correctness of solutions, with the training signal determined solely by whether or not the solution reached the correct final answer. For both methods, we use models from the GPT-3 family as our initialization, primarily focusing on the 175B and 6B model sizes. The 175B model is the largest and produces the most impressive results, while the 6B model is significantly more convenient for research purposes. We discuss hyperparameter choices in Appendix B . Our models frequently fail to accurately perform calculations. Although larger models make fewer arithmetic mistakes than smaller models, this remains a common source of errors. To mitigate this issue, we train all models to use a calculator by injecting calculation annotations into the training set. At test time, a calculator will override sampling when the model chooses to use these annotations. Details can be found in Appendix C . Finetune a model (the “generator”) for 2 epochs on the training set. Sample 100 completions from the generator for each training problem and label each solution as correct or incorrect. Train a verifier for a single epoch on this dataset.

<sub>§4 Methods</sub>

### 2. Finetuning

We perform finetuning by updating model parameters to minimize the cross-entropy loss over all training tokens. Figure 2 shows test performance after finetuning on training sets of varying sizes for 20 epochs. We visualize the same data both as a function of training set size and as a function of model size. Test performance is determined by a single low temperature ( T = 0 T=0 ) sample for each test problem. Unsurprisingly, we see that the 175B model significantly outperforms the smaller models. Assuming a log-linear trend, we can naively extrapolate these results to estimate that a model with 10 16 10^{16} parameters would be required to reach an 80 % 80\% solve rate, when using the full GSM8K training set. It is even harder to extrapolate along the data dimension, since performance does not appear to follow a log-linear trend. Nevertheless, it appears likely that the 175B model would require at least two additional orders of magnitude of training data to reach an 80 % 80\% solve rate. In Figure 3 , we show how 6B test performance varies over the course of 100 training epochs. We use test@N to denote the percentage of problems solved correctly at least once when allowing the model to make N separate guesses for each problem. We use a low temperature ( T = 0 T=0 ) to generate test@1 samples and we use a higher temperature ( T = 0.7 T=0.7 ) to generate test@100 samples. Both temperature values were chosen empirically to produce the best results. Test@1 performance improves approximately monotonically, even though we quickly begin overfitting on test loss. Unfortunately, test@100 performance degrades much more sharply than test@1 as we increase the number of epochs. This is to be expected: as the model repeatedly encounters the same data, it becomes increasingly uncalibrated and overconfident in its predictions. At test time, this overconfidence leads to poor coverage of the solution space, an effect which only becomes noticeable when we are considering multiple samples at test time. Choosing a model with good coverage is critical to successfully train verifiers. Empirically, we see that test@100 performance peaks within the first few epochs. For this reason, we use models trained for 2 epochs to generate samples for training verifiers. We provide several example solutions from 6B and 175B models in Appendix D . We also note that it is important to allow the model to generate the full natural language solution before outputting a final answer. If we instead finetune a 6B model to directly output the final answer without any intermediate steps, performance drops drastically from 20.6% to 5.2%.

<sub>§4.1 Finetuning</sub>

### 3. Verification

To improve upon the finetuning baseline, we train verifiers to judge the correctness of model-generated solutions and search against these verifiers at test time. Conditioned on the problem and a candidate solution, the verifier outputs the probability that the solution is correct. Training solutions are labeled as correct or incorrect based solely on whether they reach the correct final answer. In practice, some solutions will reach the correct final answer using flawed reasoning, leading to false positives. As shown in Figure 4 , we train the verifier as follows: Training for 2 epochs is enough for the generator to learn basic skills in this domain. We choose not to train for longer, since the diversity of generated solutions begins to collapse after this point, as shown in Figure 3 . We train separate generator and verifier models to limit the generator’s training and prevent overfitting, but in principle, it should be possible to combine these models. Unless otherwise specified, we use the same model size for the generator and the verifier. In addition to predicting solution correctness, we also train the verifier with the same language modeling objective as the generator. This serves as a valuable auxiliary objective for the verifier. We discuss additional verifier training details in Appendix E . At test time, we sample 100 completions to each test problem, rank them with the verifier, and then return the one with the highest verifier score. A comparison between verification and finetuning is shown in Figure 5 for both the 6B and 175B model sizes. We find that it is not beneficial to use verification at low dataset sizes. We believe this is due to the pressure to overfit to the correct answer: with small datasets, overfitting to the correct answer happens faster than learning more generalizable properties of correct reasoning. However, once we use a sufficiently large dataset, we see a strong boost from verifiers. It’s interesting to note that the 175B verifiers “take off” earlier than the 6B verifiers, requiring fewer training problems to surpass the finetuning baseline. See Appendix D for example solutions found by verifiers and Appendix F for a visualization of verifier confidence.

<sub>§4.2 Verification</sub>

## 总结与展望
训练验证器那篇在评估预算方向里出现，是因为它给出了「用采样换准确率」的最早形态：多采几个答案、用验证器挑。它的隐含假设是「多采一定更好」，而这个假设在预算受限时并不成立。

**It connects to**

- `2203.11171` — 自一致性的多数投票是同一思路的无监督版本。

## 不足之处
**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 论文不讨论预算分配，只讨论固定采样数下的效果。

# Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models

arXiv:2203.11171 · 2023 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2203.11171)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
Although language models have demonstrated remarkable success across a range of NLP tasks, their ability to demonstrate reasoning is often seen as a limitation, which cannot be overcome solely by increasing model scale ( Rae et al., 2021 ; BIG-bench collaboration, 2021 , inter alia ) . In an effort to address this shortcoming, Wei et al. (2022) have proposed chain-of-thought prompting , where a language model is prompted to generate a series of short sentences that mimic the reasoning process a person might employ in solving a task. For example, given the question “If there are 3 cars in the parking lot and 2 more cars arrive, how many cars are in the parking lot?” , instead of directly responding with “5” , a language model would be prompted to respond with the entire chain-of-thought: “There are 3 cars in the parking lot already. 2 more arrive. Now there are 3 + 2 = 5 cars. The answer is 5.” . It has been observed that chain-of-thought prompting significantly improves model performance across a variety of multi-step reasoning tasks ( Wei et al., 2022 ) .

<sub>§1 Introduction</sub>

## Pipeline

![The self-consistency method contains three steps: (1) prompt a language model using chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting; (2) replace the “greedy decode” in CoT prompting by sampling from the language model’s decoder to generate a diverse set of reasoning paths; and (3) marginalize out the reasoning paths and aggregate by choosing the most consistent answer in the final answer set.](https://arxiv.org/html/2203.11171/2203.11171v4/self_consistency_fig.svg)

*The self-consistency method contains three steps: (1) prompt a language model using chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting; (2) replace the “greedy decode” in CoT prompting by sampling from the language model’s decoder to generate a diverse set of reasoning paths; and (3) marginalize out the reasoning paths and aggregate by choosing the most consistent answer in the final answer set.*

<sub>figure: `2203.11171v4/self_consistency_fig.svg`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. Experiments

We conducted a series of experiments to compare the proposed self-consistency method with existing approaches on a range of reasoning benchmarks. We find that self-consistency robustly improves reasoning accuracy for every language model considered, spanning a wide range of model scales. Arithmetic reasoning . For these tasks, we used the Math Word Problem Repository ( Koncel-Kedziorski et al., 2016 ) , including AddSub ( Hosseini et al., 2014 ) , MultiArith ( Roy & Roth, 2015 ) , and ASDiv ( Miao et al., 2020 ) . We also included AQUA-RAT ( Ling et al., 2017 ) , a recently published benchmark of grade-school-math problems ( Cobbe et al., 2021 , GSM8K;) , and a challenge dataset over math word problems ( Patel et al., 2021 , SVAMP;) . Commonsense reasoning . For these tasks, we used CommonsenseQA ( Talmor et al., 2019 ) , StrategyQA ( Geva et al., 2021 ) , and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC) ( Clark et al., 2018 ) . Symbolic Reasoning . We evaluate two symbolic reasoning tasks: last letter concatenation (e.g., the input is “Elon Musk” and the output should be “nk”), and Coinflip (e.g., a coin is heads-up, after a few flips is the coin still heads-up?) from Wei et al. (2022) . UL2 ( Tay et al., 2022 ) is an encoder-decoder model trained on a mixture of denoisers with 20-billion parameters. UL2 is completely open-sourced 4 4 4 Model checkpoints at https://github.com/google-research/google-research/tree/master/ul2 . and has similar or better performance than GPT-3 on zero-shot SuperGLUE, with only 20B parameters and thus is more compute-friendly; GPT-3 ( Brown et al., 2020 ) with 175-billion parameters. We use two public engines code-davinci-001 and code-davinci-002 from the Codex series ( Chen et al., 2021 ) to aid reproducibility; 5 5 5 Public API available at https://openai.com/api/ . LaMDA-137B ( Thoppilan et al., 2022 ) is a dense left-to-right, decoder-only language model with 137-billion parameters, pre-trained on a mixture of web documents, dialog data and Wikipedia; PaLM-540B ( Chowdhery et al., 2022 ) is a dense left-to-right, decoder-only language model with 540-billion parameters, pre-trained on a high quality corpus of 780 billion tokens with filtered webpages, books, Wikipedia, news articles, source code, and social media conversations.

<sub>§3 Experiments</sub>

### 2. Tasks and datasets.

We evaluate self-consistency on the following reasoning benchmarks. 3 3 3 By default we use the test split for all datasets if the labels are available for evaluation. For CommonsenseQA we use the dev split; for StrategyQA we use the question-only set from BIG-bench collaboration (2021) : https://github.com/google/BIG-bench/tree/main/bigbench/benchmark_tasks/strategyqa .

<sub>§Tasks and datasets.</sub>

### 3. Language models and prompts.

We evaluate self-consistency over four transformer-based language models with varying scales: We perform all experiments in the few-shot setting, without training or fine-tuning the language models. For a fair comparison we use the same prompts as in Wei et al. (2022) : for all arithmetic reasoning tasks we use the same set of 8 manually written exemplars; for each commonsense reasoning task, 4-7 exemplars are randomly chosen from the training set with manually composed chain-of-thought prompts. 6 6 6 Self-consistency is robust to different sets of prompts and we provide a study in Appendix A.1.2 . Full details on the prompts used are given in Appendix A.3 .

<sub>§Language models and prompts.</sub>

### 4. Sampling scheme.

To sample diverse reasoning paths, we followed similar settings to those suggested in Radford et al. (2019) ; Holtzman et al. (2020) for open-text generation. In particular, for UL2-20B and LaMDA-137B we applied temperature sampling with T = 0.5 T=0.5 and truncated at the top- k k ( k = 40 k=40 ) tokens with the highest probability, for PaLM-540B we applied T = 0.7 , k = 40 T=0.7,k=40 , and for GPT-3 we use T = 0.7 T=0.7 without top- k k truncation. We provide an ablation study in Section 3.5 to show that self-consistency is generally robust to sampling strategies and parameters.

<sub>§Sampling scheme.</sub>

### 5. Arithmetic Reasoning

The results are shown in Table 2 . 7 7 7 The standard deviation of self-consistency is ≤ 0.5 \leq 0.5 for all tasks and is thus omitted in the table. Please refer to Figure 2 , Figure 7 and 8 for the standard deviations under varying numbers of sampled paths. Self-consistency improves the arithmetic reasoning performance over all four language models significantly over chain-of-thought prompting. More surprisingly, the gains become more significant when the language model’s scale increases, e.g., we see +3%-6% absolute accuracy improvement over UL2-20B but +9%-23% for LaMDA-137B and GPT-3. For larger models that already achieve high accuracy on most tasks (e.g., GPT-3 and PaLM-540B), self-consistency still contributes significant additional gains with +12%-18% absolute accuracy on tasks like AQuA and GSM8K, and +7%-11% on SVAMP and ASDiv. With self-consistency, we achieve new state-of-the-art results on almost all tasks: despite the fact that self-consistency is unsupervised and task-agnostic, these results compare favorably to existing approaches that require task-specific training, or fine-tuning with thousands of examples (e.g., on GSM8K).

<sub>§Arithmetic Reasoning</sub>

### 6. Commonsense and Symbolic Reasoning

Table 3 shows the results on commonsense and symbolic reasoning tasks. Similarly, self-consistency yields large gains across all four language models, and obtained SoTA results on 5 out of 6 tasks. For symbolic reasoning, we test the out-of-distribution (OOD) setting where the input prompt contains examples of 2-letters or 2-flips but we test examples of 4-letters and 4-flips (this setting is more challenging as PaLM-540B or GPT-3 can already achieve perfect in-distribution accuracy). In this challenging OOD setting, the gain of self-consistency is still quite significant compared to CoT-prompting with sufficient model sizes. To show the effect of the number of sampled reasoning paths, we plot the accuracy (mean and standard deviation over 10 runs) with respect to varying numbers of sampled paths (1, 5, 10, 20, 40) in Figure 2 . The results show that sampling a higher number (e.g., 40) of reasoning paths leads to a consistently better performance, further emphasizing the importance of introducing diversity in the reasoning paths. In Table 4 , we show self-consistency yields a richer set of reasoning paths compared to greedy decoding with a few example questions from two tasks.

<sub>§Commonsense and Symbolic Reasoning</sub>

### 7. Self-Consistency Helps When Chain-of-Thought Hurts Performance

Ye & Durrett (2022) show that sometimes chain-of-thought prompting could hurt performance compared to standard prompting in few-shot in-context learning. Here we perform a study using self-consistency to see if it can help fill in the gap, over a set of common NLP tasks, including (1) Closed-Book Question Answering: BoolQ ( Clark et al., 2019 ) , HotpotQA ( Yang et al., 2018 ) , and (2) Natural Language Inference: e-SNLI ( Camburu et al., 2018 ) , ANLI ( Nie et al., 2020 ) and RTE ( Dagan et al., 2005 ; Bar-Haim et al., 2006 ; Giampiccolo et al., 2007 ; Bentivogli et al., 2009 ) . The results over PaLM-540B are shown in Table 5 . For some tasks (e.g., ANLI-R1, e-SNLI, RTE), adding chain-of-thought does hurt performance compared to standard prompting ( Brown et al., 2020 ) , but self-consistency is able to robustly boost the performance and outperform standard prompting, making it a reliable way to add rationales in few-shot in-context learning for common NLP tasks.

<sub>§3.3 Self-Consistency Helps When Chain-of-Thought Hurts Performance</sub>

### 8. Compare to other existing approaches / Additional Studies

We conduct a set of additional studies and show that self-consistency significantly outperforms existing methods including sample-and-rank, beam search, and ensemble-based approaches. We conducted a number of additional experiments to analyze different aspects of the self-consistency method, including its robustness to sampling strategies and parameters, and how it works with imperfect prompts and non-natural-language reasoning paths.

<sub>§3.4 Compare to other existing approaches · §3.5 Additional Studies</sub>

### 9. Self-Consistency is Robust to Sampling Strategies and Scaling

We show self-consistency is robust to sampling strategies and parameters, by varying T T in temperature sampling ( Ackley et al., 1985 ; Ficler & Goldberg, 2017 ) , k k in top- k k sampling ( Fan et al., 2018 ; Holtzman et al., 2018 ; Radford et al., 2019 ) , and p p in nucleus sampling ( Holtzman et al., 2020 ) , over PaLM-540B in Figure 4 (left). Figure 4 (right) shows that self-consistency robustly improves performance across all scales for the LaMDA-137B model series. The gain is relatively lower for smaller models due to certain abilities (e.g., arithmetic) only emerge when the model reaches a sufficient scale ( Brown et al., 2020 ) .

<sub>§Self-Consistency is Robust to Sampling Strategies and Scaling</sub>

### 10. Self-Consistency Improves Robustness to Imperfect Prompts

For few-shot learning with manually constructed prompts, human annotators sometimes make minor mistakes when creating the prompts. We further study if self-consistency can help improve a language model’s robustness to imperfect prompts. 9 9 9 We use the same prompts as before, but swap all the numbers in the reasoning paths with random numbers except the final answer, e.g., from “ There are 3 cars in the parking lot already. 2 more arrive. Now there are 3 + 2 = 5 cars. ” to “ There are 7 cars in the parking lot already. 6 more arrive. Now there are 7 + 6 = 5 cars. ”. We show the results in Table 8 : while imperfect prompts decrease accuracy with greedy decoding (17.1 → \rightarrow 14.9), self-consistency can fill in the gaps and robustly improve the results. Additionally, we found that the consistency (in terms of % of decodes agreeing with the final aggregated answer) is highly correlated with accuracy (Figure 8 , over GSM8K). This suggests that one can use self-consistency to provide an uncertainty estimate of the model in its generated solutions. In other words, one can use low consistency as an indicator that the model has low confidence; i.e., self-consistency confers some ability for the model to “know when it doesn’t know”.

<sub>§Self-Consistency Improves Robustness to Imperfect Prompts</sub>

### 11. Self-Consistency Works for Non-Natural-Language Reasoning Paths and Zero-shot CoT

We also tested the generality of the self-consistency concept to alternative forms of intermediate reasoning like equations (e.g., from “ There are 3 cars in the parking lot already. 2 more arrive. Now there are 3 + 2 = 5 cars. ” to “ 3 + 2 = 5 ”). The results are shown in Table 8 (“Prompt with equations”): self-consistency still improves accuracy by generating intermediate equations; however, compared to generating natural language reasoning paths, the gain is smaller since the equations are much shorter and less opportunity remains for generating diversity in the decoding process. In addition, we tested self-consistency with zero-shot chain-of-thought ( Kojima et al., 2022 ) and show that self-consistency works for zero-shot CoT as well and improves the results significantly (+26.2%) in Table 8 .

<sub>§Self-Consistency Works for Non-Natural-Language Reasoning Paths and Zero-shot CoT</sub>

## 总结与展望
自一致性用多数投票把多次采样合成一个答案，是「重复换稳定」的最朴素做法。它在预算方向的含义是：重复的收益随次数递减，而边际收益从未被显式建模。

**It connects to**

- `2110.14168` — 一个用验证器挑，一个用投票合，都需要多次采样。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** We introduced a simple yet effective method called self-consistency, and observed that it significantly improves accuracy in a range of arithmetic and commonsense reasoning tasks, across four large language models with varying scales. Beyond accuracy gains, self-consistency is also useful for collecting rationales when performing reasoning tasks with language models, and for providing uncertainty estimates and improved calibration of language model outputs.

**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 投票假设错误是独立的，这个假设在同一个模型上通常不成立。

<sub>§5 Conclusion and Discussion</sub>

<sub>§Chapter 2 Validity: E-values under the null hypothesis</sub>

### 2. Testing using Markov’s inequality

As mentioned before, e-values can be interpreted in their own right as evidence against the null hypothesis without resort to other concepts. However, the reader may naturally be curious how exactly e-values may be transformed (perhaps with some loss of efficiency) into the more classical concepts of level- α \alpha tests and p-values. It is in this context that a central role will be played by Markov’s inequality (though it apparently was discovered by Chebyshev, Markov’s advisor). Markov’s inequality guarantees that a test rejecting an e-value larger than 1 / α 1/\alpha yields a level- α \alpha test. The proof follows directly from the simple inequality 𝟙 { x ≥ 1 } ≤ x \mathds{1}_{\{x\geq 1\}}\leq x for all x ≥ 0 x\geq 0 , by observing that For an e-variable E E and a p-variable P P , even though 1 / E 1/E is a p-variable, 1 / P 1/P is generally not an e-variable; in fact 1 / P 1/P has infinite mean if P P is exact. The p-variable 1 / E 1/E is conservative since it cannot be uniformly distributed on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] , and it is typically far away from being so. As a consequence, the type-I error of the test 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} for ℙ \mathbb{P} is usually smaller than α \alpha unless E E is distributed on the two points 0 0 and 1 / α 1/\alpha with mean 1 1 under ℙ \mathbb{P} . Proposition 2.1 can be generalized in several different directions relevant for testing with e-values; some are presented in Section 2.4 and Chapters 5 and 15 . A sequential generalization, called Ville’s inequality, is presented in Chapter 7 . With a p-variable P P and an e-variable E E for the same hypothesis, the tests 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} and 𝟙 { P ≤ α } \mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}} are in general different, except for the special case E = 𝟙 { P ≤ α } / α E=\mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}}/\alpha (see Section 3.1 for more details on such e-variables). To understand how these two tests differ, we consider the two-sided normal test in Section 1.6 , where a family of e-variables E n E_{n} is given by ( 1.3 ), indexed by δ > 0 \delta>0 , and the p-variable P n P_{n} is given by ( 1.4 ). We treat both the e-variables and the p-variable as functions of the value x x taken by the statistic Z Z . Figure 2.2 illustrates two intervals [ A 1 , A 2 ] [A_{1},A_{2}] and [ B 1 , B 2 ] [B_{1},B_{2}] , which correspond to P n ​ ( x ) = 0.01 P_{n}(x)=0.01 for x = A 1 x=A_{1} or x = A 2 x=A_{2} and E n ​ ( x ) = 100 E_{n}(x)=100 for x = B 1 x=B_{1} or x = B 2 x=B_{2} , with δ = 3 \delta=3 . Here, A 2 = | A 1 | ≈ 2.58 A_{2}=|A_{1}|\approx 2.58 and B 2 = | B 1 | ≈ 3.27 B_{2}=|B_{1}|\approx 3.27 . Other choices of δ \delta lead to different intervals, as shown in Figure 2.2 . We can see that it is harder for an e-value to reach 100 100 compared to a p-value to reach 0.01 0.01 , and this is because the Markov inequality is generally conservative. It is generally unfair to directly compare the values of E E and 1 / P 1/P . See Section 2.7 for more discussions on this point.

<sub>§2.1 Testing using Markov’s inequality</sub>

### 3. Testing using distributional knowledge

Proposition 2.1 implies that for an e-variable E E and α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , we can always reject the null hypothesis by using the level- α \alpha test 𝟙 { E ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq 1/\alpha\}} . If the distribution of E E under the null hypothesis is known or computable (there may be multiple distributions if the null is composite), then we can find thresholds that are smaller than 1 / α 1/\alpha to test based on E E . Denote by S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) the left-continuous survival function of E E under ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , that is, for x ∈ ℝ . x\in\mathbb{R}. If S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) is the same across ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , then E E is called pivotal (this will appear again in Section 14.3 ). The function S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) can be continuous or discontinuous, depending on both E E and ℙ \mathbb{P} . For a threshold t > 0 t>0 , we would like the test 𝟙 { E ≥ t } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t\}} to have level α \alpha . Clearly, choosing t ≥ 1 / α t\geq 1/\alpha is sufficient, but we can do better. Indeed, by definition, as long as sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 S ⁡ ( t | ℙ ) ≤ α \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}S(t|\mathbb{P})\leq\alpha , t t is a valid threshold for the test 𝟙 { E ≥ t } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t\}} with level α \alpha . If sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 S ⁡ ( t | ℙ ) \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}S(t|\mathbb{P}) is continuous, then the smallest t t that satisfies this condition is which is also equal to the largest left α \alpha -quantile of E E over ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} (Lemma 15.1 in Section 15.1 ). In particular, if 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} is simple, then we can easily see that which is the left α \alpha -quantile of E E under ℙ \mathbb{P} . The level- α \alpha guarantee of 𝟙 { E ≥ t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t^{*}\}} requires t ↦ sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 S ⁡ ( t | ℙ ) t\mapsto\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}S(t|\mathbb{P}) to be continuous at t ∗ t^{*} ; otherwise we can use the test 𝟙 { E > t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E>t^{*}\}} , or a suitable randomization between 𝟙 { E > t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E>t^{*}\}} and 𝟙 { E ≥ t ∗ } \mathds{1}_{\{E\geq t^{*}\}} (analogously to the Neyman–Pearson lemma). Reducing the threshold in the above manner is in fact no different from treating the e-value as any other test statistic and picking the threshold appropriately to derive a level- α \alpha test, as is standard in classical statistics. Even in situations where S ⁡ ( ⋅ , ℙ ) S(\cdot;\mathbb{P}) is difficult to fully specify or compute, bounds on t ∗ t^{*} may be obtained based on partial information of the distribution of the e-value. Several concrete cases will be studied in Chapter 15 .

<sub>§2.2 Testing using distributional knowledge</sub>

### 4. Relating p-values and e-values using calibrators

P-values and e-values can be converted between each other, via calibrators. Below, when we say “any hypothesis”, we mean any specification of the sample space ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) and hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} on the space. A p-to-e calibrator is a decreasing function f : [ 0 , ∞ ) → [ 0 , ∞ ] f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty] satisfying f = 0 f=0 on ( 1 , ∞ ) (1,\infty) such that for any hypothesis, f ⁡ ( P ) f(P) is an e-variable for any p-variable P P . An e-to-p calibrator is a decreasing function g : [ 0 , ∞ ] → [ 0 , ∞ ) g:[0,\infty]\to[0,\infty) such that for any hypothesis, g ⁡ ( E ) g(E) is a p-variable for any e-variable E E . A calibrator f f is said to dominate a calibrator g g if f ≥ g f\geq g (p-to-e) or f ≤ g f\leq g (e-to-p), and the domination is strict if further f ≠ g f\neq g . A calibrator is admissible if it is not strictly dominated by any other calibrator. We often omit “p-to-e” in “p-to-e calibrators” and simply call them calibrators, for a reason that will be made clear soon in Propositions 2.4 and 2.5 . The interval [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) can be safely replaced by [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] in the above definitions since p-values with values larger than 1 1 are not interesting for testing. A probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} (or the corresponding probability space) is called atomless if there exists a continuously distributed random variable (e.g., uniform, normal) under ℙ \mathbb{P} . Hence, when we encounter a continuously distributed random variable, implicitly the probability measure is assumed atomless. In Definition 2.3 , the property of calibrators needs to hold for any hypothesis. Nevertheless, it suffices to consider one atomless probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} on some ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) , because if for the simple hypothesis ℙ \mathbb{P} , f ⁡ ( P ) f(P) is an e-variable for any p-variable P P , then it holds also for any other, simple or composite, hypotheses. This observation will be useful in several places throughout. A formal justification of this simplification is presented in Appendix A.1 . In the next two results we characterize all admissible calibrators in both directions. We first look at the simpler direction. The following proposition says that there is, essentially, only one e-to-p calibrator, f : t ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 , 1 / t ) f:t\mapsto\min(1,1/t) , that is offered by Markov’s inequality. Instead of working with the set of all e-variables, we can also consider conditional e-to-p calibrators that are valid only on some subset ℰ \mathcal{E} of e-variables, which is a weaker requirement, and such calibrators depend on the choice of ℰ \mathcal{E} . This topic is treated in Chapter 15 . In sharp contrast to the uniqueness of the admissible e-to-p calibrator, the class of p-to-e calibrators is much richer. In the context of this proposition, being upper semicontinuous is equivalent to being left-continuous. Below we present some simple examples of admissible p-to-e calibrators. We only specify them on ( 0 , 1 ] (0,1] , as they are ∞ \infty at 0 0 and 0 0 on ( 1 , ∞ ) (1,\infty) . These calibrators include the power form Converting a p-value to an e-value using a p-to-e calibrator and then back to p-value using an e-to-p calibrator generally loses quite a lot of evidence. For instance, starting with p = 0.01 p=0.01 , a conversion with the p-to-e calibrator p ↦ p − 1 / 2 − 1 p\mapsto p^{-1/2}-1 gives e = 9 e=9 , and another conversion with the e-to-p calibrator e ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 / e , 1 ) e\mapsto\min(1/e,1) yields p ′ = 1 / 9 p^{\prime}=1/9 . Therefore, it is not recommended in practice to calibrate iteratively in both directions. A possible interpretation of the above results on calibrators is that e-variables and p-variables are connected via a very rough relation 1 / e ∼ p 1/e\sim p . In one direction, the statement is precise: the reciprocal (truncated to 1 if needed) of an e-variable is a p-variable by Proposition 2.4 . On the other hand, using a calibrator f ⁡ ( p ) = κ ​ p κ − 1 f(p)=\kappa p^{\kappa-1} in ( 2.1 ) with a small κ > 0 \kappa>0 and ignoring positive constant factors, we can see that the reciprocal of a p-variable is approximately an e-variable; this is also the case of ( 2.2 ). In fact, f ⁡ ( p ) ≤ 1 / p f(p)\leq 1/p for all p p when f f is a calibrator; otherwise ∫ 0 1 f ⁡ ( q ) ​ 𝑑 q ≥ p ​ f ​ ( p ) > 1 \int_{0}^{1}f(q)\mathrm{d}q\geq pf(p)>1 , conflicting the condition in Proposition 2.5 . However, f ⁡ ( p ) = 1 / p f(p)=1/p for a fixed p p is only possible in the extreme case f : q ↦ 𝟙 { q ∈ [ 0 , p ] } / p f:q\mapsto\mathds{1}_{\{q\in[0,p]\}}/p . In the rest of the book, we omit “p-to-e” when mentioning calibrators, unless we want to stress the direction. Although all calibrators in ( 2.1 )–( 2.5 ) are admissible, some are more useful. In hypothesis testing, very small p-values and very large e-values are interesting. Calibrators in ( 2.3 ), ( 2.4 ) and ( 2.5 ) penalize very small p-values more than ( 2.2 ), which can be seen from their asymptotic behaviour as p ↓ 0 p\downarrow 0 , and therefore they are less likely to produce very large e-values; the calibrator in ( 2.3 ) is even bounded by 2 2 for p ≠ 0 p\neq 0 . Nevertheless, they may be useful when we take the product of many independent e-values. We end this section with a simple property of admissible calibrators. It also explains why we choose the domain of the calibrators as [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) instead of [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] , although its value is set to 0 0 for input p-values larger than 1 1 . This result will be useful for later results in Chapter 12 . The next result states that any e-variable E E for an atomless probability measure ℙ \mathbb{P} can be seen as some calibrator applied to an exact p-variable. Indeed, as we see from the proof of the result below, this calibrator is a transform of the quantile function of E E .

<sub>§2.3 Relating p-values and e-values using calibrators</sub>

### 5. Randomized tests and calibrators

A randomized test is a test that utilizes independent external randomization. It is possible to improve Markov’s inequality using external randomization, and this has direct implications for converting e-variables into tests or p-variables. The proof is simple. Since U U is uniform and independent of Y Y , we get yielding the claim. The first equality in ( 2.6 ) becomes an inequality ≤ \leq if U U is stochastically larger than standard uniform, and in particular U = 1 U=1 yields an alternative proof of Markov’s inequality. Also, if Y Y is bounded, that is Y ∈ [ 0 , C ] Y\in[0,C] almost surely for some C > 0 C>0 , then the inequality in ( 2.6 ) holds with equality for any α ≤ 1 / C \alpha\leq 1/C . In contrast, Markov’s inequality holds with equality only for distributions supported on { 0 , 1 / α } \{0,1/\alpha\} . The following corollary is a direct consequence of Theorem 2.9 , which offers additional interpretations of the relation between p-variables, e-variables, and randomization. Since U < 1 U<1 almost surely, U / E U/E is a strictly smaller p-value than 1 / E 1/E in all but degenerate cases. Thus, the corresponding randomized test is typically strictly more powerful than the non-randomized one. Said differently, e ↦ ( U / e ) ∧ 1 e\mapsto(U/e)\wedge 1 can be seen as a randomized e-to-p calibrator . In fact, this randomized e-to-p calibrator is admissible (treated in Section 11.3 ) and it dominates e ↦ ( 1 / e ) ∧ 1 e\mapsto(1/e)\wedge 1 , the latter only being admissible among non-randomized e-to-p calibrators (Proposition 2.4 ). A more general treatment of combining a p-value and an e-value is presented in Section 11.3 .

<sub>§2.4 Randomized tests and calibrators</sub>

### 6. Admissible e-variables

In this section, we consider admissible e-variables, which are, intuitively, e-variables that cannot be enlarged, formally defined below. Equivalently, we may say that an e-variable is admissible if it is not dominated by another e-variable, where by domination of E ′ E^{\prime} over E E , we mean E ′ ≥ E E^{\prime}\geq E and ℙ ⁡ ( E ′ > E ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(E^{\prime}>E)>0 for some ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . A trivial example is that the constant 1 1 is an admissible e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . The above definition does not use any alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , but if all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and ℚ \mathbb{Q} are mutually absolutely continuous, then E E being admissible means that one cannot find another e-variable E ′ E^{\prime} such that ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ ≥ E ) = 1 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}\geq E)=1 and ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ > E ) > 0 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}>E)>0 . Indeed, if ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ ≥ E ) = 1 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}\geq E)=1 and ℚ ⁡ ( E ′ > E ) > 0 \mathbb{Q}(E^{\prime}>E)>0 hold for an e-variable E ′ E^{\prime} , then E ′ ∨ E E^{\prime}\vee E is an e-variable that dominates E E . for each A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} with 𝒫 A ≠ ∅ \mathcal{P}_{A}\neq\varnothing , there exists ℙ ∈ 𝒫 A \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{A} such that 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 ; for each ℙ ′ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P} , there exists ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} such that ℙ ′ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\ll\mathbb{P} and 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 ;

<sub>§2.5 Admissible e-variables</sub>

### 7. Admissible e-variables for a simple null are likelihood ratios / Characterization of admissibility

We first make a straightforward but important observation: For simple nulls, every admissible e-variable can be written as a likelihood ratio. We now characterize admissible e-variables for general 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . For A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} and δ > 0 \delta>0 , let 𝒫 A δ = { ℙ ∈ 𝒫 : ℙ ⁡ ( A ) > δ } \mathcal{P}^{\delta}_{A}=\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{P}(A)>\delta\} and 𝒫 A = { ℙ ∈ 𝒫 : ℙ ⁡ ( A ) > 0 } \mathcal{P}_{A}=\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{P}(A)>0\} . The first result gives a simple necessary condition for admissible e-variables, which boils down to the exactness condition in Proposition 2.12 when 𝒫 = { ℙ } . \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\}. Then next result gives a full characterization of admissible e-variables using its expectations under ℙ ∈ 𝒫 A δ \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{A}^{\delta} for A ∈ ℱ A\in\mathcal{F} . In this result, we use the convention inf ∅ = 0 \inf\varnothing=0 . Several sufficient conditions for ( 2.8 ) are easier to check, summarized in the next corollary. The condition (a) in Corollary 2.15 deserves some further attention. We will say that an e-variable is strongly admissible if condition (a) in Corollary 2.15 holds; note that (a) is implied by either of (b) and (c). This condition is strictly stronger than admissibility for infinite 𝒫 \mathcal{P} as characterized in Theorem 2.14 (for which there need not be any ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} with 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 ). When 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is finite, strong admissibility is equivalent to admissibility, as shown in the next result.

<sub>§2.5.1 Admissible e-variables for a simple null are likelihood ratios · §2.5.2 Characterization of admissibility</sub>

### 8. Admissible e-variables on product spaces

Next, we consider e-variables defined on product spaces. Let 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} be a set of probabilities on another measurable space ( Ω ′ , ℱ ′ ) (\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}) . We write 𝒫 × 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime} for the set { ℙ × ℙ ′ : ℙ ∈ 𝒫 , ℙ ′ ∈ 𝒫 ′ } \{\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}^{\prime}:\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P},~\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\} of product probabilities. For E E on Ω \Omega and E ′ E^{\prime} on Ω ′ \Omega^{\prime} , let E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} be defined by ( ω , ω ′ ) ↦ E ⁡ ( ω ) ​ E ′ ​ ( ω ′ ) (\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mapsto E(\omega)E^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime}) . Clearly, if E E is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and E ′ E^{\prime} is an e-variable for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , then E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is an e-variable for 𝒫 × 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime} , because for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and ℙ ′ ∈ 𝒫 ′ \mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime} . The next result further shows that E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is strongly admissible when E E and E ′ E^{\prime} are strongly admissible. Combining Propositions 2.16 and 2.17 , we obtain that E × E ′ E\times E^{\prime} is an admissible e-variable for admissible e-variables E E and E ′ E^{\prime} when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} are finite.

<sub>§2.5.3 Admissible e-variables on product spaces</sub>

### 9. More examples of e-values

Continuing from the examples in Chapter 1 , we now provide several simple examples of e-values for different testing problems. Some of these e-values also enjoy a notion of optimality, introduced in Chapter 3 and analyzed in detail in Chapter 6 , so these examples will be revisited later in the book. In most examples below, we consider a single random observation (statistic) Z Z in a measurable space 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} and the sample space is chosen as ( Ω , ℱ ) = ( 𝒵 , σ ⁡ ( Z ) ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F})=(\mathcal{Z},\sigma(Z)) ; thus ℳ 1 \mathcal{M}_{1} is the set of all probability measures on σ ⁡ ( Z ) \sigma(Z) .
