### 10. Monotone likelihood ratio

Let ( ℙ θ ) θ ∈ Θ (\mathbb{P}_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta} be a parametric family of distributions with their density functions of the test statistic Z Z given by ( p θ ) θ ∈ Θ (p_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta} , where Θ ⊆ ℝ \Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R} and 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} . Assume that monotone likelihood ratio holds: for all θ < θ ′ \theta<\theta^{\prime} , the likelihood ratio p θ ′ ​ ( z ) / p θ ​ ( z ) {p_{\theta^{\prime}}(z)}/{p_{\theta}(z)} exists and is increasing (or decreasing) in z z . The following short proof uses Chebyshev’s association inequality, which states, assuming integrability, 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ​ g ​ ( X ) ] ≥ 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ f ⁡ ( X ) ] ​ 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ g ⁡ ( X ) ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[f(X)g(X)]\geq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[f(X)]\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[g(X)] for any increasing functions f , g f,g and random variable X X . The same conclusion in Proposition 2.18 holds true for testing 𝒫 = { ℙ θ : θ ≥ θ 0 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\geq\theta_{0}\} against 𝒬 = { ℙ θ : θ ≤ θ 1 } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\leq\theta_{1}\} with θ 0 > θ 1 \theta_{0}>\theta_{1} ; moreover, either inequality (or both) in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} can be replaced by a strict inequality. The same e-variable can also be used to test 𝒫 = { ℙ θ : θ ≤ θ 0 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\leq\theta_{0}\} against 𝒬 = { ℙ θ : θ > θ 0 } . \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta>\theta_{0}\}. The example of normal distributions in Section 1.6 satisfies the monotone likelihood ratio assumption. Therefore, the e-variable in ( 1.2 ) for any μ > 0 \mu>0 is an e-variable for testing { N ⁡ ( θ , 1 ) : θ ≤ 0 } \{\mathrm{N}(\theta,1):\theta\leq 0\} against { N ⁡ ( θ , 1 ) : θ > 0 } \{\mathrm{N}(\theta,1):\theta>0\} . An important special case is given by one-parameter exponential families, which we discuss further in Section 6.7.1 . There, and in Section 6.7.2 , we will argue that the above e-variable is actually log-optimal for ℚ = ℙ θ 1 \mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{\theta_{1}} for any θ 1 > θ 0 \theta_{1}>\theta_{0} , simply because p θ 1 / p θ 0 p_{\theta_{1}}/p_{\theta_{0}} is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} .

### 12. Testing the mean and the variance

Let 𝒵 ⊆ ℝ + \mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+} so that the statistic Z Z takes nonnegative values. Fix μ > 0 \mu>0 and consider the null hypothesis that the mean of Z Z is at most μ \mu , The alternative hypothesis 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is that mean of Z Z is larger than μ \mu . Two natural e-variables are Z / μ Z/\mu and the constant one. All convex combinations of these are also e-variables; therefore, is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . This construction works outside ℝ + \mathbb{R}_{+} as long as Z Z is bounded from below, by using ( Z − z ) / ( μ − z ) (Z-z)/(\mu-z) in place of Z / μ Z/\mu , where z z is a lower bound on Z Z . An optimal choice of λ \lambda against a specific ℚ \mathbb{Q} is discussed in Section 6.7.4 in the case that 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} is a bounded interval. Corollary 2.15 (b) can be used to deduce that the above e-variable is admissible, despite not being exact. We can also consider the null hypothesis that the mean of Z Z is at most μ \mu and the variance is at most σ 2 \sigma^{2} , In this case, because 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ Z 2 ] ≤ μ 2 + σ 2 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[Z^{2}]\leq\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2} for ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , each of is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , in addition to those in ( 2.10 ), as well as the mixtures of ( 2.10 ) and ( 2.11 ). If 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} , meaning that Z Z may take negative values, then ( 2.11 ) is an e-variable for As a further example, suppose that we observe nonnegative data Z = ( X 1 , … , X n ) Z=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}) with the same marginal distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} and possibly dependent components, and we wish to test whether ℙ \mathbb{P} has a mean at most μ \mu . Then, ( X 1 + ⋯ + X n ) / ( n ​ μ ) (X_{1}+\dots+X_{n})/(n\mu) is an e-value for any dependence structure of ( X 1 , … , X n ) (X_{1},\dots,X_{n}) , and does not require making any distributional or independence assumption. An e-variable can be constructed similarly for the case of testing the mean and variance. This example shows the advantage of e-values when it comes to dependent data, and this will be further explored in Chapter 9 .

### 13. Testing sub-Gaussian means

Now take 𝒵 = ℝ \mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R} and consider the null hypothesis that the observation Z Z has a 1-sub-Gaussian distribution with nonpositive mean. This class is a nonparametric generalization of Gaussians with nonpositive mean and unit variance. Formally, we set Here the sub-Gaussian restriction is only placed on the right tail. For ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R} , let M ℙ ​ ( λ ) = 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ e λ ​ Z ] M_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda)=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[e^{\lambda Z}] , which is the moment generating function of Z Z . The constraint in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} means A Taylor expansion in a neighborhood of λ = 0 \lambda=0 yields, Therefore, for λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 small enough, and this implies 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ Z ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[Z]\leq 0 . Since the standard Gaussian distribution (zero mean, unit variance) satisfy the above with equality (inferred via their moment generating function) for every real λ \lambda , the constraint expands the class to account for two aspects: the mean can be negative, and the right tails of the random variable only have to be lighter than that of a unit-variance Gaussian. It is well-known that any bounded distribution on [ − 1 , 1 ] [-1,1] with zero mean is 1-sub-Gaussian; so the class 𝒫 \mathcal{P} clearly does not have any common reference measure, containing discrete distributions with uncountable support as well as continuous unbounded distributions. A class of e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is given by: and certainly any convex combinations (mixtures) of its elements are also e-variables. The determination of the optimal e-variable against a specific ℚ \mathbb{Q} is studied in Section 6.7.5 . The determination of all possible e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is a nontrivial task. We also consider the closely related null hypothesis that the observation Z Z has a 1-sub-Gaussian distribution with zero (instead of nonpositive) mean: Here, the sub-Gaussian restriction is placed on both the left and the right tails. Every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 0 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{0} has zero mean, because now ( 2.13 ) holds for both positive and negative λ \lambda . Again, a natural class of e-variables for 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}^{0} is and certainly any convex combinations (mixtures) of its elements are also e-variables. It turns out that every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 0 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{0} also has variance bounded by one, and this follows from ( 2.13 ) with 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ Z ] = 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[Z]=0 . Thus, Z 2 Z^{2} is also an e-variable, as is any convex combination with the aforementioned class. Indeed, 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}^{0} is a subset of the hypothesis in ( 2.12 ) with μ = 0 \mu=0 and σ = 1 \sigma=1 , and also a subset of the one-sided sub-Gaussian class 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . However, Z 2 Z^{2} is not an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , because those could potentially have very heavy left tails, meaning that their variance is not necessarily bounded. We note in passing that both the above classes of e-variables are admissible, but the rather technical proof is omitted.

### 14. Constrained hypotheses

Many of the above examples of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , and many others we encounter in this book, can be called “constrained hypotheses”, meaning that they are defined via integral constraints. For such classes, we can sometimes cleanly characterize the set of all e-variables (and all admissible ones) in terms of those constraints. Let ℒ \mathcal{L} denote the set of all real-valued measurable functions on the underlying probability space. A constraint set is any nonempty set of functions Ψ ⊆ ℒ \Psi\subseteq\mathcal{L} . Define the null hypothesis generated by Ψ \Psi as We say that a property holds 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely if it holds everywhere outside of a set A A such that sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ℙ ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}(A)=0 . We start with the case where Ψ = { g 1 , … , g K } \Psi=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{K}\} is finite, as in the case of testing a mean, or mean and variance. One can show that the set of all e-variables 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is given by all [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] -valued measurable functions which are 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely dominated by 1 + ∑ k = 1 K π k ​ g k 1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}g_{k} for some π ∈ ℝ + K \pi\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K} . As a corollary, every admissible e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely equal to 1 + ∑ k = 1 K π k ​ g k 1+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}g_{k} for some π \pi in the set Clearly, the zero vector 𝟎 \mathbf{0} is in Π Ψ \Pi^{\Psi} . But for some choices of Ψ \Psi , Π Ψ \Pi^{\Psi} may be equal to { 𝟎 } \{\mathbf{0}\} , in which case all e-variables for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} are dominated by the constant 1 1 . In settings where Ψ \Psi is infinite, such as the sub-Gaussian example in the previous subsection, the corresponding statement is more complex. Define where cone ​ ( Ψ ) \text{cone}(\Psi) is the conic hull of Ψ \Psi . Then, the set of all e-variables 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is given by all [ 0 , ∞ ] [0,\infty] -valued measurable functions that are 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -quasi surely equal to 1 + f 1+f for some f ∈ 𝒞 ¯ f\in\overline{\mathcal{C}} , where 𝒞 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{C}} is the weak closure of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} (which is the closure with respect to a particular weak topology that is omitted here for brevity). The constraints in ( 2.14 ) are formulated via integrals of f ∈ Ψ f\in\Psi over ℙ \mathbb{P} . In some applications, the constraints are formulated via other functionals, such as risk measures, and some of them are studied in Chapter 16 .

<sub>§2.6.5 Constrained hypotheses</sub>

### 15. Testing likelihood ratio bounds

Let 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} be a general sample space. Let ℙ 0 \mathbb{P}_{0} be a fixed probability measure and γ ∈ [ 1 , ∞ ) \gamma\in[1,\infty) , and consider the null hypothesis against an alternative ℚ ≪ ℙ 0 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P}_{0} . To interpret, testing 𝒫 \mathcal{P} means to test whether the true data-generating distribution is close to a given benchmark distribution ℙ 0 \mathbb{P}_{0} in terms of likelihood ratio being bounded by γ \gamma . The case γ = 1 \gamma=1 corresponds to the simple null hypothesis { ℙ 0 } \{\mathbb{P}_{0}\} . Clearly, the likelihood ratio Z ∗ = d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ 0 Z^{*}=\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0} is not an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} unless γ = 1 \gamma=1 , but if we modify it to E = Z ∗ / γ E=Z^{*}/\gamma , then 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] ≤ γ ​ 𝔼 ℙ 0 ​ [ Z ∗ / γ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq\gamma\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[Z^{*}/\gamma]\leq 1 . Hence, E = Z ∗ / γ E=Z^{*}/\gamma is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . We can actually improve this e-variable, as described below. For any random variable Z Z , denote by q t ​ ( Z ) = inf { x ∈ ℝ : ℙ 0 ​ ( Z ≤ x ) ≥ 1 − t } q_{t}(Z)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{P}_{0}(Z\leq x)\geq 1-t\} for t ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) t\in(0,1) ; that is, t ↦ q 1 − t ​ ( Z ) t\mapsto q_{1-t}(Z) is the left quantile function of Z Z under ℙ 0 \mathbb{P}_{0} . Let E ∗ E^{*} be a transform of the likelihood ratio given by where z 0 ≥ 0 z_{0}\geq 0 is the largest constant such that Clearly E ∗ ≥ E E^{*}\geq E . Let us verify that E ∗ E^{*} is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . This follows by noting that where the first equality is a classic result on risk measures; see ( 16.6 ) in Chapter 16 . The optimality of the e-variable E ∗ E^{*} is proved in Section 6.7.6 .

### 16. Testing exchangeability

Let 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} be arbitrary, and suppose Z = ( Z 1 , … , Z n ) Z=(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}) . Denote by 𝒮 n \mathcal{S}_{n} the set of all permutations of the set [ n ] [n] . For any σ ∈ 𝒮 n \sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n} , let Z σ Z_{\sigma} denote the permuted vector ( Z σ ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , Z σ ⁡ ( n ) ) (Z_{\sigma(1)},\dots,Z_{\sigma(n)}) . Consider testing the null hypothesis of exchangeability where = d \mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\mathrm{d}}} denotes equality of distribution. Let ℚ \mathbb{Q} be an alternative distribution of Z Z with density q q , which is not exchangeable, i.e., not in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . Let π \pi be uniformly distributed on 𝒮 n \mathcal{S}_{n} independent of Z Z , and let By exchangeablity, 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ q ⁡ ( Z ) / q ⁡ ( Z π ) ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[q(Z)/q(Z_{\pi})]=1 for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , so E E is an exact e-variable, which is admissible by Corollary 2.15 . The e-variable E E has the form of the soft-rank e-value in Section 1.7 , where we use r = 0 r=0 and R b = L b = q ⁡ ( Z σ ) R_{b}=L_{b}=q(Z_{\sigma}) for σ ∈ 𝒮 n \sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n} . The optimality of the e-variable E E is proved in Section 6.7.7 .

### 17. Informal grids for realized e-values

In testing scientific hypotheses, thresholds for p-values are often chosen as 0.01 0.01 or 0.05 0.05 which correspond to type-I errors controlled at these levels. The e-to-p calibrator e ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 / e , 1 ) e\mapsto\min(1/e,1) implies that thresholds of 100 100 and 20 20 for e-values also have the above type-I error control. However, it is not recommended in practice to directly use these thresholds as the conversion e ↦ min ⁡ ( 1 / e , 1 ) e\mapsto\min(1/e,1) is typically wasteful. In Section 1.5 , and also in Section 4.1 later, we see that for a ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) a\in(0,1) , an e-value of 1 / a 1/a carries more information than a p-value of a a . In order to judge how significant results of testing using e-values are, the type-I error, based on which p-values are defined, may not be the desirable metric. Although there are no universally agreed thresholds to use for e-values, the rule of thumb of Jeffreys, originally designed for likelihood ratios, may offer some insight, as e-values are generalizations of likelihood ratios (for example, recall the result presented in Section 2.5.1 ). We summarize this rule of thumb in Table 2.19 . For instance, if e > 3.16 e>3.16 , we recommend to associate it with “substantial” (similar to p < 0.05 p<0.05 ), and if e > 10 e>10 , we recommend to associate it with “strong” (similar to p < 0.01 p<0.01 ). One should keep in mind that p-values and e-values do not have a one-to-one correspondence with each other, and the interpretation of the informal grid for e-values does not rely on converting into p-values. Because Jeffreys focused on likelihood ratios (or Bayes factors), the above rule of thumb applies well to e-values that are obtained “naturally”, such as likelihood ratios or their generalizations in the subsequent chapters. At the other extreme, for all-or-nothing e-variables (see Definition 3.5 ), such as E = 𝟙 { P ≤ α } / α E=\mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}}/\alpha for some p-variable P P , one should not refer to the grids in Table 2.19 , because for such an e-variable, E = 10 E=10 is equivalent to P ≤ 0.1 P\leq 0.1 . Finally, Section 6.6 draws deeper connections between e-values and Bayes factors, but it builds on some more advanced material. It is important to note that in general, p-values and e-values need not even “point in the same direction”. We can easily have situations where the p-value appears to provide strong evidence against the null, but the e-value supports the null, or vice versa. Let us elaborate with a simple example. Consider testing μ = 0 \mu=0 against μ = δ \mu=\delta (for some δ > 0 \delta>0 ) when we see data Z ∼ N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) Z\sim N(\mu,1) . Since the classic z z -test is uniformly most powerful in this setting, we would get the same p-value P = 1 − Φ ⁡ ( Z ) P=1-\Phi(Z) , regardless of the alternative mean δ \delta , while the (arguably natural) likelihood ratio e-value E = exp ⁡ ( δ ​ Z − δ 2 / 2 ) E=\exp(\delta Z-\delta^{2}/2) depends strongly on δ \delta . For instance, with δ = 5 \delta=5 and X = 2 X=2 , the p-value P ≈ 0.023 P\approx 0.023 (suggesting evidence against the null), but the e-value E ≈ 0.08 E\approx 0.08 (suggesting evidence for the null as a likelihood ratio). Thus the realized p-value and e-value could “point in different directions” depending on δ \delta and the realized data. This is another reason that we do not like to in general compare e-value and p-value based methods: while calibration can often preserve the direction (large/small e-values yield small/large p-values and vice versa), natural p-values and e-values that we may directly construct from the data need not have any natural concordance with each other, and so the resulting methods are not always directly comparable.

<sub>§2.7 Informal grids for realized e-values</sub>

### 18. Bibliographical note

This chapter largely resembles results and concepts in various important papers on e-values, but also it contains many results that are derived for the book to make a coherent piece. The example in Section 2.1 is based on Vovk and Wang (2023) . The concept of a calibrator in Section 2.3 dates back, at least, to Vovk (1993) ; the admissibility results are in Vovk and Wang (2021) . Bickel (2025) discussed p-to-e calibrators that depend on the sample size based on Bayes factors. Section 2.4 is based on Ignatiadis et al. (2024b) and Ramdas and Manole (2025) . The definition of admissibility in Section 2.5 is derived from Ramdas et al. (2020) , which studies it more generally in sequential settings. Some sufficient conditions in Corollary 2.15 also follow from the aforementioned paper. Among the examples in Section 2.6 , exponential families and monotone likelihood ratios were studied by Grünwald et al. (2024a) ; Grünwald et al. (2024b) . The testing symmetry example was first studied by Ramdas et al. (2020) , with follow-up work in Koning (2023b) ; Vovk and Wang (2024b) ; Larsson et al. (2025a) . The bounded mean example is a variant of one studied in Waudby-Smith and Ramdas (2024) , revisited by Larsson et al. (2025a) . The mean-and-variance example was studied by Fan et al. (2025) . Sub-Gaussian e-values were studied by Howard et al. (2020) ; Ramdas et al. (2020) ; Larsson et al. (2025a) . The example on constrained hypotheses was studied by Clerico (2024) ; Larsson et al. (2025b) ; our presentation is based on the latter. The exchangeability example was studied in Vovk (2021) and Ramdas et al. (2022) in a sequential context. In the batch context, the soft-rank e-value was proposed in the first arXiv preprint of Wang and Ramdas (2022) (but omitted in the journal version), and revisited by Koning (2023b) . The grids in Section 2.7 can be found in ( Jeffreys, 1961 , Appendix B) , with a variant proposed in Kass and Raftery (1995) .

<sub>§Bibliographical note</sub>

### 19. Efficiency: E-values under the alternative hypothesis

In Chapter 2 we focused only on the properties of the e-values under the null hypothesis. In this chapter, we describe its desired properties under the alternative hypothesis. This requires the notions of powered e-values, e-power, and log-optimality. The power of a test ϕ \phi against an alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} is simply 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi] . The type-II error (rate) of ϕ \phi equals one minus its power. When testing a null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , a test ϕ \phi is powered at level α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if and it is uniformly powered if further inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] > α \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]>\alpha . An e-variable E E is powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 for every ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and it is uniformly powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 . A closely related concept is that of level- α \alpha unbiased tests, i.e., tests ϕ \phi satisfying 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] ≤ α \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi]\leq\alpha for every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] ≥ α \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]\geq\alpha for every ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . For the purpose of this chapter, our definition of powered tests is needed, and it excludes trivial cases such as the constant test ϕ = α \phi=\alpha . Suppose that iid data X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\dots,X_{n} are sampled from N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) N(\mu,1) with unknown μ \mu . If we test H 0 : μ ≤ 0 H_{0}:\mu\leq 0 against H 1 : μ > 0 H_{1}:\mu>0 , then exp ⁡ ( X 1 + ⋯ + X n − n / 2 ) \exp(X_{1}+\dots+X_{n}-n/2) is a powered e-variable (but not uniformly e-powered). This e-variable is uniformly powered against H 1 : μ ≥ ε H_{1}:\mu\geq\varepsilon for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . If we test H 0 : μ < 0 H_{0}:\mu<0 against H 1 : μ ≥ 0 H_{1}:\mu\geq 0 , then there does not exist a powered e-variable (or test) because it is impossible to have power against the boundary point 0 0 . Example 3.3 and its proof demonstrate a more general phenomenon. When testing a parametric model (like the one in the example), if the null hypothesis is an open subset of the parameter space, the alternative is its complement, and densities exist and are continuous in the parameter, then powered tests and e-variables do not exist. An e-variable E E is called an all-or-nothing e-variable if E = 𝟙 A / c E=\mathds{1}_{A}/c for some event A A and c > 0 c>0 . For any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , there is a one-to-one correspondence between [ 0 , 1 / α CLOSE [0,1/\alpha ]-valued e-variables and level- α \alpha tests. Specifically, for any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \alpha\in(0,1] , we can use the relationship to move between a level- α \alpha test ϕ \phi and a corresponding e-variable E E . If ϕ \phi is binary, then E E is an all-or-nothing e-variable, which takes values in { 0 , 1 / α } \{0,1/\alpha\} . In particular, for any binary test ϕ \phi that rejects the null hypothesis when a realized p-value is smaller than or equal to α \alpha , ϕ \phi can be expressed in terms of the all-or-nothing e-variable 𝟙 { P ≤ α } / α \mathds{1}_{\{P\leq\alpha\}}/\alpha . Thus, there exists a powered level- α \alpha binary test if and only if there exists a powered e-variable taking values in { 0 , 1 / α } \{0,1/\alpha\} . We will expand this observation to include p-variables in Theorem 3.9 . For β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \beta\in(0,1] , a p-variable P P is powered on [ 0 , β ] [0,\beta] against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if for every ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , we have ℚ ⁡ ( P ≤ t ) ≥ t \mathbb{Q}(P\leq t)\geq t for all t ∈ [ 0 , β ] t\in[0,\beta] and strict inequality holds for some t ∈ [ 0 , β ] t\in[0,\beta] . We say P P is powered if the above holds for β = 1 \beta=1 ; this means that P P is strictly stochastically smaller than a uniform random variable on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] under each ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . To satisfy Assumption ( U ), it may be helpful to consider a lifted sample space Ω × [ 0 , 1 ] \Omega\times[0,1] from Ω \Omega and product probability measures ℙ × λ \mathbb{P}\times\lambda and ℚ × λ \mathbb{Q}\times\lambda from ℙ \mathbb{P} and ℚ \mathbb{Q} , where λ \lambda is the Lebesgue measure. Then, choose U U with U ⁡ ( ω 1 , ω 2 ) = ω 2 U(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\omega_{2} , and extend random variables X X in the original space to the mappings ( ω 1 , ω 2 ) ↦ X ⁡ ( ω 1 ) (\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mapsto X(\omega_{1}) , so that U U is independent of the other random variables that we consider. Let Ω = ℕ 0 \Omega=\mathbb{N}_{0} , ℙ = ( 1 / 2 ) ​ δ 0 + ( 1 / 2 ) ​ δ 1 \mathbb{P}=(1/2)\delta_{0}+(1/2)\delta_{1} where δ x \delta_{x} is the point mass at x x , and 𝒬 = { ℚ n : n ∈ ℕ } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{Q}_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\} , where ℚ n = ( 1 / n ) ​ δ n + ( 1 − 1 / n ) ​ δ 0 \mathbb{Q}_{n}=(1/n)\delta_{n}+(1-1/n)\delta_{0} . Nevertheless, if we have an infinite iid sequence E 1 ′ , E 2 ′ , … E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2},\dots following the same distribution as E ′ E^{\prime} under ℚ \mathbb{Q} , then the product M n = ∏ i = 1 n E i ′ M_{n}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}E^{\prime}_{i} goes to ∞ \infty ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely as n → ∞ n\to\infty , due to the law of large numbers applied to ( log ⁡ M n ) n ≥ 1 (\log M_{n})_{n\geq 1} , and this is consistent with Kelly’s criterion. A similar example in the sequential setting will be discussed in Section 7.11 . For testing 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} with a common reference measure, the following are equivalent: When comparing two e-variables E 1 E_{1} and E 2 E_{2} for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} , E 1 E_{1} is said to have greater (resp. equal) e-power than E 2 E_{2} if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E 2 / E 1 ) ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E_{2}/E_{1})]\leq 0 (resp. = 0 =0 ). Moreover, E E is log-optimal if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ / E ) ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime}/E)]\leq 0 for any other e-variable E ′ E^{\prime} . For testing any 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , a log-optimal e-variable against ℚ \mathbb{Q} always exists and is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely unique. For testing ℙ \mathbb{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} with ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , the ( ℙ \mathbb{P} -almost surely) unique log-optimal e-variable is the likelihood ratio d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P} . Thus, the optimal e-power equals KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) \mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}) , which could be infinite. Continue the setting of testing ℙ = N ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) \mathbb{P}=\mathrm{N}(0,1) against ℚ = N ⁡ ( μ , 1 ) \mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{N}(\mu,1) with n n observations in Example 3.13 . Simple algebra yields that θ = μ \theta=\mu gives the largest e-power, confirming the implication of Theorem 3.20 . Let 𝒢 \mathcal{G} be a sub- σ \sigma -algebra of ℱ \mathcal{F} . Let P P be an exact p-variable testing ℙ \mathbb{P} against ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} . Assume that 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ | P ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}~|~P] is decreasing in P P . Then the log-optimal calibrator exists and it is given (almost everywhere uniquely) by Consider the standard t-testing situation with 𝒫 = { N ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ) : σ > 0 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathrm{N}(0,\sigma^{2}):\sigma>0\} and 𝒬 = { N ( μ , σ 2 ) : σ > 0 , μ ≠ 0 } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathrm{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}):\sigma>0,~\mu\neq 0\} (elements of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} are the marginal distributions). Suppose we observe n n iid observations X 1 , … , X n X_{1},\dots,X_{n} from some ℙ ∈ 𝒫 ∪ 𝒬 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{Q} . Let S n = ∑ i = 1 n X i S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i} and V n = ∑ i = 1 n X i 2 V_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2} . Then for any c > 0 c>0 , is an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} that satisfies ( 3.8 ) for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . Note that this e-variable is scale-invariant, meaning that rescaling the data by a constant does not change the e-variable, and when n = 1 n=1 , it equals 1 and has zero e-power. While the following observation is not about the method of mixtures, it feels appropriate to point out here the surprising fact that there do exist non-constant e-variables that have positive e-power against some alternatives, even when n = 1 n=1 . For example, for any a ∈ ℝ a\in\mathbb{R} , is one such e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} ; note however that it is not scale-invariant. To see that E 1 E_{1} is an e-variable, note that for all σ \sigma , the inequality | x | ≤ σ ​ exp ⁡ ( x 2 / ( 2 ​ σ 2 ) − 1 / 2 ) |x|\leq\sigma\exp(x^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})-1/2) holds. Thus, we see that under any ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , which equals N ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ) \mathrm{N}(0,\sigma^{2}) for some unknown σ \sigma , we have where the final inequality holds by recognizing that we are evaluating the expectation of a likelihood ratio of N ⁡ ( a , 1 ) \mathrm{N}(a,1) to ℙ \mathbb{P} . For the distribution ℚ = N ⁡ ( a , 1 ) \mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{N}(a,1) , we can see 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E 1 ] = 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ | Z + a | ​ exp ⁡ ( 1 − Z 2 / 2 ) ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E_{1}]=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[|Z+a|\exp(1-Z^{2}/2)] , where Z Z follows a standard normal distribution under ℚ \mathbb{Q} , and hence 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E 1 ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E_{1}]>1 when a a is large enough. The alternative hypothesis equals 𝒬 = { ℙ n − k ​ ℚ k } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{P}^{n-k}\mathbb{Q}^{k}\} for some unknown k ∈ [ n ] k\in[n] and known ℚ \mathbb{Q} , meaning that X 1 , … , X n − k X_{1},\dots,X_{n-k} are iid from ℙ \mathbb{P} , and the rest are iid from ℚ \mathbb{Q} . It is natural to consider a mixture over the unknown k k , in particular forming the e-value based on uniform-mixture likelihood ratios. Given an atomless probability measure ℚ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , a mapping Λ ℚ : 𝒳 → ℝ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} satisfies Axioms 3.8 and 3.8 if and only if it can be represented by for some strictly increasing function f f .

<sub>§Chapter 3 Efficiency: E-values under the alternative hypothesis</sub>

### 20. Powered tests, e-values, and p-values

We first introduce the concept of power for tests and e-values. The power of a binary test ϕ \phi against ℚ \mathbb{Q} is defined as the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis under the alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} . This concept can be naturally generalized to non-binary tests as we present below. When we mention a powered test ϕ \phi without specifying the level, it should be understood as α = sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] \alpha=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi] . Tests that are not powered can still be practically useful. For instance, the existence of powered tests and e-variables requires that the alternative 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} does not contain a “boundary point” of the null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , as shown by Example 3.3 below. Nevertheless, although powered tests do not exist for testing H 0 : μ < 0 H_{0}:\mu<0 against H 1 : μ ≥ 0 H_{1}:\mu\geq 0 in that example, any powered test for testing H 0 H_{0} against H 1 ′ : μ > 0 H^{\prime}_{1}:\mu>0 can still be useful to test H 0 H_{0} against H 1 H_{1} in practical applications. Next, we discuss the natural connection between e-values and tests. Given any level- α \alpha binary test, one can always reproduce its decision by combining an “all-or-nothing e-value” with Markov’s inequality, as we explain (and generalize) below. All-or-nothing e-variables take on only two values: zero or 1 / c 1/c for some c c . Further, it is clear that A A must satisfy ℙ ⁡ ( A ) ≤ c \mathbb{P}(A)\leq c for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . For such e-variables, Markov’s inequality at level c c holds with equality, so there is no loss in translating between e-values, tests, and p-values. As a consequence of Fact 3.6 , all binary tests based on p-values — routinely used in the sciences — can be replicated using e-values. A non-binary test ϕ \phi is usually provided with the interpretation that it outputs a rejection with probability ϕ \phi , realized as a number in [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . In other words, one can convert a non-binary test ϕ \phi to a binary test ϕ ′ \phi^{\prime} as ϕ ′ = 𝟙 { U ≤ ϕ } \phi^{\prime}=\mathds{1}_{\{U\leq\phi\}} , where U U is an independent uniform random variable on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . This is closely related to the randomized Markov’s inequality discussed in Section 2.4 . An e-variable E E taking values in [ 0 , 1 / α ] [0,1/\alpha] is associated with a level- α \alpha test ϕ = α ​ E \phi=\alpha E ; in fact, it also yields a level- β \beta test for any β < α \beta<\alpha . A single e-variable yields a family of level- α \alpha tests (indexed by α \alpha ), but in the other direction, every level- α \alpha test yields a different e-variable. The above discussion highlights an important conceptual point — e-values suffice for testing: If a problem is testable (i.e., a powered test exists), then it is testable with e-values. This also applies to a notion of powered p-variables, although it is conceptually slightly more complicated, as we define below. For practical purposes, it is more than sufficient to consider β = 0.25 \beta=0.25 , for example, as rejections via p-values are always done at small levels. In the next section, we will connect powered tests, powered e-variables, and powered p-variables.

<sub>§3.1 Powered tests, e-values, and p-values</sub>

### 21. E-power and powered e-values

Given this high-level equivalence between tests and bounded e-values discussed in Fact 3.6 , one may be tempted to ask: What is new here? Are we just simply working with tests in disguise? The answer is this: While all-or-nothing e-values are interesting conceptually in order to relate them to tests and p-values, these are not typically how one would construct e-values in practice. Recall the example from Section 1.6 on likelihood ratios. They will commonly have support on the entire positive real line (not just on two points), and never take on the value zero if ℙ \mathbb{P} and ℚ \mathbb{Q} are absolutely continuous with respect to each other. To explain this, the following concept of e-power is useful. The e-power of an e-variable could equal ∞ \infty or − ∞ -\infty (these still count as being well-defined). Indeed, whenever we make statements involving expected logarithms, we require them to be well-defined. The e-power (higher is better) of all-or-nothing e-variables is − ∞ -\infty . The concept of e-power is closely related to the Kelly criterion in betting and investment. The logarithm reflects the fact that in many applications, e-values are multiplied together. For a simplest example, consider a sequence of iid e-variables E 1 , E 2 , … E_{1},E_{2},\dots with finite e-power and the process M = ( M t ) t ∈ ℕ M=(M_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}} defined by The e-power of E 1 E_{1} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} equals the asymptotic growth rate of M M , that is, the ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost sure limit of ( log ⁡ M t ) / t (\log M_{t})/t as t → ∞ t\to\infty (see Definition 7.4 ), justified by the strong law of large numbers. The asymptotic growth rate will be important in the methodology of testing by betting, formally treated in Chapter 7 . When converting M t M_{t} into the level- α \alpha test 𝟙 { M t ≥ 1 / α } \mathds{1}_{\{M_{t}\geq 1/\alpha\}} for α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) , the role of e-power is clear from the following proposition. The proof of Proposition 3.12 is a simple application of the strong law of large numbers. Note that for each t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , E 1 E_{1} has positive e-power against ℚ \mathbb{Q} is and only if M t M_{t} has positive e-power against ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Proposition 3.12 implies that in the simple iid setting, a positive e-power leads to asymptotic power- 1 1 tests, and a negative e-power leads to asymptotic power- 0 0 tests. An axiomatic justification of e-power is presented in Section 3.8 , which uses an axiom based on an idea that is similar to Proposition 3.12 . The condition 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] > 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]>0 in Definition 3.11 is equivalent to saying that the geometric mean of X X is larger than one: exp ⁡ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] > 1 \exp\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]>1 , which is a stronger constraint than requiring the (arithmetic) mean of E E to be larger than 1, that is, 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 in Definition 3.1 (this can be seen from Jensen’s inequality). Hence, if E E has positive e-power, then E E is a powered e-variable, but the reverse implication is not true in general. These two conditions are closely related. Indeed, for a simple alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , from any powered e-variable one can easily find an e-variable with positive e-power, through the transform E ↦ 1 − λ + λ ​ E E\mapsto 1-\lambda+\lambda E for some small λ > 0 \lambda>0 that depends on E E and ℚ \mathbb{Q} . This is formalized in the following result. The forward direction in ( 3.3 ) fails if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is infinite and X X is unbounded. Below, we give an example of an unbounded e-variable E E satisfying inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] > 1 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]>1 (that is, uniformly powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ), but for all λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] . \lambda\in(0,1]. Moreover, this example also illustrates that a bounded powered e-variable may not exist even if a uniformly powered e-variable exists. Let E E be given by E ⁡ ( n ) = 2 ​ n E(n)=2n for n ∈ ℕ 0 n\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . This gives 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] = 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]=1 and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E ] = 2 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E]=2 for all ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . Thus, E E is a uniformly powered e-variable, and it is unbounded. For any λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in(0,1] , we have Since ( 1 − λ ) n − 1 ​ ( 1 + ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ λ ) < 1 (1-\lambda)^{n-1}(1+(2n-1)\lambda)<1 for n n large enough, we know that inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) ] < 0 \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(1-\lambda+\lambda E)]<0 for all λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in(0,1] . There does not exist any bounded e-variable that is powered against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . To see this, for an e-variable E E to be powered against ℚ 1 \mathbb{Q}_{1} , we have E ⁡ ( 1 ) > 1 E(1)>1 . For E E to be powered against ℚ n \mathbb{Q}_{n} , we need ( 1 / n ) ​ E ​ ( n ) + ( 1 − 1 / n ) ​ E ​ ( 0 ) > 1 (1/n)E(n)+(1-1/n)E(0)>1 for all n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} . Since E E is bounded, this means E ⁡ ( 0 ) ≥ 1 E(0)\geq 1 . Together with E ⁡ ( 1 ) > 1 E(1)>1 , we have 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] > 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]>1 , conflicting the fact that E E is an e-variable for ℙ \mathbb{P} . As hinted above, the e-power 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] may not always be well-defined, due to the unbounded nature of the logarithm function. Moreover, there are some special (perhaps pathological) examples in which the e-power, although well-defined, may be infinite, and does not have a useful interpretation. We give a stylized, unrealistic, example here.

<sub>§3.3 E-power and powered e-values</sub>

### 22. Conditions for the existence of powered e-variables

In this section, we address the existence of powered e-variables and p-variables by means of two characterization results. These results connect the existence of p-variables and e-variables to the structure of the null and alternative hypotheses, different from Theorem 3.9 , which does not discuss how to check the existence. In these results, let L L and M M be two positive integers and we continue to assume ( U ) in Section 3.2 . The following result uses the total variation distance between sets of distributions, which we now define. For any two distributions ℙ , ℚ \mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q} , define where the supremum is taken over all measurable sets A A . Then, given two sets of distributions 𝒫 , 𝒬 \mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q} , we can define The following result is closely connected to a classic theorem of Kraft (1955) , proved by Le Cam in private communication. The Kraft–Le Cam theorem states that for general 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} (not necessarily finite) that have a common reference measure, the following holds: for any ε ≥ 0 \varepsilon\geq 0 , there exists a test ϕ \phi such that inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] − sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] > ε \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]-\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi]>\varepsilon if and only if the total variation distance between the closed convex hulls of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is greater than 2 ​ ε 2\varepsilon . there exists a test ϕ \phi such that inf ℚ ∈ 𝒬 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ϕ ] > sup ℙ ∈ 𝒫 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ ϕ ] \inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\phi]>\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\phi] ; there exists a uniformly powered bounded e-variable E E for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ; d TV ​ ( Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) , Conv ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) ) > 0 d_{\text{TV}}(\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}),\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{Q}))>0 . If there is no common reference measure, then condition (iii) is not equivalent to the earlier ones. In fact, even when 𝒬 = { ℚ } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{Q}\} is simple, d TV ​ ( Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) , ℚ ) > 0 d_{\text{TV}}(\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}),\mathbb{Q})>0 does not imply the existence of a powered e-variable. The appropriate condition actually involves an object called the bipolar of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , and is formally presented in Theorem 6.10 . Separately, note that when 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} are finite, condition (iii) above reduces to Conv ⁡ ( ℚ 1 , … , ℚ M ) ∩ Conv ⁡ ( ℙ 1 , … , ℙ L ) = ∅ \mathrm{Conv}(\mathbb{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{Q}_{M})\cap\mathrm{Conv}(\mathbb{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{P}_{L})=\varnothing . The existence of exact p-variables and e-variables is technically more advanced, and studied in Section 14.3 , where we obtain a similar result (Theorem 14.6 ) to Theorem 3.17 .

<sub>§3.4 Conditions for the existence of powered e-variables</sub>

### 23. Log-optimality and likelihood ratios

Next, we turn to the key concept of log-optimality for a simple alternative { ℚ } \{\mathbb{Q}\} , which is defined essentially via the comparison of e-power. Definitions 3.11 and 3.18 are obviously connected. If the maximum e-power, defined as is finite, then the log-optimal e-variable maximizes the e-power, and the definition of log-optimality reduces to saying 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ′ ] ≤ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E^{\prime}]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] . The current definition allows the maximum e-power to be infinite, which could occur even in simple cases such as testing a Gaussian against a Cauchy because the tails of the Cauchy are much heavier than that of the Gaussian; see also Example 3.16 . Note that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ / E ) ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime}/E)]\leq 0 and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ) ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime})]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E)] are equivalent when E E and E ′ E^{\prime} have finite e-power, but the two conditions are not equivalent in general. We do not prove the first part in this book, since the proof is quite technical, and defer the second part to Chapter 6 . However, in the case of a simple null hypothesis ℙ \mathbb{P} with ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , existence and uniqueness of the log-optimal e-variable can be easily argued, as we will see in Theorem 3.20 . In this section, we present an important result on the log-optimal e-values for a simple null hypothesis 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} and a simple alternative 𝒬 = { ℚ } \mathcal{Q}=\{\mathbb{Q}\} . We will assume that ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} . Let q q and p p represent the densities of ℚ \mathbb{Q} and ℙ \mathbb{P} with respect to some reference measure 𝕃 \mathbb{L} , by which we can write ( d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ ) ​ ( x ) = q ⁡ ( x ) / p ⁡ ( x ) ({\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}/{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}})(x)={q(x)}/{p(x)} . The classic Neyman–Pearson lemma for hypothesis testing states that when testing a simple null ℙ \mathbb{P} against a simple alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the most powerful level- α \alpha test involves thresholding the likelihood ratio. We now present a result that can be seen as the e-analog of the Neyman–Pearson lemma. Importantly, we are not interested in designing tests (which can be obtained via Markov’s inequality as discussed in Chapter 2 ), but simply in designing e-variable that is log-optimal (as e-powerful as possible). To prepare for the statement, define the Kullback–Leibler divergence between ℚ \mathbb{Q} and ℙ \mathbb{P} as where it is defined as ∞ \infty if ℚ \mathbb{Q} is not absolutely continuous with respect to ℙ \mathbb{P} . Gibbs’ inequality, or the Shannon–Kolmogorov information inequality, states that the Kullback–Leibler divergence between any two distributions is nonnegative: KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) ≥ 0 \mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\geq 0 . Further, equality holds if and only if ℙ = ℚ \mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q} . In Chapter 6 , we present a more general result encompassing Theorem 3.20 , which even relaxes the assumption ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} .

<sub>§3.5 Log-optimality and likelihood ratios</sub>

### 24. Conditional log-optimality and log-optimal calibrators

In statistical applications, e-variables and p-variables are constructed based on available data, and thus they belong to a σ \sigma -algebra 𝒢 ⊆ ℱ \mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F} generated by the data. With this restriction, we need to consider the log-optimal e-variable with respect to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , that is, an e-variable E E measurable with respect to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} that satisfies 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ ( E ′ / E ) ] ≤ 0 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log(E^{\prime}/E)]\leq 0 for all e-variables E ′ E^{\prime} that are measurable with respect to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . In what follows, for ℙ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , ℙ | 𝒢 \mathbb{P}|_{\mathcal{G}} is the restriction of ℙ \mathbb{P} to 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , that is, ℙ | 𝒢 ​ ( A ) = ℙ ​ ( A ) \mathbb{P}|_{\mathcal{G}}(A)=\mathbb{P}(A) for A ∈ 𝒢 A\in\mathcal{G} . A direct consequence of Proposition 3.22 is the log-optimal calibrator. Suppose that we know a p-variable P P is uniformly distributed on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] under ℙ \mathbb{P} ; thus it is exact. For a given simple alternative ℚ \mathbb{Q} , we can define the log-optimal calibrator , that is, a calibrator f f such that for any other calibrator g g . In other words, f ⁡ ( P ) f(P) is the log-optimal e-variable among all possible e-variables that are calibrated from P P .

<sub>§3.6 Conditional log-optimality and log-optimal calibrators</sub>

### 25. Asymptotic log-optimality: methods of mixtures and plug-in

Consider now the case of testing a simple null hypothesis ℙ \mathbb{P} against a composite alternative 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . It is easiest to think about the case where all the distributions involved are iid product probability measures, in which case, with only a slight overload of notation, we can let ℙ \mathbb{P} and ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} refer to the distributions of each individual observation, and let these be associated with densities p p and q q . Let the data be represented by a vector X n X^{n} . A reasonable objective is that of asymptotic log-optimality . Here, the goal is to define a sequence of e-variables E n = E ⁡ ( X n ) E_{n}=E(X^{n}) such that for any ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , The right-hand side of the above expression is the e-power of an oracle that knows which alternative distribution is generating the data. If the above limit holds, our e-variable is able to asymptotically achieve the same e-power without this knowledge. There are two broad classes of methods that do achieve these goals under weak assumptions (that we do not expand on here): the mixture and the plug-in methods.

<sub>§3.7 Asymptotic log-optimality: methods of mixtures and plug-in</sub>

### 26. Mixture method.

Pick a mixture distribution ν \nu over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , and define the e-variable Note the order of integral and product: we are calculating the mixture over likelihood ratios. The key limitation to applicability of this method is that the mixture integral should be analytically or computationally easy to evaluate.

<sub>§Mixture method.</sub>

### 27. Plug-in method.

Let ℚ i − 1 ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}_{i-1}\in\mathcal{Q} be a distribution of the i i -th observation, picked based on the first i − 1 i-1 observations, and let q i − 1 q_{i-1} be its density. Define It is not hard to check that E n E_{n} is an e-variable. It is possible to prove the asymptotic log-optimality ( 3.8 ) of the above methods under some rather weak assumptions, but we omit these details. We only make some informal remarks here, which are still useful for the practitioner. For asymptotic log-optimality of the mixture method, it is necessary that ν \nu has full support over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ; for parametric problems with finite dimensional alternatives, this condition is often sufficient as well. For the plug-in method, a sensible choice of ℚ i − 1 \mathbb{Q}_{i-1} is the posterior mean of ℚ ∈ 𝒬 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} after seeing X i − 1 X^{i-1} , when starting with prior ν \nu . If the mixture method with ν \nu is asymptotically log-optimal, then one would typically expect the aforementioned plug-in method to also have the same property. The mixture method is also useful for composite nulls. Here we give an explicit example without derivation. Next, we demonstrate a setting with non-iid data where the mixture method is helpful and natural. In fact, the above example underlies the much more general, nonparametric, sequential change detection methodology of e-detectors , which is out of the scope of this book.

<sub>§Plug-in method.</sub>

### 28. Axiomatic justification of the e-power

In this section we justify the definition of e-power in Definition 3.11 through an axiomatic approach. Let 𝒳 \mathcal{X} be the set of all nonnegative random variables that are bounded from above and away from 0 0 . We will restrict our consideration of e-power on 𝒳 \mathcal{X} to avoid issues with infinity, and it can be naturally extended to the set of all nonnegative random variables. As standard in the axiomatic approach in decision theory, axioms become weaker when they are formulated on a smaller set of objects, making the axiomatic characterization results stronger. Therefore, our restriction to 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is harmless. Let ℳ 1 \mathcal{M}_{1} be the set of all probability measures on ( Ω , ℱ ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) . We will fix the alternative hypothesis ℚ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , which is an atomless probability measure. The requirement of ℚ \mathbb{Q} being atomless is only used to justify the existence of a non-degenerate iid sequence. In general, e-variables for the null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} may be arbitrarily distributed under the alternative hypothesis ℚ \mathbb{Q} , and the e-power is a concept concerning the performance of an e-variable under the alternative hypothesis. Therefore, the choice of the null hypothesis 𝒫 \mathcal{P} is irrelevant for defining e-power. For this reason, without loss of generality, we directly consider the candidates for the e-power as mappings Λ ℚ : 𝒳 → ℝ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R} , without specifying that e-variables need to satisfy 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq 1 for ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . For Λ ℚ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} to represent the e-power under ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the natural interpretation of Λ ℚ ​ ( E 1 ) > Λ ℚ ​ ( E 2 ) \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E_{1})>\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E_{2}) is that E 1 E_{1} is considered as more powerful than E 2 E_{2} . We keep this important interpretation in mind in the following discussions. Suppose that a statistician has designed a two-stage experiment, and observes an e-value of e 0 e_{0} at the first stage. She needs to choose between two e-variables E 1 E_{1} and E 2 E_{2} at second stage. If E 1 E_{1} is more powerful than E 2 E_{2} , then she should choose E 1 E_{1} over E 2 E_{2} . However, the effective e-variables to compare in this procedure are e 0 ​ E 1 e_{0}E_{1} and e 0 ​ E 2 e_{0}E_{2} . Hence, there should be a consistency between the pair ( E 1 , E 2 ) (E_{1},E_{2}) and the pair ( e 0 ​ E 1 , e 0 ​ E 2 ) (e_{0}E_{1},e_{0}E_{2}) , which is formalized by the following axiom. The second axiom concerns the design of e-variables in an asymptotic sense. It states that, to choose between two configurations of iid e-variables, if the product e-process of less powerful configuration has asymptotic power 1 1 , then that from the more powerful configuration should also have asymptotic power 1 1 . This axiom reflects the consideration of the Kelly criterion, which we discussed in Section 3.3 . It turns out that Axioms 3.8 and 3.8 are sufficient to jointly characterize the e-power, which has the form 𝔼 Q ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{Q}[\log E] as we saw in Definition 3.11 . Theorem 3.28 implies that if Axioms 3.8 and 3.8 are reasonable for a notion of e-power, then the only possible way to quantify the e-power is to use a strictly increasing transform of 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] , which generates the same ordinal structure as using 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] directly. It also shows what the e-power essentially guarantees: It is the most suitable when considering the product e-value with asymptotic consistency (also discussed in Section 3.3 ). A simple example of a strictly increasing transform of the e-power is the geometric mean of the e-variable E E , given by exp ⁡ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \exp\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] . It is nonnegative, and it is larger than 1 1 if the e-power is positive. In the above result, the e-power 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] in Definition 3.11 is justified as a comparative notion. This serves a very similar purpose to the classic notion of power of tests (Definition 3.1 ) when we compare e-variables to determine which ones are more “powerful”. When it comes to the meaning of the actual value, its natural interpretation is the asymptotic growth rate in a multiplicative setting, which is different from the classic notion of power, whose interpretation is the probability of rejection under the alternative. We can easily extend the mapping Λ ℚ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} to the set of all nonnegative random variables, provided that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] is well-defined, that is, at least one of 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ( log ⁡ E ) + ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(\log E)_{+}] and 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ ( log ⁡ E ) − ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[(\log E)_{-}] is finite. for any nonnegative random variable X X . We treat Λ ℚ ​ ( X ) \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(X) as undefined if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ X ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log X] is not well-defined. The next proposition gives some convenient properties of Λ ℚ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}} . If Λ ℚ ​ ( E ) > 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E)>0 , then Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) > 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E)>0 for all λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in(0,1] . If Λ ℚ ​ ( E ) ≥ 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E)\geq 0 , then Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) ≥ 0 \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E)\geq 0 for all λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in[0,1] . Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E) is always well-defined for λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \lambda\in[0,1) . If Λ ℚ ​ ( E ) < ∞ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(E)<\infty , then Λ ℚ ​ ( 1 − λ + λ ​ E ) ∈ ℝ \Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}(1-\lambda+\lambda E)\in\mathbb{R} for all λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \lambda\in[0,1) .

<sub>§3.8 Axiomatic justification of the e-power</sub>

### 29. Bibliographical note

The connection between tests and bounded e-variables described in Fact 3.6 was discussed in Koning (2024) in detail. The e-power in Section 3.3 is formally defined in Vovk and Wang (2024b) . Example 3.16 was discussed in Wang (2024a) . The notion of log-optimality was proposed by Kelly (1956) in the context of betting and investment. Larsson et al. (2025a) showed the existence and uniqueness of a log-optimal e-variable with no conditions on 𝒫 , ℚ \mathcal{P},\mathbb{Q} . The result in Section 3.5 essentially dates back to Breiman (1961) , though its presentation here mirrors that in Shafer (2021) . The mixture and plug-in methods to handle composite alternatives, as presented in Section 3.7 , are very well known; in fact, both ideas were already mentioned by Wald (1947) . Later, Robbins and Siegmund (1974) derived some strong connections between these methods. The t-test e-variable is derived and studied in, for example, Lai (1976) , Pérez-Ortiz et al. (2024) , and Wang and Ramdas (2025) , with the latter paper having the e-variable for n = 1 n=1 . The asymptotic optimality of the method of mixtures is strongly related to information-theoretic topics such as redundancy and description length, and many more such optimality results can be inferred using a plethora of results on the latter topic in Grünwald (2007) . The changepoint e-value in Example 3.25 is borrowed from Shin et al. (2024) .

<sub>§Bibliographical note</sub>

### 30. The log-optimal e-value and reverse information projection

While the universal inference e-variable (or e-process) exists in quite some generality and is relatively straightforward to construct, it is only known to be asymptotically log-optimal but does not have a finite-sample log-optimality guarantee. It turns out that in even more generality, a log-optimal e-variable exists and is unique. In fact, it always dominates universal inference, rendering the latter method inadmissible. The catch, of course, is that it may not be numerically as easy to construct, but we will be able to characterize it analytically in many cases. This chapter is dedicated to describing a duality theory that is central to designing log-optimal e-variables. The two main characters in this duality are a special e-variable, called the numeraire, and a special (sub-probability) distribution called the reverse information projection (RIPr). Our most general results make no assumptions on ℚ \mathbb{Q} and 𝒫 \mathcal{P} whatsoever, not even the existence of a common reference measure or the minimum KL divergence between ℚ \mathbb{Q} and 𝒫 \mathcal{P} being finite. However, some of these results have sophisticated proofs that are omitted. We will instead prove some of the results in simpler special cases, making some simplifying assumptions. When we use the term e-variable below, we always mean e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . We let 𝔈 \mathfrak{E} denote the set of e-variables: A numeraire e-variable (or just numeraire for short) is a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive e-variable E ∗ E^{*} such that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E / E ∗ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E/E^{*}]\leq 1 for every e-variable E ∈ 𝔈 E\in\mathfrak{E} . Let E ∗ E^{*} denote the numeraire for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , and consider a larger null hypothesis 𝒫 ′ ⊇ 𝒫 \mathcal{P}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{P} . If E ∗ E^{*} is still an e-variable for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , then it is also the numeraire for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} . Let E ∗ E^{*} be a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive e-variable. Then E ∗ E^{*} is a numeraire if and only if it is log-optimal. In particular, a log-optimal e-variable is unique up to ℚ \mathbb{Q} -nullsets. A numeraire always exists. Moreover, the following conditions are equivalent: For all e-variables E ∈ 𝔈 E\in\mathfrak{E} and distributions ℙ ∈ Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) \mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P}) , we have 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] ≤ KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E]\leq\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}) (where if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E] is undefined, we treat it as − ∞ -\infty ), and thus Suppose 𝒫 = { ℙ 1 , … , ℙ K } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{P}_{K}\} with ℙ k ≪ ℚ \mathbb{P}_{k}\ll\mathbb{Q} for all k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] , and min ℙ ∈ Conv ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) ⁡ KL ⁡ ( ℚ , ℙ ) < ∞ \min_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathrm{Conv}(\mathcal{P})}\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})<\infty . Then, where E ∗ E^{*} is the numeraire and ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} achieves the minimum. Let E ∗ E^{*} be the numeraire for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} against ℚ \mathbb{Q} . The reverse information projection of ℚ \mathbb{Q} onto 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , denoted ℙ ∗ \mathbb{P}^{*} , is defined via where the right-hand side is understood to equal zero on { E ∗ = ∞ } \{E^{*}=\infty\} . Suppose the null 𝒫 \mathcal{P} consists of all unit variance Gaussians with mean less than 0 0 . Then the standard Gaussian is not in the convex hull of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , but it is in 𝒫 ∘ ⁣ ∘ ∩ ℳ 1 \mathcal{P}^{\circ\circ}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1} , which contains all unit variance Gaussians with nonnegative mean (and their mixtures). <sub>§Chapter 6 The log-optimal e-value and reverse information projection</sub>

### 31. The numeraire e-variable

We first give the definition of the numeraire e-variable, which is closely connected to the log-optimal e-variable in Section 3.5 . Numeraires are unique up to ℚ \mathbb{Q} -nullsets. Indeed, if E 1 ∗ E^{*}_{1} and E 2 ∗ E^{*}_{2} are numeraires, then the ratio Y = E 2 ∗ / E 1 ∗ Y=E^{*}_{2}/E^{*}_{1} satisfies 1 ≤ 1 / 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ Y ] ≤ 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ 1 / Y ] ≤ 1 1\leq 1/\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[Y]\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[1/Y]\leq 1 thanks to the numeraire property of E 1 ∗ E^{*}_{1} , Jensen’s inequality, and the numeraire property of E 2 ∗ E^{*}_{2} . Thus Jensen’s inequality holds with equality, so Y Y is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely equal to a constant which must be one. It follows that E 1 ∗ E^{*}_{1} and E 2 ∗ E^{*}_{2} are ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely equal. In view of this uniqueness, we often speak of ‘the’ numeraire. In the case of a simple null 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} with ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , the numeraire is just the likelihood ratio E ∗ = d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ E^{*}=\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P} . Indeed, E ∗ E^{*} is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely strictly positive, it is an e-variable, and for any other e-variable E ∈ 𝔈 E\in\mathfrak{E} we have by a simple change of measure that 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E / E ∗ ] = 𝔼 ℙ ​ [ E ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E/E^{*}]=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[E]\leq 1 . Even for composite nulls, the numeraire is a likelihood ratio of ℚ \mathbb{Q} to the ‘reverse information projection’. Before getting there, we present a few other simple results. The proof is simple. If E E is an e-variable for 𝒫 ′ \mathcal{P}^{\prime} , it is also an e-variable for 𝒫 \mathcal{P} and one has 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ E / E ∗ ] ≤ 1 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[E/E^{*}]\leq 1 by assumption, proving the lemma. A numeraire E ∗ E^{*} is also log-optimal (Section 3.5 ) in the sense that where the left-hand side may be − ∞ -\infty . This follows directly from Jensen’s inequality and the numeraire property. The converse is also true, and we record the equivalence in the following proposition. The proof shows that the numeraire property is really the first-order condition for log-optimality. Note also that a numeraire E ∗ E^{*} is the ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely unique log-optimal e-variable in the sense of ( 6.1 ) even if 𝔼 ℚ ​ [ log ⁡ E ∗ ] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[\log E^{*}] happens to be infinite. The following result says that a numeraire always exists without any assumptions whatsoever on 𝒫 \mathcal{P} or ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Although no assumptions on 𝒫 \mathcal{P} or ℚ \mathbb{Q} are needed in general, we do not present this more general theory for reasons of brevity. It turns out that the theory simplifies under the following condition, which, despite being nontrivial, is still very weak. We say that ℚ \mathbb{Q} is absolutely continuous with respect to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , written ℚ ≪ 𝒫 , \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P}, if ℚ \mathbb{Q} does not assign positive probability to any event that is null under every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Equivalently, This natural generalization of absolute continuity for pairs of measures is satisfied in most statistically relevant settings, while being significantly weaker than requiring a common dominating reference measure. It is also weaker than requiring that ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} for every ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} , which would require that ℚ ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \mathbb{Q}(A)=0 as long as any (as opposed to every) ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} assigns ℙ ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \mathbb{P}(A)=0 (or contrapositively, that ℚ ⁡ ( A ) > 0 \mathbb{Q}(A)>0 implies ℙ ⁡ ( A ) > 0 \mathbb{P}(A)>0 for all ℙ ∈ 𝒫 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P} ). The numeraire is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely finite. Every e-variable is ℚ \mathbb{Q} -almost surely finite. ℚ \mathbb{Q} is absolutely continuous with respect to 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , that is ℚ ≪ 𝒫 \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathcal{P} . We end with a few additional properties of the numeraire. Since the constant one is always an e-variable, the numeraire E ∗ E^{*} satisfies the aesthetically pleasing property that When 𝒫 = { ℙ } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}\} is simple and ℚ ≪ ℙ \mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P} , both inequalities above hold with equality, attained by the numeraire d ​ ℚ / d ​ ℙ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P} . Another pleasing property is obtained by Jensen’s inequality: The numeraire E ∗ E^{*} satisfies Finally, note that the definition of KL divergence in ( 3.4 ) can be rewritten and generalized to nonnegative measures ℙ \mathbb{P} in the following manner. Let ℳ + \mathcal{M}_{+} denote the set of all nonnegative measures on Ω \Omega . Then, for any ℙ ∈ ℳ + \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{+} and ℚ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , define where ℙ a \mathbb{P}^{a} is the absolutely continuous part of ℙ \mathbb{P} with respect to ℚ \mathbb{Q} . When ℙ ∈ ℳ 1 \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{1} , this definition coincides with ( 3.4 ). Note that both sides of ( 6.4 ) are nonnegative and well defined, since the constant 1 1 is always an e-variable.
