# Qwen2.5 Technical Report

arXiv:2412.15115 · 2024 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.15115)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
The sparks of artificial general intelligence (AGI) are increasingly visible through the fast development of large foundation models, notably large language models (LLMs) ( Brown et al., 2020 ; OpenAI, 2023 ; OpenAI, 2024a ; Gemini Team, 2024 ; Anthropic, 2023a ; Anthropic, 2023b ; Anthropic, 2024 ; Bai et al., 2023 ; Yang et al., 2024a ; Touvron et al., 2023a ; Touvron et al., 2023b ; Dubey et al., 2024 ) . The continuous advancement in model and data scaling, combined with the paradigm of large-scale pre-training followed by high-quality supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) ( Ouyang et al., 2022 ) , has enabled large language models (LLMs) to develop emergent capabilities in language understanding, generation, and reasoning. Building on this foundation, recent breakthroughs in inference time scaling, particularly demonstrated by o1 ( OpenAI, 2024b ) , have enhanced LLMs’ capacity for deep thinking through step-by-step reasoning and reflection. These developments have elevated the potential of language models, suggesting they may achieve significant breakthroughs in scientific exploration as they continue to demonstrate emergent capabilities indicative of more general artificial intelligence.

<sub>§1 Introduction</sub>

## Pipeline
```mermaid
flowchart LR
  N0["§2 Architecture & Tokenizer"]
```

![In the iterative development of the Qwen series, data scaling has played a crucial role. Qwen 2.5, which leverages 18 trillion tokens for pre-training, has demonstrated the most advanced capabilities within the Qwen series, especially in terms of domain expertise, underscoring the importance of scale together with mixture in enhancing the model’s capabilities.](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.15115/2412.15115v2/main.png)

*In the iterative development of the Qwen series, data scaling has played a crucial role. Qwen 2.5, which leverages 18 trillion tokens for pre-training, has demonstrated the most advanced capabilities within the Qwen series, especially in terms of domain expertise, underscoring the importance of scale together with mixture in enhancing the model’s capabilities.*

<sub>figure: `2412.15115v2/main.png`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. Architecture & Tokenizer

Basically, the Qwen2.5 series include dense models for opensource, namely Qwen2.5-0.5B / 1.5B / 3B / 7B / 14B / 32B / 72B, and MoE models for API service, namely Qwen2.5-Turbo and Qwen2.5-Plus. Below, we provide details about the architecture of models. For dense models, we maintain the Transformer-based decoder architecture ( Vaswani et al., 2017 ; Radford et al., 2018 ) as Qwen2 ( Yang et al., 2024a ) . The architecture incorporates several key components: Grouped Query Attention (GQA, Ainslie et al., 2023 ) for efficient KV cache utilization, SwiGLU activation function ( Dauphin et al., 2017 ) for non-linear activation, Rotary Positional Embeddings (RoPE, Su et al., 2024 ) for encoding position information, QKV bias ( Su, 2023 ) in the attention mechanism and RMSNorm ( Jiang et al., 2023b ) with pre-normalization to ensure stable training. Building upon the dense model architectures, we extend it to MoE model architectures. This is achieved by replacing standard feed-forward network (FFN) layers with specialized MoE layers, where each layer comprises multiple FFN experts and a routing mechanism that dispatches tokens to the top-K experts. Following the approaches demonstrated in Qwen1.5-MoE ( Yang et al., 2024a ) , we implement fine-grained expert segmentation ( Dai et al., 2024 ) and shared experts routing ( Rajbhandari et al., 2022 ; Dai et al., 2024 ) . These architectural innovations have yielded substantial improvements in model performance across downstream tasks. For tokenization, we utilize Qwen’s tokenizer ( Bai et al., 2023 ) , which implements byte-level byte-pair encoding (BBPE, Brown et al., 2020 ; Wang et al., 2020 ; Sennrich et al., 2016 ) with a vocabulary of 151,643 regular tokens. We have expanded the set of control tokens from 3 to 22 compared to previous Qwen versions, adding two new tokens for tool functionality and allocating the remainder for other model capabilities. This expansion establishes a unified vocabulary across all Qwen2.5 models, enhancing consistency and reducing potential compatibility issues.

<sub>§2 Architecture & Tokenizer</sub>

# BERTScore : Evaluating Text Generation with BERT

arXiv:1904.09675 · 2020 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/1904.09675)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
Automatic evaluation of natural language generation, for example in machine translation and caption generation, requires comparing candidate sentences to annotated references. The goal is to evaluate semantic equivalence. However, commonly used methods rely on surface-form similarity only. For example, Bleu ( Papineni et al., 2002 ) , the most common machine translation metric, simply counts n n -gram overlap between the candidate and the reference. While this provides a simple and general measure, it fails to account for meaning-preserving lexical and compositional diversity.

<sub>§1 Introduction</sub>

## Pipeline
```mermaid
flowchart LR
  N0["§4 Experimental Setup"]
  N1["§Contextual Embedding Models"]
  N2["§Machine Translation"]
  N3["§Image Captioning"]
  N0 --> N1
  N1 --> N2
  N2 --> N3
```

![Illustration of the computation of the recall metric R BERT R_{\textsc{BERT}}{} . Given the reference x x and candidate x ^ \hat{x}{} , we compute BERT embeddings and pairwise cosine similarity. We highlight the greedy matching in red, and include the optional idf {\rm idf} importance weighting.](https://arxiv.org/html/1904.09675/1904.09675v3/bert_fig.png)

*Illustration of the computation of the recall metric R BERT R_{\textsc{BERT}}{} . Given the reference x x and candidate x ^ \hat{x}{} , we compute BERT embeddings and pairwise cosine similarity. We highlight the greedy matching in red, and include the optional idf {\rm idf} importance weighting.*

<sub>figure: `1904.09675v3/bert_fig.png`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. Experimental Setup / Contextual Embedding Models

We evaluate our approach on machine translation and image captioning. We evaluate twelve pre-trained contextual embedding models, including variants of BERT ( Devlin et al., 2019 ) , RoBERTa ( Liu et al., 2019b ) , XLNet ( Yang et al., 2019b ) , and XLM ( Lample & Conneau, 2019 ) . We present the best-performing models in Section 5 . We use the 24-layer RoBERTa large \text{RoBERTa}_{{\rm large}} model 5 5 5 We use the tokenizer provided with each model. For all Hugging Face models that use the GPT-2 tokenizer, at the time of our experiments, the tokenizer adds a space to the beginning of each sentence. for English tasks, 12-layer BERT chinese \textsc{BERT}_{{\rm chinese}} model for Chinese tasks, and the 12-layer cased multilingual BERT multi \textsc{BERT}_{{\rm multi}} model for other languages. 6 6 6 All the models used are from https://github.com/huggingface/pytorch-transformers . We show the performance of all other models in Appendix F . Contextual embedding models generate embedding representations at every layer in the encoder network. Past work has shown that intermediate layers produce more effective representations for semantic tasks ( Liu et al., 2019a ) . We use the WMT16 dataset ( Bojar et al., 2016 ) as a validation set to select the best layer of each model (Appendix B ).

<sub>§4 Experimental Setup · §Contextual Embedding Models</sub>

### 2. Machine Translation

Our main evaluation corpus is the WMT18 metric evaluation dataset ( Ma et al., 2018 ) , which contains predictions of 149 translation systems across 14 language pairs, gold references, and two types of human judgment scores. Segment-level human judgments assign a score to each reference-candidate pair. System-level human judgments associate each system with a single score based on all pairs in the test set. WMT18 includes translations from English to Czech, German, Estonian, Finnish, Russian, and Turkish, and from the same set of languages to English. We follow the WMT18 standard practice and use absolute Pearson correlation | ρ | \lvert\rho\rvert and Kendall rank correlation τ \tau to evaluate metric quality, and compute significance with the Williams test ( Williams, 1959 ) for | ρ | \lvert\rho\rvert and bootstrap re-sampling for τ \tau as suggested by Graham & Baldwin (2014) . We compute system-level scores by averaging BERTScore for every reference-candidate pair. We also experiment with hybrid systems by randomly sampling one candidate sentence from one of the available systems for each reference sentence ( Graham & Liu, 2016 ) . This enables system-level experiments with a higher number of systems. Human judgments of each hybrid system are created by averaging the WMT18 segment-level human judgments for the corresponding sentences in the sampled data. We compare BERTScore s to one canonical metric for each category introduced in Section 2 , and include the comparison with all other participating metrics from WMT18 in Appendix F . In addition to the standard evaluation, we design model selection experiments. We use 10K hybrid systems super-sampled from WMT18. We randomly select 100 out of 10K hybrid systems, and rank them using the automatic metrics. We repeat this process 100K times. We report the percentage of the metric ranking agreeing with the human ranking on the best system (Hits@1). In Tables 23 - 28 , we include two additional measures to the model selection study: (a) the mean reciprocal rank of the top metric-rated system according to the human ranking, and (b) the difference between the human score of the top human-rated system and that of the top metric-rated system. Additionally, we report the same study on the WMT17 ( Bojar et al., 2017 ) and the WMT16 ( Bojar et al., 2016 ) datasests in Appendix F . 7 7 7 For WMT16, we only conduct segment-level experiments on to-English pairs due to errors in the dataset. This adds 202 systems to our evaluation.

<sub>§Machine Translation</sub>

### 3. Image Captioning

We use the human judgments of twelve submission entries from the COCO 2015 Captioning Challenge. Each participating system generates a caption for each image in the COCO validation set ( Lin et al., 2014 ) , and each image has approximately five reference captions. Following Cui et al. (2018) , we compute the Pearson correlation with two system-level metrics: the percentage of captions that are evaluated as better or equal to human captions (M1) and the percentage of captions that are indistinguishable from human captions (M2). We compute BERTScore with multiple references by scoring the candidate with each available reference and returning the highest score. We compare with eight task-agnostic metrics: Bleu ( Papineni et al., 2002 ) , Meteor ( Banerjee & Lavie, 2005 ) , Rouge -L ( Lin, 2004 ) , CIDEr ( Vedantam et al., 2015 ) , BEER ( Stanojević & Sima’an, 2014 ) , EED ( Stanchev et al., 2019 ) , chrF++ ( Popović, 2017 ) , and CharacTER ( Wang et al., 2016 ) . We also compare with two task-specific metrics: Spice ( Anderson et al., 2016 ) and Leic ( Cui et al., 2018 ) . Spice is computed using the similarity of scene graphs parsed from the reference and candidate captions. Leic is trained to predict if a caption is written by a human given the image.

<sub>§Image Captioning</sub>

## 总结与展望
BERTScore 用嵌入相似度替代 n-gram 匹配，是「用代理指标替代人工判断」的一个成功案例。它在预算方向的意义是：代理指标值得用，前提是它与真值的相关性被反复测量过。

**It connects to**

- `2402.14992` — 同样用便宜的代理去逼近贵的真值。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** We propose BERTScore , a new metric for evaluating generated text against gold standard references. BERTScore is purposely designed to be simple, task agnostic, and easy to use. Our analysis illustrates how BERTScore resolves some of the limitations of commonly used metrics, especially on challenging adversarial examples. We conduct extensive experiments with various configuration choices for BERTScore , including the contextual embedding model used and the use of importance weighting. Overall, our extensive experiments, including the ones in the appendix, show that BERTScore achieves better correlation than common metrics, and is effective for model selection. However, there is no one configuration of BERTScore that clearly outperforms all others. While the differences between the top configurations are often small, it is important for the user to be aware of the different trade-offs, and consider the domain and languages when selecting the exact configuration to use. In general, for machine translation evaluation, we suggest using F BERT F_{\textsc{BERT}} , which we find the most reliable. For evaluating text generation in English, we recommend using the 24-layer RoBERTa large \text{RoBERTa}_{{\rm large}} model to compute BERTScore . For non-English language, the multilingual BERT multi \textsc{BERT}_{{\rm multi}} is a suitable choice although BERTScore computed with this model has less stable performance on low-resource languages. We report the optimal hyperparameter for all models we experimented with in Appendix B

**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 它与人工判断的相关性在长文本上明显下降。

<sub>§7 Discussion</sub>

---

# Empirical Bernstein Bounds and Sample Variance Penalization

arXiv:0907.3740 · 2009 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/0907.3740)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
The method of empirical risk minimization (ERM) is so intuitive, that some of the less plausible alternatives have received little attention by the machine learning community. In this work we present sample variance penalization (SVP), a method which is motivated by some variance-sensitive, data-dependent confidence bounds, which we develop in the paper. We describe circumstances under which SVP works better than ERM and provide some preliminary experimental results which confirm the theory.

<sub>§3 Empirical Bernstein bounds for function classes of polynomial growth</sub>

### 3. Sample variance penalization versus empirical risk minimization

Since empirical Bernstein bounds are observable, have estimation errors which can be as small as O ⁡ ( 1 / n ) O\left(1/n\right) for small sample variances, and can be adjusted to hold uniformly over realistic function classes, they suggest a method which minimizes the bounds of Corollary 5 or Theorem 6 . Specifically we consider the algorithm where λ \lambda is a non-negative parameter. We call this method sample variance penalization (SVP). Choosing the regularization parameter λ = 0 \lambda=0 reduces the algorithm to empirical risk minimization (ERM). It is intuitively clear that SVP will be inferior to ERM if losses corresponding to better hypotheses have larger variances than the worse ones. But this seems to be a somewhat unnatural situation. If, on the other hand, there are some optimal hypotheses of small variance, then SVP should work well. To make this rigorous we provide a result, which can be used to bound the excess risk of S ​ V ​ P λ SVP_{\lambda} . Below we use Theorem 6 , but it is clear how the argument is to be modified to obtain better constants for finite hypothesis spaces. Proof: Denote the hypothesis S ​ V ​ P λ ​ ( 𝐗 ) SVP_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{X}\right) by f ^ \hat{f} . By Theorem 6 we have with probability at least 1 − δ / 3 1-\delta/3 that The second inequality follows from the definition of S ​ V ​ P λ SVP_{\lambda} . By Bennett’s inequality (Theorem 3 ) we have with probability at least 1 − δ / 3 1-\delta/3 that and by Theorem 10 ( 4 ) we have with probability at least 1 − δ / 3 1-\delta/3 that Combining these three inequalities in a union bound and using ln ⁡ ( 3 ​ ℳ ​ ( n ) / δ ) ≥ 1 \ln\left(3\mathcal{M}\left(n\right)/\delta\right)\geq 1 and some other crude but obvious estimates, we obtain with probability at least 1 − δ 1-\delta If we let f ∗ f^{\ast} be an optimal hypothesis we obtain a bound on the excess risk. The square-root term in the bound scales with the standard deviation of this hypothesis, which can be quite small. In particular, if there is an optimal (minimal risk) hypothesis of zero variance, then the excess risk of the hypothesis chosen by SVP decays as ( ln ⁡ ℳ ⁡ ( n ) ) / n \left(\ln\mathcal{M}\left(n\right)\right)/n . In the case of finite hypothesis spaces ℳ ⁡ ( n ) = | ℱ | \mathcal{M}(n)=\left|\mathcal{F}\right| is independent of n n and the excess risk then decays as 1 / n 1/n . Observe that apart from the complexity bound on ℱ \mathcal{F} no assumption such as convexity of the function class or special properties of the loss functions were needed to derive this result. To demonstrate a potential competitive edge of SVP over ERM we will now give a very simple example of this type, where the excess risk of the hypothesis chosen by ERM is of order O ⁡ ( 1 / n ) O\left(1/\sqrt{n}\right) . Suppose that ℱ \mathcal{F} consists of only two hypotheses ℱ = { c 1 / 2 , b 1 / 2 + ϵ } \mathcal{F}=\left\{c_{1/2},b_{1/2+\epsilon}\right\} . The underlying distribution μ \mu is such that c 1 / 2 ​ ( X ) = 1 / 2 c_{1/2}\left(X\right)=1/2 almost surely and b 1 / 2 + ϵ ​ ( X ) b_{1/2+\epsilon}\left(X\right) is a Bernoulli variable with expectation 1 / 2 + ϵ 1/2+\epsilon , where ϵ ≤ 1 / 8 \epsilon\leq 1/\sqrt{8} . The hypothesis c 1 / 2 c_{1/2} is optimal and has zero variance, the hypothesis b 1 / 2 + ϵ b_{1/2+\epsilon} has excess risk ϵ \epsilon and variance 1 / 4 − ϵ 2 1/4-\epsilon^{2} . We are given an i.i.d. sample 𝐗 = ( X 1 , … , X n ) ∼ μ n \mathbf{X}=\left(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\right)\sim\mu^{n} on which we are to base the selection of either hypothesis. It follows from the previous theorem (with f ∗ = c 1 / 2 f^{\ast}=c_{1/2} ), that the excess risk of S ​ V ​ P λ SVP_{\lambda} decays as 1 / n 1/n , for suitably chosen λ \lambda . To make our point we need to give a lower bound for the excess risk of empirical risk minimization. We use the following inequality due to Slud which we cite in the form given in [ 1 , p. 363] . Now ERM selects the inferior hypothesis b 1 / 2 + ϵ b_{1/2+\epsilon} if We therefore obtain from Theorem 16 , with p = 1 / 2 − ϵ p=1/2-\epsilon and t = n / 2 t=n/2 that A well known bound for standard normal random variables gives for η > 0 \eta>0 If we assume n ≥ ϵ − 2 n\geq\epsilon^{-2} we have n ​ ϵ / 1 / 4 − ϵ 2 ≥ 2 \sqrt{n}\epsilon/\sqrt{1/4-\epsilon^{2}}\geq 2 , so where we used ϵ ≤ 1 / 8 \epsilon\leq 1/\sqrt{8} in the last inequality. Since this is just the probability that the excess risk is ϵ \epsilon we arrive at the following statement: For every n ≥ ϵ − 2 n\geq\epsilon^{-2} there exists δ \delta ( = e − 8 ​ n ​ ϵ 2 =e^{-8n\epsilon^{2}} ) such that the excess risk of the hypothesis generated by ERM is at least with probability at least δ \delta . Therefore the excess risk for ERM cannot have a faster rate than O ⁡ ( 1 / n ) O\left(1/\sqrt{n}\right) . This example is of course a very artificial construction, chosen as a simple illustration. It is clear that the conclusions do not change if we add any number of deterministic hypotheses with risk larger than 1 / 2 1/2 (they simply have no effect), or if we add any number of Bernoulli hypotheses with risk at least 1 / 2 + ϵ 1/2+\epsilon (they just make things worse for ERM). To obtain a more practical insight into the potential advantages of SVP we have conducted a simple experiment, where 𝒳 = [ 0 , 1 ] K \mathcal{X}=\left[0,1\right]^{K} and the random variable X ∈ 𝒳 X\in\mathcal{X} is distributed according to ∏ k = 1 K μ a k , b k \prod_{k=1}^{K}\mu_{a_{k},b_{k}} where Each coordinate π k ​ ( X ) \pi_{k}\left(X\right) of X X is thus a binary random variable, assuming the values a k − b k a_{k}-b_{k} and a k + b k a_{k}+b_{k} with equal probability, having expectation a k a_{k} and variance b k 2 b_{k}^{2} . The distribution of X X is itself generated at random by selecting the pairs ( a k , b k ) \left(a_{k},b_{k}\right) independently: a k a_{k} is chosen from the uniform distribution on [ B , 1 − B ] \left[B,1-B\right] and the standard deviation b k b_{k} is chosen from the uniform distribution on the interval [ 0 , B ] \left[0,B\right] . Thus B B is the only parameter governing the generation of the distribution. As hypotheses we just take the K K coordinate functions π k \pi_{k} in [ 0 , 1 ] K \left[0,1\right]^{K} . Selecting the k k -th hypothesis then just means that we select the corresponding distribution μ a k , b k \mu_{a_{k},b_{k}} . Of course we want to find a hypothesis of small risk a k a_{k} , but we can only observe a k a_{k} through the corresponding sample, the observation being obscured by the variance b k 2 b_{k}^{2} . We chose B = 1 / 4 B=1/4 and K = 500 K=500 . We tested the algorithm ( 10 ) with λ = 0 \lambda=0 , corresponding to ERM, and λ = 2.5 \lambda=2.5 . The sample sizes ranged from 10 to 500. We recorded the true risks of the respective hypotheses generated, and averaged these risks over 10000 randomly generated distributions. The results are reported in Figure 1 and show clearly the advantage of SVP in this particular case. It must however be pointed out that this advantage, while being consistent, is small compared to the risk of the optimal hypotheses (around 1 / 4 1/4 ). If we try to extract a practical conclusion from Theorem 15 , our example and the experiment, then it appears that SVP might be a good alternative to ERM, whenever the optimal members of the hypothesis space still have substantial risk (for otherwise ERM would do just as good), but there are optimal hypotheses of very small variance. These two conditions seem to be generic for many noisy situations: when the noise arises from many independent sources, but does not depend too much on any single source, then the loss of an optimal hypothesis should be sharply concentrated around its expectation (e.g. by the bounded difference inequality - see [ 8 ] ), resulting in a small variance.

<sub>§4 Sample variance penalization versus empirical risk minimization</sub>

## 总结与展望
这篇给出基于经验方差的 Bernstein 型置信界，用于在样本有限且有风险时做选择。它对本方向的意义是：区间不是事后报告，而是选择规则的一部分——我们的噪声棒其实是这一类界的最粗版本。

**It connects to**

- `2410.23614` — 一个给固定样本的界，一个给可选停时的界。

## 不足之处
**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 界需要样本方差，而我们很多轮只有一次观测。

# How Many Tasks Are Enough for Agent Benchmark Decisions? A Replay Analysis of Public LLM Agent Benchmarks

arXiv:2607.12338 · 2026 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.12338)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
Agent benchmarks turn many task outcomes into comparisons between systems. In this paper, a system is a public leaderboard entry, including the model, prompt, tools, scaffolding, retry logic, and other components that produce task outcomes. Once every task has run, the benchmark gives each system a success rate. For a pair of systems, a benchmark user may then ask whether system A A ’s paired success rate is high enough relative to system B B ’s to justify preferring A A for the decision at hand.

<sub>§1. Introduction</sub>

  N6["§Error denominators."]
  N7["§When a Budget Counts as Sufficient"]
  N8["§Policies Compared in Replay"]
  N9["§Task-ordering policies."]
  N10["§Bootstrap policies."]
  N11["§Comparator policies."]
  N0 --> N1
  N1 --> N2

  N4 --> N5

  N6 --> N7
  N7 --> N8

![Replay tests whether partial task evidence can support the same pairwise decision as the completed benchmark. Flow diagram showing completed benchmark records, partial replay, and a decision report with decision error, task-group coverage, unresolved comparisons, and minimum budget.](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.12338/2607.12338v1/system_decision_layer.png)

*Replay tests whether partial task evidence can support the same pairwise decision as the completed benchmark. Flow diagram showing completed benchmark records, partial replay, and a decision report with decision error, task-group coverage, unresolved comparisons, and minimum budget.*

<sub>figure: `2607.12338v1/system_decision_layer.png`</sub>

## 机制
### 1. From Partial Scores to Decisions

The setup starts from a completed benchmark record. Two systems have pass/fail outcomes on the same tasks. The completed record tells us how much the tested system outperformed the comparison system on that fixed task set. Once the evaluator chooses an improvement threshold, the completed benchmark induces the full-benchmark decision: a positive conclusion for the tested system if its paired improvement is strictly above the threshold, and a negative conclusion otherwise. Metric names below use false accept and false reject for these two error directions. Ordinary prose uses positive conclusion, negative conclusion, and unresolved comparison. The replay asks what would have happened with less evidence. If the evaluator had seen only a fixed fraction of the task set, would the observed subset have led to the same pairwise conclusion? A useful partial evaluation must do more than match the final decision on easy cases. It must cover the relevant task groups and return final conclusions often enough to be useful. Formally, let T = { t 1 , … , t N } T=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{N}\} be the fixed task set. Each task can have metadata such as repository, domain, difficulty, or expected cost. For an ordered pairwise comparison, let A A be the tested system and B B be the comparison system. The comparison asks a one-sided evaluation question: does tested system A A clear threshold δ \delta over comparison system B B ? The two orientations of an unordered pair share the same task outcomes. The ordered count therefore counts evaluation questions, while the unordered count describes system-pair evidence. We report both counts and also repeat the analysis after keeping one orientation per unordered pair. For task t i t_{i} , Y i , A ∈ { 0 , 1 } Y_{i,A}\in\{0,1\} and Y i , B ∈ { 0 , 1 } Y_{i,B}\in\{0,1\} are verified outcomes. The paired task difference is For a subset S b S_{b} observed by budget b b , the partial paired improvement is

<sub>§From Partial Scores to Decisions</sub>

### 2. Threshold.

Before replaying partial evaluations, the evaluator sets an improvement threshold δ \delta . The evaluator supplies this threshold as part of the decision problem; the benchmark supplies the task outcomes. The threshold can be zero when the question is only whether one system scores strictly above another, or positive when the evaluator requires a larger margin before preferring the tested system. The full-benchmark decision is positive for the tested system when Δ full > δ \Delta_{\mathrm{full}}>\delta and negative otherwise. This is the decision that each replay tries to match. The threshold should be set before replay to the smallest improvement that would justify preferring the tested system over the comparison system, given evaluation cost and operational risk. The public experiments report 0 pp, 5 pp, and 10 pp. The 0 pp setting measures strict ordering, where one-task differences can decide the full-benchmark label. The 5 pp and 10 pp settings show how the sufficient task budget changes when the evaluator requires a larger improvement. The analysis can be rerun at any chosen threshold, so a practitioner should use the threshold that matches the decision they face. We report the number of positive and negative full-benchmark decisions at each threshold because larger thresholds can change the problem itself: they can make positive decisions rare, which can make some error rates look easier to satisfy.

<sub>§Threshold.</sub>

### 3. Policy outputs.

A budgeted policy observes a subset S b ⊂ T S_{b}\subset T before reaching budget b b . Along a budget schedule, the rule may continue sampling at intermediate budgets. At a reported budget cap, it returns a positive conclusion for A A , returns a negative conclusion for A A , or leaves the comparison unresolved. The equations denote these three outputs as accept, reject, and defer, but the prose uses the more descriptive terms above. An unresolved comparison means the budget is exhausted and the observed tasks do not support either final decision. The rule withholds a conclusion by design: a partial report should not convert weak evidence into a final pairwise claim. At a 5 pp threshold, for instance, a 4 pp partial advantage for A A does not support a positive conclusion. If uncertainty in the observed subset also prevents a confident negative conclusion, the comparison remains unresolved.

<sub>§Policy outputs.</sub>

### 4. Coverage.

Coverage is designed to prevent a specific error: a partial run can see a narrow task slice, reach a confident pairwise conclusion within that slice, and miss groups that would change the completed-benchmark decision. The relevant groups come from metadata available in each source: repository for SWE-bench, split by difficulty for AppWorld, and domain for tau-bench. The primary rule therefore requires every group to appear, with larger groups represented roughly in proportion to their share of the fixed task set. Let q i q_{i} be the group for task t i t_{i} , n g n_{g} the number of tasks in group g g , b ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] b\in(0,1] the task-budget fraction, and k b = ⌈ b ​ N ⌉ k_{b}=\lceil bN\rceil . The required count for group g g is An observed subset satisfies coverage when it contains at least r g ​ ( b ) r_{g}(b) tasks from every group g g . This floor approximates the completed benchmark’s group mixture at budget b b while guarding against the loss of a task group that appears in the completed benchmark. If the sum of the required counts exceeds the current budget, then coverage is infeasible at that budget and the coverage-aware rule leaves the comparison unresolved. A coverage failure occurs when a policy returns a final conclusion before satisfying these group-count requirements. The slack floor tests how much this proportional requirement affects the reported budget. It uses which subtracts one task from the proportional floor while still requiring every group to appear.

<sub>§Coverage.</sub>

### 5. Replay outcomes.

We measure four outcomes against the full-benchmark decision. A false accept occurs when a partial policy gives a positive conclusion for A A when the full benchmark gives a negative conclusion. A false reject occurs when a partial policy gives a negative conclusion for A A when the full benchmark gives a positive conclusion. Coverage failure records final conclusions made before the observed tasks cover the required task groups. The unresolved-comparison rate records cases where the policy leaves the comparison unresolved within budget. Together, these quantities define what the replay measures for a completed benchmark record. For a fixed completed record, threshold, budget, and policy, we average the policy’s output over sampled task orders and ordered system pairs.

<sub>§Replay outcomes.</sub>

### 6. Error denominators.

Conditional false-accept and false-reject rates are conditional on the full-benchmark decision class, not on whether the policy returns a final conclusion. The conditional false-accept denominator is all replayed cases whose full-benchmark decision is negative for A A ; unresolved cases stay in that denominator and count as neither false accepts nor correct negative conclusions. The conditional false-reject denominator is all replayed cases whose full-benchmark decision is positive for A A ; unresolved cases again stay in the denominator. Coverage failure and rates of unresolved comparisons are averaged over all replayed cases. The result describes the behavior of a chosen partial-evaluation policy on the completed benchmark record, not an estimate of future benchmark performance.

<sub>§Error denominators.</sub>

### 7. When a Budget Counts as Sufficient

A partial budget counts as sufficient only when it rules out three ways a partial run can mislead. First, the policy should rarely give the wrong positive or negative conclusion relative to the completed benchmark. Second, it should not give a final conclusion before the observed tasks cover the required groups. Third, it should resolve enough comparisons to be useful, rather than achieving low error by declining hard comparisons. The target on unresolved comparisons sets the largest fraction of replayed cases that may end without a partial conclusion at the budget. We define the minimum sufficient task budget as the smallest tested task budget b b for which Here FAR cond \mathrm{FAR}_{\mathrm{cond}} is measured over negative full-benchmark cases. FRR cond \mathrm{FRR}_{\mathrm{cond}} is measured over positive full-benchmark cases. We also report the smallest task budget that meets the error and coverage targets without constraining unresolved comparisons. The fourth line is the rate of unresolved comparisons, denoted defer ⁡ ( b ) \mathrm{defer}(b) in the equations. This line prevents a policy that resolves only easy cases from appearing reliable merely because its false-accept and false-reject rates are low. Unless otherwise stated, α = 0.05 \alpha=0.05 , γ = 0.05 \gamma=0.05 , and η = 0.25 \eta=0.25 . Together, these targets define a fixed comparison point across public sources: 5 percent conditional decision-error and coverage-failure targets, plus a requirement that at most 25 percent of comparisons remain unresolved. A practitioner should set these targets before replay to match the risk and cost of the evaluation decision. The threshold δ \delta and unresolved-comparison target η \eta have different roles. The threshold states how large a paired improvement would justify preferring A A over B B after the completed benchmark. The unresolved-comparison target states how often a partial report may return no conclusion. In a leaderboard triage setting, a larger η \eta may be acceptable if unresolved pairs are simply sent to the full benchmark. In a deployment or procurement setting, a smaller η \eta may be needed because the partial report is expected to resolve most comparisons. The public experiments therefore report 0 pp, 5 pp, and 10 pp thresholds under a fixed η = 0.25 \eta=0.25 , but these values are examples of the replay report rather than universal evaluation rules. The replay target is whether the partial task run matches the full-benchmark decision over different task orders in a fixed, completed task set. Because those orders are permutations of completed records, the reported rates describe those records. All sufficiency claims in this paper are empirical replay claims under stated thresholds, policies, and targets. Figure 1 summarizes this workflow. Completed task-level records are replayed under partial budgets to test whether a partial run can support the same pairwise decision as the completed benchmark. Each replayed case records decision errors, coverage failures, and unresolved comparisons. The aggregate report then records the minimum tested budget that meets all targets.

<sub>§When a Budget Counts as Sufficient</sub>

### 8. Policies Compared in Replay

The replay analysis uses completed records to simulate many partial evaluations without changing task outcomes. For each ordered pairwise comparison, we sample reveal orders over tasks. At each budget point, a policy observes the tasks that fall within the budget, computes the partial paired effect, checks task-group coverage, and either returns a final decision or leaves the comparison unresolved. The replay records false accept, false reject, overall decision error, unresolved comparisons, coverage failure, cost fraction when available, and budget used.

<sub>§Policies Compared in Replay</sub>

### 9. Task-ordering policies.

The same replay procedure can evaluate any rule that chooses tasks and maps observed outcomes to a final conclusion, a request for more tasks, or an unresolved comparison. We focus on policy families that represent practical evaluation choices. Forced policies must return a final conclusion at the budget, so they show what can go wrong when an evaluator insists on a decision. Deferring policies may leave the comparison unresolved when the observed tasks are not enough for either final decision. Uniform forced policies sample tasks without group or cost metadata. Group-stratified forced policies spread task groups across the reveal order, using repository for SWE-bench, split by difficulty for AppWorld, and domain for tau-bench. Cost-aware forced policies, available only when cost fields exist, reveal tasks in increasing tested-system cost.

<sub>§Task-ordering policies.</sub>

### 10. Bootstrap policies.

Bootstrap tail policies are the main policies that can leave comparisons unresolved. They work directly with paired binary task outcomes, give a tunable uncertainty check, and withhold a final decision when the observed subset is too close to the threshold. They make a final decision only when most resamples agree that the observed paired improvement is on the same side of the threshold as Δ ^ b \widehat{\Delta}_{b} . The coverage-aware version also requires task-group coverage before making a final decision. For coverage-aware bootstrap, write a a for the bootstrap tail-probability cutoff used by the policy. Larger a a values make the policy less cautious, because fewer resamples must stay away from the opposite side of the threshold. The policy returns a positive conclusion only if the sample mean is strictly above the threshold and at most a fraction a a of bootstrap resamples are at or below the threshold. It returns a negative conclusion only if the sample mean is at or below the threshold and at most a fraction a a of bootstrap resamples are above the threshold. Otherwise, the comparison remains unresolved. The main policy uses a = 0.05 a=0.05 . This has the same numerical value as the replay-level conditional error target α \alpha , but the two quantities have different roles: a a is a policy tuning parameter, while α \alpha is an empirical replay target. The empirical replay determines whether false accepts, false rejects, coverage failures, and unresolved comparisons are all within their stated limits. Bootstrap tail quantities summarize the observed subset; they are not population guarantees. The completed task set is fixed, and the replay measures how decisions change with the tasks observed at a budget. We therefore report a sweep over a ∈ { 0.02 , 0.05 , 0.10 } a\in\{0.02,0.05,0.10\} and a sweep over bootstrap replicate counts.

<sub>§Bootstrap policies.</sub>

### 11. Comparator policies.

Two comparator families separate decision-rule effects from task-allocation effects. Statistical decision-rule comparators test whether the bootstrap decision rule itself drives the budget. A coverage paired-normal test applies a one-sided paired mean test to the observed paired differences against threshold δ \delta . At the 0 pp threshold, where binary disagreements directly define the directional comparison, we also report a coverage exact McNemar test. Second, allocation comparators test whether different task allocation could reduce the budget. A Neyman coverage bootstrap first satisfies the group-coverage floor, then allocates the remaining task budget in proportion to N g ​ S g N_{g}S_{g} , where N g N_{g} is the group size and S g S_{g} is the completed-record standard deviation of paired differences in group g g . This diagnostic estimates how much variance information could help; a live evaluator requires pilot or historical records to estimate those variances before evaluation. A Serfling coverage policy uses a finite-population Hoeffding-Serfling radius on the same stratified task subsets. Together, these comparisons test whether budgets are mainly driven by allocation, uncertainty in the observed task subsets, or the bootstrap decision rule. The reveal policies fix task order before observing the current partial effect. They cover practical choices an evaluator might make: run a random subset, enforce task-group coverage, prefer cheaper tasks, require resampling evidence, or require both resampling evidence and task-group coverage. Comparing them separates effects that a single aggregate score hides: wrong decisions, unresolved comparisons, task coverage, and cost.

<sub>§Comparator policies.</sub>

## 总结与展望
这篇问「多少任务才够做一个决定」，用回放分析给出接受/拒绝/推迟三态策略，并明确指出：分数区间能提示决定，但不能替代决定策略——阈值、必须覆盖的任务组、可接受的错误类型都要另行说明。这句话几乎是我们 spec 的判据来源。

**It connects to**

- `2402.14992` — 一个决定跑多少题，一个决定题目够不够。
- `2410.23614` — 三态策略的统计基础来自 e-value 那一族。

## 不足之处
**The authors say:** The evidence supports claims about completed benchmark records. The replay estimates whether observed task subsets match the full-benchmark decision in those records. It does not estimate performance on future tasks, because the replay orders permute a fixed completed record. Extending the claim to future task draws would require an explicit sampling model and benchmark records collected under that model.

**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 回放分析基于已有基准的完整结果，现实中我们无法预知剩余题目的结果。
- 它的「推迟」依赖于有更多预算可花，我们的循环可能没有下一轮。

<sub>§6. Limitations</sub>

---

# Admissible anytime-valid sequential inference must rely on nonnegative martingales

arXiv:2009.03167 · 2022 · [source](https://arxiv.org/html/2009.03167)

> Written by an agent that read the body (DeepSeek Harness, 2026-11-24) after reading the paper; the mechanism quotes are the paper’s own words.

## 出发点
Consider a laboratory that wishes to understand if a particular intervention (‘treatment’) has any positive effect on a prespecified outcome of interest. Without getting bogged down by details, suppose the ‘average treatment effect’ of the intervention over the relevant population is denoted by θ \theta . Suppose the lab wants to test H 0 : θ ≤ 0 H_{0}:\theta\leq 0 against H 1 : θ > 0 H_{1}:\theta>0 , or to estimate θ \theta using a confidence interval. The lab believes that there is an effect, but has no idea how many subjects to collect data from: a larger sample size means more power, but also more time and money. So they conduct their experiment sequentially: subjects enter the study one at a time and are assigned to treatment or control completely at random; denote the data from subject t t as X t X_{t} .

## 机制
### 1. Martingales, max-martingales, and Ville’s inequality

A martingale is a stochastic process adapted to an underlying filtration, whose value at any time is the conditional expectation of its value at any later time. This is however not the only possible notion of martingale; another interesting notion is obtained by replacing conditional expectations by so-called conditional suprema, leading to max-martingales . Both notions play an important role in this paper. In particular, max-martingales turn out to be particularly suitable for dealing with p-processes. We briefly review their definitions and basic properties next.

<sub>§3 Martingales, max-martingales, and Ville’s inequality</sub>

### 2. Martingales (based on conditional expectation)

Given a filtration ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) and a measure 𝙿 \mathtt{P} on ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} , a process ( M t ) t ∈ ℕ 0 (M_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}} is called a martingale (with respect to ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) ) if M t M_{t} is ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} -measurable, 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -integrable, and (Sub- and supermartingales are defined by relaxing the martingale property and allowing for inequality, ≥ \geq respectively, ≤ \leq .) Since we had earlier mentioned that ℱ 0 \mathcal{F}_{0} includes an initial source of independent randomness, M 0 M_{0} is itself allowed to be random. Naturally, we have 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ M t ] = 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ M 0 ] \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[M_{t}]=\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[M_{0}] . Often, in this paper, ( M t ) (M_{t}) will be nonnegative and the latter quantity equals one and so when we say ‘a nonnegative martingale with initial value one’, we implicitly mean with initial expected value one. Given an ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} -measurable integrable random variable Y Y , the process M t := 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ Y | ℱ t ] M_{t}:=\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[Y|\mathcal{F}_{t}] is known as the Doob (or Doob–Lévy) martingale associated with Y Y . The fact that this is a martingale follows from the tower rule of the conditional expectation: 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ M t | ℱ s ] = 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ Y | ℱ t ] | ℱ s ] = M s \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[M_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[Y|\mathcal{F}_{t}]~|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=M_{s} if s ≤ t s\leq t . We now generalize these definitions to hold for an entire set of measures. Given a set 𝒫 \mathcal{P} of measures on ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} , a process ( M t ) t ∈ ℕ 0 (M_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}} is called a nonnegative 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -supermartingale ( 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NSM) (with respect to ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) ) if M t M_{t} is nonnegative, ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} -measurable, and If ( 6 ) holds with equality, ( M t ) (M_{t}) is called a nonnegative 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -martingale ( 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NM). If 𝒫 = { 𝙿 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathtt{P}\} is a singleton, we write ‘ 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -NSM’ instead of ‘ { 𝙿 } \{\mathtt{P}\} -NSM’. This notational choice is also applied to other objects. We refer to a 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NM (or NSM) as a ‘composite’ NM (or NSM), while a 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -NM is called a ‘pointwise’ NM (or NSM). Doob’s optional stopping theorem [ 6 ] extends the (sub-/super-) martingale property from deterministic times to stopping times. In general, only bounded stopping times are allowed in the optional stopping theorem; however, the nonnegativity of an NSM relieves us of this restriction. In particular, if ( N t ) (N_{t}) is (upper bounded by) a 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NSM starting in N 0 N_{0} , the optional stopping theorem implies that In fact, if ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NSM, then we additionally have 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ M τ | ℱ ρ ] ≤ M ρ \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[M_{\tau}|\mathcal{F}_{\rho}]\leq M_{\rho} for all stopping times ρ \rho and τ \tau such that ρ ≤ τ \rho\leq\tau , 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -almost surely, for each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Further, Doob’s supermartingale convergence theorem implies that if ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NSM with initial expected value one, then its limit M ∞ := lim t → ∞ M t M_{\infty}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}M_{t} exists 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -almost surely and 𝙴 𝙿 ​ [ M ∞ ] ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{P}}[M_{\infty}]\in[0,1] , for each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Stemming from his 1939 PhD thesis [ 23 ] , Ville’s inequality is a time-uniform generalization of Markov’s inequality; for our purposes, the relevant version states that if ( M t ) (M_{t}) is (upper-bounded by) a 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NSM with initial expected value one, then the following three equivalent statements hold: Note that ( 7b ) and ( 7c ) usually only hold with inequality (for example, for the singleton 𝒫 = { 𝙿 } \mathcal{P}=\{\mathtt{P}\} ) but it can hold with equality for larger nontrivial nonparametric classes 𝒫 \mathcal{P} , as we shall encounter later in Section 8.2 . We also remark that a conditional version of Ville’s inequality is also true, though we do not utilize it much in this paper. Specifically, if ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -NSM, then

<sub>§3.1 Martingales (based on conditional expectation)</sub>

### 3. The relationship between likelihood ratios and nonnegative martingales

A simple NM that arises naturally is the likelihood ratio; this is at the heart of Wald’s sequential likelihood ratio test [ 26 , 27 ] . To be specific, when testing H 0 : X s ∼ Q H_{0}:X_{s}\sim Q versus H 0 : X s ∼ P H_{0}:X_{s}\sim P , define the likelihood ratio M t := ∏ s ≤ t d ​ P d ​ Q ​ ( X s ) M_{t}:=\prod_{s\leq t}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(X_{s}) , assuming that the Radon-Nikodym derivative d ​ P / d ​ Q \mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}Q exists. If P , Q P,Q have densities p , q p,q with respect to a common measure then each term in the product is just p ⁡ ( X s ) / q ⁡ ( X s ) p(X_{s})/q(X_{s}) . Let Q ∞ Q^{\infty} now denote the distribution under which the sequence is i.i.d., each element distributed according to Q Q . Wald effectively proved that ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a Q ∞ Q^{\infty} -NM and a test that rejects if M t ≥ 1 / α M_{t}\geq 1/\alpha controls the Type-I error at level α \alpha due to Ville’s inequality (Wald proved the result from scratch, but he was not unaware of the evolving language of martingales and Ville’s thesis [ 2 ] ). It is also apparent that every nonnegative martingale is a product of nonnegative random variables with conditional mean one, meaning that if ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NM, then M t = ∏ s ≤ t Y s M_{t}=\prod_{s\leq t}Y_{s} , where ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is adapted to ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) and 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ Y t | ℱ t − 1 ] = 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[Y_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=1 for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} ; to see this, simply define the multiplicative increment as Y t := M t / M t − 1 Y_{t}:=M_{t}/M_{t-1} with 0 / 0 := 1 0/0:=1 . At first sight, despite having a product form, it may appear like nonnegative martingales are strict generalizations of likelihood ratio processes. However, in fact, a converse statement is also true: not only is every likelihood ratio a martingale (under the null), but every martingale is also implicitly a likelihood ratio; this was discussed by Shafer et al. [22] for point nulls, and we generalize it below to the composite case, borrowing the terminology of ‘implied alternative’ from Shafer [21] . To make the following result precise we assume that the sequence of observations ( U , ( X t ) t ∈ ℕ ) (U,(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}) is a process on the space Ω = ℝ ℕ \Omega=\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}} of real-valued sequences, and we let ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) be the canonical filtration. We recognize that the above statement may be known to different researchers in some form, but it does provide useful intuition and we have not seen it stated in the generality above in the statistics literature. The proof is in Section B . The informal takeaway message is that nonnegative martingales are implicitly likelihood ratios, but the former are typically easier to identify (or construct) in composite null settings.

<sub>§3.2 The relationship between likelihood ratios and nonnegative martingales</sub>

### 4. Max-martingales (based on conditional supremum)

Max-martingales are defined by replacing the conditional expectation by the conditional supremum , so we start by reviewing this notion; more information can be found in Barron et al. [1] and Larsson [15] ; see also Appendix A.2 . For a given probability measure 𝙿 \mathtt{P} , random variable Y Y , and sub- σ \sigma -algebra 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , the 𝒢 \mathcal{G} -conditional supremum is defined as the smallest 𝒢 \mathcal{G} -measurable almost sure upper bound on Y Y : Note that ⋁ 𝙿 [ Y ∣ 𝒢 ] ≥ Y {\textstyle\bigvee_{\mathtt{P}}}[Y\mid\mathcal{G}]\geq Y by construction, and is the smallest 𝒢 \mathcal{G} -measurable random variable with that property. (Here and in the rest of this subsection, equalities are understood in the 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -almost sure sense.) The conditional supremum can be viewed as a nonlinear analog of the conditional expectation, and has similar properties. In particular, one has a ‘tower property’ which states that for nested sub- σ \sigma -algebras 𝒢 ⊂ ℋ \mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{H} , one has Given a filtration ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) , a process ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is called a 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -max-martingale , or 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -MM for short, if Any max-martingale ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is almost surely decreasing, which ensures that the limit lim t → ∞ Y t \lim_{t\to\infty}Y_{t} exists in [ − ∞ , ∞ ] [-\infty,\infty] . We call a max-martingale closed if Y t = ⋁ 𝙿 [ lim s → ∞ Y s ∣ ℱ t ] Y_{t}=\bigvee_{\mathtt{P}}[\lim_{s\to\infty}Y_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}] for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . As earlier, given a set 𝒫 \mathcal{P} of measures on ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} , a process ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is called a (closed) 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -max-martingale ( 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -MM) if ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is a (closed) 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -MM for each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} . One can also introduce notions of sub- and supermartingales using the conditional supremum, although we will not use these here. Moreover, one can analogously define conditional infimum martingales using ⋀ \bigwedge instead of ⋁ \bigvee , and all properties stated above and below also hold analogously. In further analogy with (standard) martingales, max-martingales satisfy an optional stopping theorem [ 15 , Lemma 2.10] . Specifically, consider the process Y t := ⋁ 𝙿 [ Y ∣ ℱ t ] Y_{t}:=\bigvee_{\mathtt{P}}[Y\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}] for an ℱ ∞ \mathcal{F}_{\infty} -measurable Y Y . Thanks to the tower property, ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is then a max-martingale; we call such a construction a Doob-Lévy MM. We then have for all finite stopping times ρ \rho and τ \tau such that ρ ≤ τ \rho\leq\tau . The connection between 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -MMs and 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -NMs goes beyond mere analogies. If ( M t ) (M_{t}) is a 𝙿 \mathtt{P} -NM with M 0 > 0 M_{0}>0 , the following statement easily follows from [ 15 , Proposition 4.1] : A word of warning: although the conditional supremum and conditional expectation are in some ways similar, they sometimes behave very differently. In particular, the conditional supremum only depends on the underlying measure 𝙿 \mathtt{P} through its zero measure sets. Computing the conditional supremum under a different measure 𝙿 ′ \mathtt{P}^{\prime} thus gives the same result whenever the two measures are mutually absolutely continuous. This is in stark contrast to the behavior of the conditional expectation. (On the other hand, if 𝙿 \mathtt{P} and 𝙿 ′ \mathtt{P}^{\prime} are mutually singular, as is often the case in our infinite-horizon situations, then one can make no general statements about the relation between the corresponding conditional suprema.) Although any nonnegative max-martingale ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is almost surely decreasing, which ensures that the limit lim t → ∞ Y t \lim_{t\to\infty}Y_{t} exists, it is possible that Y t Y_{t} and ⋁ 𝙿 [ lim s → ∞ Y s ∣ ℱ t ] \bigvee_{\mathtt{P}}[\lim_{s\to\infty}Y_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}] are not necessarily the same, i.e., that ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is not closed. Indeed, we have the following non-uniqueness property. If ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) is a max-martingale then we usually can find another max-martingale ( Y t ′ ) (Y_{t}^{\prime}) with Y t ′ > Y t Y_{t}^{\prime}>Y_{t} for each t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , but ( Y t ′ ) (Y^{\prime}_{t}) and ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) have the same limit, almost surely. For example, assume ( Z t ) (Z_{t}) is i.i.d. Bernoulli( 1 / 2 1/2 ), independent of ( Y t ) (Y_{t}) , and adapted to ( ℱ t ) (\mathcal{F}_{t}) . Define Y t ′ = Y t + ∏ s ≤ t ( Z s ∨ 1 / 2 ) Y_{t}^{\prime}=Y_{t}+\prod_{s\leq t}(Z_{s}\vee 1/2) . Then ( Y t ′ ) (Y_{t}^{\prime}) is also a max-martingale, with Y t ′ ≥ Y t + 2 − t > Y t Y_{t}^{\prime}\geq Y_{t}+2^{-t}>Y_{t} for all t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , but converging almost surely to the same limit as Y t ′ Y^{\prime}_{t} as t → ∞ t\to\infty . A similar phenomenon also holds true for martingales: if ( M t ) (M_{t}) is an NM then M t ′ = M t + ∏ s ≤ t ( Z s + 1 / 2 ) M_{t}^{\prime}=M_{t}+\prod_{s\leq t}(Z_{s}+1/2) is another NM with M t ′ ≥ M t + ( 1 / 2 ) t > M t M_{t}^{\prime}\geq M_{t}+(1/2)^{t}>M_{t} for all t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , but lim t → ∞ M t ′ = lim t → ∞ M t \lim_{t\to\infty}M^{\prime}_{t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}M_{t} . Hence also for nonnegative martingales, we often do not have that M t M_{t} and 𝙴 ⁡ [ lim s → ∞ M s | ℱ t ] \mathtt{E}[\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}M_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{t}] are the same. We conclude this subsection with an interesting example.

### 5. Tools for anytime sequential inference and their admissibility

We formally introduce the four instruments for anytime-valid sequential inference that play central roles in our paper. We present a rather natural definition of admissibility for each of the instruments below, but recognize that other alternatives may be suitable depending on the context. If ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process, then { ( 𝟏 𝔭 t ≤ α ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{p}_{t}\leq\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is a nested family of ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -STs. ### 6. 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-processes, 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-processes, and ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests

Let the (unknown) distribution of the observed data sequence be denoted 𝙿 \mathtt{P} . Suppose we wish to test the null H 0 : 𝙿 ∈ 𝒬 H_{0}:\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{Q} for some 𝒬 ⊂ 𝒫 \mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P} , against H 1 : 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 H_{1}:\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} (or against H 1 : 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \ 𝒬 H_{1}:\mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P}\backslash\mathcal{Q} ). A sequence ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is called a p-process for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} if it satisfies Above, we have implicitly defined and used For succinctness, we say ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process , or simply a p-process if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is clear from context. By Lemma 2 , the above condition is equivalent to requiring that Note that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a p-process if and only if the running infimum ( inf s ≤ t 𝔭 s ) (\inf_{s\leq t}\mathfrak{p}_{s}) is a p-process, so it helps to think of ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) as a nonincreasing sequence; in this case, we have 𝔭 ∞ = lim t → ∞ 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t} (the limit exists and equals the lim inf \liminf used earlier). When we refer only to the validity of the single random variable 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t} and not the sequence ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) , we say that 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t} is a p-value for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , meaning that its distribution is stochastically larger than uniform under any 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . The connection to Ville’s inequality ( 7a ), and thus to martingales, should be apparent. A sequence ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is called an e-process for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , or a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process , if Analogous to the p-process case above, we have implicitly defined and used In fact, e-processes themselves can be defined in many equivalent ways, and we record this below. Property (i) is used as the defining property in Grünwald et al. [9] , while (vi) is the defining property used in Howard et al. [10] , and while these seem to be visually different, they are in fact identical. The proof below establishes that property (iv) implies (v) , by constructing the Snell envelope (under each 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} ) for ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) , and taking its Doob decomposition. The optional stopping theorem for nonnegative supermartingales establishes that property (vi) implies (i) . All other implications are straightforward. The implications (i) ⇒ \Rightarrow (ii) ⇒ \Rightarrow (iii) ⇒ \Rightarrow (iv) and (v) ⇒ \Rightarrow (vi) ⇒ \Rightarrow (i) are immediate. We thus focus on the non-trivial implication (iv) ⇒ \Rightarrow (v) . To do so we fix any 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} and define the Snell envelope of ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) as the process ( L t ) (L_{t}) given by where τ \tau ranges over all bounded stopping times. It is clear that L t ≥ 𝔢 t L_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t} since τ = t \tau=t is one of the stopping time occurring in the essential supremum, for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . We claim that ( L t ) (L_{t}) is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -supermartingale with 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ L 0 ] ≤ 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[L_{0}]\leq 1 . The supermartingale property of the Snell envelope is well-known, but for the convenience of the reader we include the short proof. This uses properties of the essential supremum reviewed in Appendix A.2 , in particular Proposition 45 . For each fixed t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , L t L_{t} is the essential supremum of the family consisting of all 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ M τ | 𝒢 t ] \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[M_{\tau}|\mathcal{G}_{t}] where τ ≥ t \tau\geq t is a bounded stopping time. This family is closed under maxima. To see this, let τ \tau and τ ′ \tau^{\prime} be two bounded stopping times, define A = { 𝙴 𝚀 [ 𝔢 τ | 𝒢 t ] > 𝙴 𝚀 [ 𝔢 τ ′ ∣ 𝒢 t ] } A=\{\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}|\mathcal{G}_{t}]>\mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau^{\prime}}\mid\mathcal{G}_{t}]\} , and set τ ′′ = τ ​ 𝟏 A + τ ′ ​ 𝟏 A c \tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau\mathbf{1}_{A}+\tau^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{A^{c}} . Since A A lies in 𝒢 t \mathcal{G}_{t} and τ , τ ′ ≥ t \tau,\tau^{\prime}\geq t , we have that τ ′′ \tau^{\prime\prime} is a (bounded) stopping time and we obtain This demonstrates closure under maxima. Consequently we can apply Proposition 45 to obtain a sequence of bounded stopping times { τ n } n ∈ ℕ \{\tau_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}} with τ n ≥ t \tau_{n}\geq t such that 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 τ n | 𝒢 t ] ↑ L t \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau_{n}}|\mathcal{G}_{t}]\uparrow L_{t} almost surely. The conditional version of the monotone convergence theorem, the tower rule, and the definition of L t − 1 L_{t-1} , then yield Note that this argument applies even with t = 0 t=0 , where we use the convention that 𝒢 − 1 \mathcal{G}_{-1} is the trivial sigma-field and L − 1 = 1 L_{-1}=1 . This is where the hypothesis (iv) is used: in the last inequality we have 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 τ n ] ≤ 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau_{n}}]\leq 1 . We conclude that 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ L 0 ] ≤ 1 \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[L_{0}]\leq 1 , that each L t L_{t} is integrable, and that ( L t ) (L_{t}) is a supermartingale. Since we have established that the Snell envelope ( L t ) (L_{t}) is a supermartingale we can write down its Doob decomposition as L t = M t − A t L_{t}=M_{t}-A_{t} for a unique 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -martingale ( M t ) (M_{t}) with M 0 = L 0 M_{0}=L_{0} , and a unique nondecreasing predictable process ( A t ) (A_{t}) with A 0 = 0 A_{0}=0 . Thus M t ≥ L t ≥ 𝔢 t M_{t}\geq L_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t} 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -almost surely for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , showing that ( M t ) (M_{t}) is the required 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NM that upper bounds ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) . ∎ It is worth noting that already in the case of a singleton 𝒬 := { 𝚀 } \mathcal{Q}:=\{\mathtt{Q}\} , if one additionally desired a conditional safety property to hold for ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) , namely that 𝙴 𝚀 ​ [ 𝔢 τ | ℱ s ] ≤ 𝔢 s ∧ τ \mathtt{E}_{\mathtt{Q}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\leq\mathfrak{e}_{s\wedge\tau} for arbitrary times s s and stopping times τ \tau , then ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) must necessarily be a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM; indeed by taking τ = t \tau=t for t ≥ s t\geq s , we recover the definition of a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM. In fact, if one would like such a property to hold for every 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} in a composite 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , such a requirement can only be satisfied by a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NSM. Despite the fact that we will not require this conditional property, we will see that (composite) 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NMs or (pointwise) 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NMs play a central, universal role in constructing e-processes that may not themselves be martingales. A p-process or e-process does not directly yield a decision rule for when to reject the null hypothesis; they are real-valued measures of evidence, and need to be coupled with a decision rule in order to yield a test. A binary sequence ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is called a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential test for H 0 H_{0} —or ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST —if As with p-processes, we think of ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) as nondecreasing, and have implicitly defined ψ ∞ := lim t → ∞ ψ t \psi_{\infty}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\psi_{t} .

### 7. Defining inadmissibility and admissibility

We follow the standard convention of using the term ‘admissible’ as a shorthand for ‘not inadmissible’, so we only define inadmissibility below. We say that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists ( 𝔭 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{t}) that is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process, and is always at least as good but sometimes strictly better; more formally, 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔭 t ′ ≤ 𝔭 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{t}\leq\mathfrak{p}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and that 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔭 t ′ < 𝔭 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{t}<\mathfrak{p}_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . We say that ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process ( 𝔢 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}) , such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ ≥ 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and also 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ > 𝔢 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}>\mathfrak{e}_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . Finally, ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential test ( ψ t ′ ) (\psi^{\prime}_{t}) , such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( ψ t ′ ≥ ψ t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\psi^{\prime}_{t}\geq\psi_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and 𝚀 ⁡ ( ψ t ′ > ψ t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(\psi^{\prime}_{t}>\psi_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . The above definition of admissibility does not specify any alternative. What allows us to do so is that the admissibility conditions are stated ‘almost surely’. To elaborate, assume there was an alternative, say 𝙿 ∗ \mathtt{P}^{*} such that there existed some time t t and an event A ∈ ℱ t A\in\mathcal{F}_{t} with 𝙿 ∗ ​ ( A ) > 0 \mathtt{P}^{*}(A)>0 but 𝚀 ⁡ ( A ) = 0 \mathtt{Q}(A)=0 for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . In this case, on the event A A we would always set 𝔭 t = 0 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=0 , 𝔢 t = ∞ \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\infty , and ψ t = 1 \psi_{t}=1 . We could always do so because no 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} ‘is aware of’ the event A A , hence modifying a p-process on A A would not change any of its 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -distributional properties (like validity). For this reason, and to avoid any notational complication arising, from now on we always assume the following: If there exists some t ∈ ℕ t\in\mathbb{N} , some A ∈ ℱ t A\in\mathcal{F}_{t} , and some 𝙿 ∗ ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}^{*}\in\mathcal{P} with 𝙿 ∗ ​ ( A ) > 0 \mathtt{P}^{*}(A)>0 then there exists also some 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} with 𝚀 ⁡ ( A ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}(A)>0 . This is a very mild assumption! It is, for example, satisfied if there exists some 𝚀 ∗ ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q} such that every 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} is locally absolutely continuous with respect to 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} (see Appendix A.1 for a review). This previous condition is satisfied, for example, if all measures in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} are locally equivalent. In interesting situations a locally dominating measure 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} may not actually exist—for example when testing for symmetry, as we encounter later—but our standing assumption above is still true. Specifically, the aforementioned assumption also holds if each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} is locally absolutely continuous with respect to some specific 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} (potentially different for each 𝙿 \mathtt{P} )—this is often easier to check, and indeed holds in the symmetric example. Under the above assumption, the inadmissibility condition ( 10 ) holds with 𝚀 , 𝒬 \mathtt{Q},\mathcal{Q} swapped to 𝙿 , 𝒫 \mathtt{P},\mathcal{P} . Note that validity is subset-proof, meaning that validity for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} implies validity for any subset of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . In the presence of validity, admissibility is — under a condition involving polar sets — superset-proof. The following proposition will play a key role in Section 9 . Let ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) be as described in the proposition, and assume it is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admisible. We must show it is 𝒬 ′ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime} -admissible. Consider another 𝒬 ′ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime} -e-process ( 𝔢 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}) such that 𝚀 ′ ​ ( 𝔢 t ′ ≥ 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ′ ∈ 𝒬 ′ \mathtt{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime} , hence all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} . By 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissibility, 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ = 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}=\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} and then also for all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ′ \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime} thanks to the assumption regarding the polar sets. Thus ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 ′ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime} -admissible. ∎ We sometimes call 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissibility as just admissibility if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is clear from context. A family of sequential tests { ( ψ t ​ ( α ) ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\psi_{t}(\alpha))\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is said to be ‘nested in α \alpha ’, if ψ t ​ ( α ′ ) ≥ ψ t ​ ( α ) \psi_{t}(\alpha^{\prime})\geq\psi_{t}(\alpha) for any t t and α ′ ≥ α \alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha . This ensures that it is easier to reject the null with a larger error budget. We conclude this subsection with the following observation. The proof (Appendix B ) uses transfinite induction. We now move from testing to estimation.

<sub>§4.2 Defining inadmissibility and admissibility</sub>

### 8. ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequences

Let ϕ \phi be a measurable map from 𝒫 \mathcal{P} to an arbitrary set 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} . For every γ ∈ 𝒵 \gamma\in\mathcal{Z} , define and note that { 𝒫 γ } γ ∈ 𝒵 \{\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}} is a partition of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . Of special interest is the i.i.d. case where 𝙿 = μ ∞ \mathtt{P}=\mu^{\infty} . As examples, consider ϕ ​ ( 𝙿 ) = ϕ med ​ ( μ ) \phi(\mathtt{P})=\phi^{\rm{med}}(\mu) denoting the median of μ \mu , or ϕ ​ ( 𝙿 ) = ϕ mean ​ ( μ ) \phi(\mathtt{P})=\phi^{\rm{mean}}(\mu) denoting the mean of μ \mu . Another case of interest is where each 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} can be represented as 𝙿 θ \mathtt{P}_{\theta} for some unique θ ∈ Θ \theta\in\Theta (the ‘parametric’ setting) and then ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 θ ) = θ \phi(\mathtt{P}_{\theta})=\theta . One may also care about fully nonparametric functionals, for example ϕ cdf \phi^{\rm{cdf}} , which maps μ \mu to its cumulative distribution function (or ϕ pdf \phi^{\rm{pdf}} if μ \mu has a Lebesgue density). For a set of distributions 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , define ϕ ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) = ⋃ 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ϕ ⁡ ( 𝚀 ) \phi(\mathcal{Q})=\bigcup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\phi(\mathtt{Q}) for short. We then define a ( 1 − α ) (1-\alpha) -confidence sequence for a functional ϕ \phi as an adapted sequence of confidence sets ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) such that This error condition can be phrased equivalently as a coverage criterion: for every 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} , we have 𝙿 ( ∀ t ∈ ℕ 0 : ϕ ( 𝙿 ) ∈ ℭ t ) ≥ 1 − α \mathtt{P}\left(\forall t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\phi(\mathtt{P})\in\mathfrak{C}_{t}\right)\geq 1-\alpha . Above, we have suppressed the dependence of ℭ t \mathfrak{C}_{t} on α \alpha for notational succinctness. Moreover, we emphasize that the probability 𝙿 \mathtt{P} on the outside matches with the inner functional ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 ) \phi(\mathtt{P}) , as it should. Indeed, the coverage probability for ϕ ⁡ ( 𝙿 ) \phi(\mathtt{P}) should hold given that the data used to construct ℭ t \mathfrak{C}_{t} was drawn according to this 𝙿 \mathtt{P} . We then say that ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -CS . If required, we define ℭ ∞ := lim inf t → ∞ ℭ t \mathfrak{C}_{\infty}:=\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mathfrak{C}_{t} . Note that even though we defined ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests without explicit reference to any functional ϕ \phi , one could associate 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to the subset of 𝒫 \mathcal{P} in which ϕ \phi takes on certain values. In other words, we can absorb the role of any such ϕ \phi into the definition of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to reduce notation. We say ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is inadmissible if there exists ( ℭ t ′ ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}^{\prime}) that is a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -CS such that 𝙿 ⁡ ( ℭ t ′ ⊆ ℭ t ) = 1 \smash{\mathtt{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{t}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}_{t})=1} for all t t and all 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} , and 𝙿 ⁡ ( ℭ t ′ ⊊ ℭ t ) > 0 \mathtt{P}(\mathfrak{C}_{t}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathfrak{C}_{t})>0 for some t t and some 𝙿 ∈ 𝒫 \mathtt{P}\in\mathcal{P} . Finally, let us mention a minor technical point. A CS ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is by definition an adapted sequence of random sets . In this paper, we take ‘adapted’ to simply mean that all events of the form { z ∈ ℭ t } \{z\in\mathfrak{C}_{t}\} belong to ℱ t \mathcal{F}_{t} . This is sufficient for our needs, and lets us avoid dealing with σ \sigma -algebras on spaces of sets. Last, as for sequential tests, a family of CSs { ( ℭ t ​ ( α ) ) } α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \{(\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha))\}_{\alpha\in[0,1]} is said to be ‘nested in α \alpha ’ if ℭ t ​ ( α ′ ) ⊆ ℭ t ​ ( α ) \mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\alpha) for any t t and α ′ ≥ α \alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha .

<sub>§4.3 ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequences</sub>

### 9. Reductions between the four instruments

We now demonstrate how to transform one tool for sequential inference into another; some of these are well-known or ‘obvious’ but some are new, especially in the composite setting. These transformations are usually not lossless or bidirectional, meaning that something may be lost in going from one to another and back again. All proofs are relegated to Appendix B . We start with the simplest direction: forming ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -sequential tests from the other three. Next, we show how to form a composite p-process from the other three. A confidence sequence can be formed by inverting families of tests, as follows. Finally, we can form e-processes by calibrating p-processes, following Shafer et al. [22] . We end this section with some basic closure properties. The proof can be found in Appendix B . Example 48 in Appendix C shows that the statement of Proposition 15 does not extend to confidence sequences.

<sub>§4.4 Reductions between the four instruments</sub>

### 10. Reducing admissible composite inference to the pointwise case

To build intuition towards composite admissibility, we begin with a question on validity: is there a systematic way to construct tools for valid (potentially inadmissible) inference in composite settings? ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process if and only if 𝔭 t ≥ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -a.s., for all t t and 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , where ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is some p-process for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} . ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process if and only if 𝔢 t ≤ 𝔢 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -a.s., for all t t and 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , where ( 𝔢 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is some e-process for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} . ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST if and only if ψ t ≤ ψ t 𝚀 \psi_{t}\leq\psi_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -a.s., for all t t and 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , where ( ψ t 𝚀 ) (\psi_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is some ( 𝚀 , α ) (\mathtt{Q},\alpha) -ST. If 𝔭 t = ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 1 ∧ inf s ≤ t 1 / N s 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}1\wedge\inf_{s\leq t}1/{N^{\mathtt{Q}}_{s}} , where ( N t 𝚀 ) (N^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) is upper bounded by a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM, then ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. If 𝔢 t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 N t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\mathrm{ess}\inf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}N_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , where ( N t 𝚀 ) (N_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is upper bounded by a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM, then ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -e-process. If ψ t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 sup s ≤ t 𝟏 N s 𝚀 ≥ 1 / α \smash{\psi_{t}=\essinf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\sup_{s\leq t}\mathbf{1}_{N^{\mathtt{Q}}_{s}\geq 1/\alpha}} , where ( N t 𝚀 ) (N_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is upper bounded by a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NSM, then ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is a ( 𝒬 , α ) (\mathcal{Q},\alpha) -ST. If ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then 𝔭 t = ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible. If ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then 𝔢 t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔢 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{e}_{t}=\essinf_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( 𝔢 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible. If ( ψ t ) (\psi_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then ψ t = ess ​ inf 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ψ t 𝚀 \smash{\psi_{t}={\essinf}_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}~\psi^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}} for all t t , where ( ψ t 𝚀 ) (\psi_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible. If ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, then ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is nonincreasing and 𝔭 t = ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} for all t t , where ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is a closed 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -MM.
