<sub>§7 Reducing admissible composite inference to the pointwise case</sub>

### 11. Necessary and sufficient conditions for valid (composite) inference

The following observations are straightforward and arguably well-known in some form or another, but are nevertheless useful to spell out formally in order to lay the path for the admissibility results. We only prove (1) , the other two assertions are argued analogously. Suppose we are given that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process. Then, by definition, 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t} is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -p-process for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} ; so choosing ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) := ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}):=(\mathfrak{p}_{t}) itself, we have proved the ‘only if’ direction. For the other direction, suppose for every 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} we are given a p-process ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} , and that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) satisfies 𝔭 t ≥ 𝔭 t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}} , 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -almost surely, for all t t . Let 𝚀 ∗ ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q} be some true (arbitrary) data-generating distribution. We must argue that ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is a 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} -p-process. Indeed, for any α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \alpha\in[0,1] , we have where the first inequality follows because 𝔭 t ≥ 𝔭 t 𝚀 ∗ \mathfrak{p}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}^{*}} , and the second inequality follows because ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ∗ ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}^{*}}) is a 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} -p-process by assumption. This concludes the proof. ∎ The proposition provides a generic reduction from the composite setting to the pointwise setting for performing valid inference, but we can deduce a similar result for admissible inference, presented later. While Proposition 23 forms a useful building block, it makes no mention of martingales. The notions of essential supremum and essential infimum used below are reviewed in Appendix A.2 . Note that we will need the additional restriction that 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is locally dominated in order for these essential extrema to be well defined. This corollary follows from Proposition 23 and so its proof is omitted; see also Remark 16 .

<sub>§7.1 Necessary and sufficient conditions for valid (composite) inference</sub>

### 12. Necessary conditions for admissible composite inference

Not all constructions using martingales are admissible: we provide an analog to Proposition 23 for admissibility. Let ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) denote a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible p-process. For each 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , let ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) be a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -admissible p-process that dominates ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) . Such ( 𝔭 t 𝚀 ) (\mathfrak{p}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t}) exists thanks to Proposition 9 . Let us now define 𝔭 t ′ := ess ​ sup 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ 𝔭 ~ t 𝚀 \mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\prime}:=\esssup_{\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathtt{Q}}_{t} , which is a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -p-process thanks to Proposition 23 (1) . Clearly, we have 𝔭 t ′ ≥ 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\prime}\geq\mathfrak{p}_{t} for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} . Since ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible, we indeed have 𝔭 t ′ = 𝔭 t \mathfrak{p}_{t}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}_{t} for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , yielding the assertion for p-processes. The assertions for e-processes and sequential tests are shown in the same manner. ∎ The following corollary describes the restrictions that every admissible construction necessarily satisfies. The above statements are a direct consequence of Proposition 25 and Theorem 18 .

<sub>§7.2 Necessary conditions for admissible composite inference</sub>

### 13. Sufficient conditions for admissible (composite) inference

The next proposition argues that it suffices to consider only a subset of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} when constructing 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -admissible p-processes, e-processes, or sequential tests. Note that we do not require 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to be locally dominated below. Let us only argue here the case of e-processes. The other cases follow in exactly the same manner. Assume that there exists an e-process ( 𝔢 t ′ ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}^{\prime}) for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( 𝔢 t ′ ≥ 𝔢 t ) = 1 \mathtt{Q}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}\geq\mathfrak{e}_{t})=1 for all t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} and all 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , and that 𝚀 ∗ ​ ( 𝔢 t ′ > 𝔢 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}>\mathfrak{e}_{t})>0 for some t ∈ ℕ 0 t\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , and some 𝚀 ∗ ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}^{*}\in\mathcal{Q} . By assumption, there exists some i ∈ I i\in I such that 𝚀 i ​ ( 𝔢 t ′ > 𝔢 t ) > 0 \mathtt{Q}_{i}(\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{t}>\mathfrak{e}_{t})>0 . Since ( 𝔢 t ) (\mathfrak{e}_{t}) is assumed to be 𝚀 i \mathtt{Q}_{i} -admissible, we get a contradiction. ∎ Of course, two special cases are found at the extremes: when the reference family is a singleton, it means there is a common reference measure 𝚁 \mathtt{R} , and when the reference family is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} itself, the proposition is vacuous. The proposition is particularly useful in the first case; then to get an admissible e-process, for example, it suffices to construct a 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -NSM ( M t ) (M_{t}) that is also a 𝚀 ∗ \mathtt{Q}^{*} -NM, thanks to Proposition 27 (2) and Theorem 18 (2) . The following example demonstrates one such setting. This corollary is again a direct consequence of Proposition 27 and Theorem 18 . Recall that as in Corollary 21 , a sufficient condition for ( 𝔭 t ) (\mathfrak{p}_{t}) to be a closed 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -MM and inf t ∈ ℕ 0 𝔭 t \inf_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mathfrak{p}_{t} be 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -uniformly distributed, for some fixed 𝚀 ∈ 𝒬 \mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{Q} , is that the p-process has the representation 𝔭 t = F 𝚀 ​ ( inf s ≤ t 1 / M s 𝚀 ) \mathfrak{p}_{t}=F^{\mathtt{Q}}(\inf_{s\leq t}1/M_{s}^{\mathtt{Q}}) , where ( M t 𝚀 ) (M_{t}^{\mathtt{Q}}) is a 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} -NM and inf s ∈ ℕ 0 1 / M s \inf_{s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}1/M_{s} has an atomless distribution function F 𝚀 F^{\mathtt{Q}} under 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} . As an immediate consequence of Corollary 29 (2) , recall the Gaussian example in Section 5.1 , and consider testing if the underlying mean is zero. Since ( G t 0 ) (G_{t}^{0}) is a 𝒢 0 \mathcal{G}^{0} -NM, it is also a 𝒢 0 \mathcal{G}^{0} -e-process, and hence a 𝒢 0 \mathcal{G}^{0} -admissible e-process when testing against, for example, 𝒫 = ⋃ m ∈ ℝ 𝒢 m \mathcal{P}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}^{m} .

<sub>§7.3 Sufficient conditions for admissible (composite) inference</sub>

### 14. Necessary and sufficient conditions for confidence sequences

Proposition 13 shows that we can construct a confidence sequence by inverting a family of sequential tests. We now show that their admissibility is also tightly linked to those of the underlying tests. For the first statement of the theorem, note that ℭ t = { γ ∈ 𝒵 : ψ t γ = 0 } \mathfrak{C}_{t}=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}:\psi_{t}^{\gamma}=0\} yields a ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequence by Proposition 13 . Suppose for contradiction that ( ℭ t ′ ) (\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{t}) is another ( ϕ , 𝒫 , α ) (\phi,\mathcal{P},\alpha) -confidence sequence that witnesses the inadmissibility of ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) . Proposition 11 (3) then yields a corresponding family { ( η t γ ) } γ ∈ 𝒵 \{(\eta_{t}^{\gamma})\}_{\gamma\in\mathcal{Z}} of sequential tests. The inadmissibility of ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) then yields some γ ∈ 𝒵 \gamma\in\mathcal{Z} such that ( η t γ ) (\eta_{t}^{\gamma}) strictly dominates ( ψ t γ ) (\psi_{t}^{\gamma}) , a contradiction to the assumption that ( ψ t γ ) (\psi^{\gamma}_{t}) is admissible. The second statement follows in exactly the same way, again by an application of Propositions 13 and 11 (3) . Statements (1) and (2) are direct corollaries of combining the first part of the theorem with Corollary 24 (3) and Corollary 29 (3) . Assume now that 𝙿 γ \mathtt{P}^{\gamma} is locally dominated, for each γ \gamma , and that ( ℭ t ) (\mathfrak{C}_{t}) is 𝒫 \mathcal{P} -admissible. The statement then follows from Proposition 25 (3) and Corollary 26 (3) . ∎ This and the previous section argued in detail that restricting our attention to constructions based on NMs (not NSMs!) does not hurt us: these are universal constructions. Indeed, if one is presented with a p-process, e-process, sequential test, or confidence sequence constructed in some arbitrary fashion, we show that one can always uncover a ‘hidden’ underlying NM, such that applying Ville’s inequality or the optional stopping theorem yields an instrument that is at least as good as the original one.

<sub>§7.4 Necessary and sufficient conditions for confidence sequences</sub>

## 总结与展望
这篇证明：满足某种有效性的随时可停推断，其检验量必须来自一个非负鞅。它对我们的意义是限制性的——如果我们的配对序列不满足这个结构，那么算出来的 E 值就没有它声称的保证。

**It connects to**

- `2210.01948` — 框架的数学基石。
- `2410.23614` — 构造方法必须遵守这里的约束。

## 不足之处
**What a reader should still not conclude**

- 论文的结果是必要条件的刻画，不是充分条件；满足结构不等于有功效。
